--- Page 16 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<meta>Institutional stamp from Yale University Library in right margin. The text appears to be an English translation of the "Epigramma Authoris" from Michael Maier's alchemical work, Atalanta Fugiens.</meta>

->The Author's Epigramme.<-

The Hesperian apples of the Cyprian dame
Gaue to the nimble youth a victory fame:
Who close pursu'd by the swift virgins speed
Throws one in view, that shee her might retard:
Shees gone; shee after, fleet or more than wind,
Whilest with another lure hee affects her mind,
Diverting once againe the Damsels chase;
But hold, they neither yet haue lost the race:
On on they runne, the youth a third lets fall,
And then Atalanta yeilds herselfe and all.
Hippomenes the strength of Sulphur is,
Shee wise of Hermes is overcome by his:
Who mixing loves in Cybele sacred place,
Move her chast soule to wrath by that disgrace,
Transmuting in revenge their formall state
Into a Lyon and a Lyons mate.
By princely chase the better to portray,
My Muse describes it in a triple way;
Depicting first the speed retarding fruit,
One runne before, the third in hott pursuit:
Your eyes with ease these Emblems may behold,
But reason must the mysteryes unfold:
These things I've made familiar, that the mind
The treasures here inclos'd may seeke and find:
What wealth ith world, or health in medicine lyes
These two united Lyons doe comprize.

<vocab>Hesperian apples, Cyprian dame, Atalanta, Hippomenes, Sulphur, Hermes, Cybele, Lyon, alchemical emblem, Michael Maier, medicine</vocab>

--- Page 17 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>dedication</page-type>
<meta>English manuscript translation of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens". Library stamp from Yale University (Yale University Library) is visible on the center-left edge.</meta>

## Michael Majerus Doctor of Physicke, Præsident of the Imperiall Consistory, Knight, &c. most humbly offers, presents, and dedicates this testimony (such as it is) of his good will and gratitude to the most magnificent, famous, and most excellent Senatory Order of the Imperiall Republicke of Mülhausen in Thuringia, consisting of men incomparable for virtue, learning, and true nobleness of mind;

->as also<-

->to the most vigilant Syndicke thereof Christopher<-
->Reinart, a most eminent Doctor of both lawes, &c.<-
->being all his honored and much esteemed<-
->freinds and Masters.<-

Most honorable and most prudent Senators! As touching that Tripod given by Vulcan to Pelops the Bridegroome when hee married Hippodamia the daughter of Oenomaus King of Elis, they report, that, being of singular artifice, it was againe presented by Pelops to Apollo Pythius at Delphos, upon which oracles were by the inspiration of that God sometimes forth uttered by a virgin: Soe this Tripod curiously wrought by Vulcan coming to my hand, moved by the example of Pelops, I resolved to present and consecrate it to the most worthy place and order, and indeed before all others, to your Honours and Excellencies; yet not to the end that Oracles should arise from thence (though they cannot be here expected, but <term>Chymicall</term> things) but to testify publickly the promptitude and good will of my mind some way towards you, who in your most high and worthy state were some few years since pleased to declare to mee 
<margin>of blessed memory</margin> 
(then serving as physitian to his Caesarean Majesty Rudolph 2<sup>d</sup>) your opinion hereto relating: after which time I have allwayes, soe farre as my ability could extend, advanced your virtues abroad not onely by words, but have also been carefull and studious how I might actually and more evidently declare and more abundantly discharge my mind to the same, but apprehending it could not be done otherwise than by some present of literature, and having taken some pains in this running Atalanta, I was desirous to dedicate it wholly, as it is, to your Honours and Excellencies, herein imitating the writers both of our and former ages, who would never appear in publicke without the conduct of a Scipio, for if they should have fallen, who could have helped them? I entreat that I

<vocab>Michael Maier, Michael Majerus, Physicke, Imperiall Consistory, Mülhausen, Thuringia, Christopher Reinart, Tripod, Vulcan, Pelops, Hippodamia, Oenomaus, Apollo Pythius, Delphos, Chymicall, Rudolph 2nd, Atalanta, Scipio</vocab>

<page-type>preface</page-type>

<meta>The page contains the conclusion of the dedication to the Senators of Mülhausen and the beginning of the Preface to the Reader from an English manuscript translation of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens". The handwriting is a 17th-century secretary hand with italic elements.</meta>

may serve you the patrons of this little worke, not that you have oppor-
tunities, either theirs or mine, soe farre as I know, in this study (for neither
have you soe much leisure from most weighty affairs) but because you
seem to mee able to defend any branch of learning, and as the case stands
I have not mett with any matter more worthy, or more honest, abuse ex-
cepted and excluded: let it be what it will, esteem of my endeavour, not
according to the meanness of the writing, but the candor of my mind,
which I am assured of, and for the future receive and register mee
amongst the most dutifull of your Honours servants. Farewell.

Frankfurt ad Manum
1617. August.

---

## The preface to the Reader.

Candid Reader!
It is a truth denyed by none that man in his composition
represents a compendium of the universe, and is destinated to live three kinds
of life: namely a vegetable, in his mothers womb, where indeed hee growes
and is encreased like a plant; a sensible, liveth in this world, where for the
most part hee is led by the senses, as all other animals, from which hee
differs, in beginning to exercise the understanding, though imperfectly; and
lastly an intelligible, in the other world, with God, and the Intelligences,
or good Angells assistant to him; in this life, the nearer a man comes to the
divine nature, the more doth hee rejoyce and is delighted in searching out
things subtile, wonderfull, and rare, by the understanding: and on the contrary,
the more a man declines and degenerates to a bestiall kind, the lesse is hee
affected with them, and is bound to a more corporall sense.

<margin>X Wee see examples of either, that some, namely the more learned, are adorned with these arts & sciences, but very many others, to use their owne words, who are slaves to the pleasures of the body, lust, gluttony, sloath, pride, pomp.</margin>

X Now to instruct
and perfect the understanding God hath hidden infinite secrets in nature,
which are found out <insert>by</insert> innumerable arts and sciences, as fire out of a flint,
and transferred to use: amongst these <term>Chymicall</term> secrets are not the mea-
nest, but next to the indagation of divine things, the principall and most
precious of all, not to be investigated by circumforaneous impostors, and
<term>pseudochymicall</term> drones (who in these things are <term>asini ad Lyram</term>, because
altogether strangers to all good learning and intention) but by more lofty
witts, generously educated, and borne to greater things, they being very sub-
tile, concise, <unclear>sacred</unclear>, rare, and abstruse, and therefore to be comprehended
by the understanding sooner than by sense, rather by profound contem=

<header>The preface to the Reader.</header>
<meta>This is the fifth page of a 17th-century English manuscript translation of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens" (1617). The text continues the "Preface to the Reader," discussing the philosophical value of music and optics as intellectual sciences. It cites classical authorities such as Socrates, Plato, Pythagoras, and Cicero, and explains how the work combines visual emblems, music, and alchemical theory (Chymy) to engage the "spiritual senses."</meta>

plation upon the reading of authors, and comparing them together, and
with the works of nature, than sensitive operation, or manuall experi-
mentation, which is blind without previous theory; next after such intel-
lectuall sciences as these are welcome those which treat of a visible and au-
dible object, as are Opticks, or perspectives, and picture, which is by some
poets called mute, as they on the contrary a speaking picture; as also Mu-
sick, whether vocale, or instrumentale: herein the antient philosopho-
dis soe exercise themselves, that hee was reputed unlearned, and forth
to sing to the myrtle, who refuses the Harp in festivale, as is declared of
Themistocles: in Musick was Socrates instructed, and Plato himselfe, who
concludes him not harmoniously compounded, that delightes not in musicall
harmony: Pythagoras was very famous in the same; who is sayd to have used
the symphony of musick morning, and evening to compose the minds of
his disciples: for there is a peculiar virtue of musick, to quicken or refresh the
affections by the different musicall measures: Soe the Phrygian tune was
by the Greeks termed warlike, because it was sung in warre; and upon en-
gagement, and has a singular virtue in stirring up the spirit of the
soldiers; in stead of which the Cornete is sometimes used for the same pur-
pose, which was formerly esteemed amatory (as the Phrygian is <insert>now</insert> re-
puted) from which wee gather things change one with another: it is sayd that
Timothy Milesio used the Phrygian to make Alexander the great more
spritefull, and couragious in warlike affaires, which Cicero takes notice of 2.
de Legibus, but Terpander resting of the Cornete; who being sent for by
the Lacedemonians, sedition then raging among them, did by the sweet-
nesse of singing soe titillate and affect their minds, that they returned
into friendship, and desisted from sedition: upon which consideration the
Lesbian singers were always preferred in the judgment of the Spartans:

<margin>
X <unclear>are</unclear> <unclear>joyned</unclear> with <unclear>not</unclear> <unclear>only</unclear> <unclear>maney</unclear> <unclear>sences</unclear> but very many <unclear>others</unclear> to this
</margin>
Fabius sayth why of Musick: Musick is an honest and pleasant recrea-
tion most proper for generous wits: that therefore wee might have those
three objects of the more spirituale senses, namely seeing and hearing, as
also the understanding itselfe, as it were in one view and embrace; and
insinuate all at once into many minds for the better understanding thereof,
behold wee have joyned the Opticks together with musick, and sense
with the understanding, that is, things rare to be seen and heard of with
Chymicalle emblems, which are peculiar to this science: for all other arts,
if they represent emblems of manners, or other things, than of the secrets
of nature, they seeme different from their scope and end, they being
willing and requisite to be understood by all men; Chymy not soe,
which as a chast virgin ought to be seen through a veile; and as Diana,
not without a garment of various colours, for reasons elsewhere expres-
sed: I say, these foure emblematicale, poëticale, and allegoricale, pu-
re notions, graved in Venus or copper, not without Venus or elegancy, most
abstruse Chymicale things, and lastly most rare Musicale things to be enu-
cleated by the understanding, you have altogether in one booke, and may
as designes apply them to your owne use, which if it be more intel-

--- Page 20 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>

<header>The preface to the Reader.</header>

lectual, than sensuale, the more profitable and delightfull will it be in time: but if it be first in bondage to the sense, there is noe doubt but there will be a passage from the sense to the understanding as by a doore: for nothing is sayd to be in the understanding, which had not admission by some sense; the understanding of a man new borne being supposd as a smooth table; wherein nothing is as yet written, but anything may be written by the helpe of sense, as a style: and it is comonly sayd, there is noe desire of a thing unknowne; because the senses being inquisitors and messengers must first convey, and as warders watching at the gates <gloss>the instruments of a citty</gloss> <gloss>(the sense organs)</gloss> informe and impart everything that is to be knowne, to the understanding as the dictator and arbiter. As to the reason of engraving these Emblems, lest even they may seeme monstrous and incongruous to you, I will add a few things: Atalanta is renowned by the poets for running, in which exercise shee surpasses all her suitors; and therefore, the reward of victory being propounded, death ensues to those <gloss>that were</gloss> being overrunne by the virgin, till shee herselfe was overcome by Hippomenes, a youth <gloss>very</gloss> hardy and circumspect, casting three golden apples <gloss>at three</gloss> severale times in her way, which taking up, shee was in the meane time outgone by him, being now near and touching the goale: as this Atalanta runns, soe one musicall note always runns before another, as Hippomenes, and the other followes: yet they are established and confirmed in a third, which is single and of one validity, as a golden apple: This same virgin is merely <term>Chymicall</term>, namely <term>philosophicall mercury</term>, fuges in flight and restrained by the <term>golden Sulphur</term>, which if a man know how to make stans, hee will have that spouse which hee desires, if not, destruction and ruines of his affaires: from Hippomenes and Atalanta lying together in the temple of the mother of the Gods, that is, a vesselle, are Lyons produced, or they acquire a red colour: This virgin also obtaines a victory before men, killing a certaine boar of notable bignesse, for which shee was rewarded by Meleager: Striking a rocke at Rhetany the temple of Aesculapius shee brought forth water, <gloss>whereof</gloss> being thirsty, shee dranke: which things being truely allegoricall and emblematicall, and noe way historicalle, I was willing that this intellectuall emblematicall treatise should indigitate and take notice of them in commemoration of this noble processe, especially the apples cast before her being taken out of the <term>Hesperian garden</term>, and administred to Hippomenes by Venus, the Goddesse of favour and sweetness: in those musicall measures or flights you see this observed, that every distich being conveniently adapted to these three voices may be of profit in singing, in which soe many variety of flights being accomodated to one single voice, every wise and intelligent man will also thinke well of this kind of emblematicall figuration in them, as also the adaptation in the distich to every voice: for if some merchants doe esteeme and buy an artifice of picture with a great summe of money where the eyes alone are deceivers, they judging it very near to nature; why should not the learned have these figures in good estimation

<lang>English</lang>

<header>Discourse i.</header>
<meta>Later signature at top right: A Howell 1821</meta>

Hermes the most industrious inquisitor into every naturall secret doth in his Smaragdine table elegantly, though concisely, describe the naturale worke; where amongst other things hee sayth: the wind carryes him in his belly: as if hee should say, hee, whose father is Sol, and mother Luna, must, before hee can be brought to light, be carryed by windy fumes, even as a bird is carryed by the aire, when it flyes: now from fumes or winds, which are nothing els but aire moved, coagulate is produced water, from which mixed with earth doe proceed all mineralls and metalls: yea these very things are determined to consist and be imediately coagulated of fumes: whether therefore it be ascribed to water or fumes, the thing is the same, because they are both the matter of wind: the same may be sayd of mineralls and metalls, though more remotely: now the question is, who hee is, that ought to be carryed by wind; I answer, Chymically, it is Sulphur, which is <margin>Lully here.</margin> carryed in Argent vive, as Lully in his booke, Chapt 32 attest, and all the rest, physically, it is an infant, which ought in a little time to be borne into the world: I say also Arithmetically, that it is the root of a Cube; Musically, <margin>the wind carryes him in his belly, what Sulphur is carryed in 1. and Chap. 47.</margin> that it is the Diapason; Geometrically, that it is a punctum the beginning of a running line: Astronomically, the center of the planetts, Saturne, <margin>the stone is fire carryed in the belly of aire.</margin> Jupiter, and Mars: though these be different subjects, yet if they be well compared together, they will easily demonstrate the offspring of wind, which must be left to every mans industry more or lesse: But I indigitate the matter more plainly thus: every Mercury is compounded of fumes, that is, of water elevating earth with it into an airy parity, and of earth forcing aire to returne into a watry earth or earthy water: for the Elements being altogether in it, and mixed, and subdued and produced together into a viscous nature, they doe not easily proceed one from the other, but either the volatile doe follow above, or remaine below with the fixed, the first whereof is evident in common Mercury, the other in the philosophicall and fixed metalls: in these the fixed Elements doe predominate the volatile, in that the volatile oversway the fixed: nor indeed is it without reason, that Mercury is called and reputed the messenger, interpreter, and, as it were, the running intermediate minister of the other Gods, with wings fitted to his head and feet: for hee is windy, and flyes through the aire as wind itselfe, as is commonly and really confirmed to the damage of many men; but carrying a white wand girt with two serpents crosswise, which can draw soules out of bodys, and bring them backe againe; and doe many such contrary things, hee exquisitely represents the philosophicall Mercury. Mercury therefore is wind, which takes Sulphur or <margin>because Volatile</margin> Dionysus, or, if you had rather, Aesculapius, being yet an imperfect Embryo, out of the mothers body, or even out of the ashes of the mother body burned, and carryes it thither, where it <margin>digested & fixed</margin> may be matured; and the Embryo is Sulphur, which is infused into the body of Boreas by the celestiall sunne, that hee may bring it <margin>the white & red light</margin> forth being matured, who in the compleat time of his carriage produceth twins, one with white haire, called Calais, the other with red, named Zothes: these sonns of Boreas were (as Orpheus the Chymicale poet writes)

## Embleme 2. Of the secrets of Nature.
### The earth is his Nurce.

## Epigramme 2.

> A courteous Wolfe to Romulus displayd
> Her milky duggs, to Jove a Goat, tis sayd:
> Nor is it strange to assert our mother Earth
> Gave sucke to th' tender philosophick birth;
> If beasts for small bodies soo great souls breed,
> Know great will hee be, whome the <del>whole</del> earth doth feed!

<vocab>Embleme, Epigramme, Romulus, Jove, mother Earth, philosophick birth</vocab>

--- Page 26 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Discourse 2.</header>

It is concluded by the peripateticks, and Orthodoxe philosophers, that the thing nourishing is converted into the substance of the thing nourishes, and assimilates to it, not indeed before; but after the alteration of it, and that is accounted an infallible axiome: for how would the thing nourishing (which is already before the like and the same with the thing nourished) have need of the mutation of its forme; which if it should happen, it would not remaine the same nor the like; or how should those things, which cannot be assimilated to the thing nourisher, be taken for nutriment, such as are wood, stones, and the like? as therefore the first is vayne, soe the other is contrary to nature; but for an Infant new borne to be nourished by the milke of animals, is a thing not repugnant to nature: because assimilation of it may be made, but far more easily of the mothers, than of another: wherefore physitians doe conclude, that it conduceth to the health, similitude of substance and manner, as also strength of an Infant, if it be alwayes nourished by the milke of its owne mother, on the contrary if by another. This is the harmony of every nature, that like delight in its like, and followes its foot steps, soe farre as it is able, in all things, with a certaine tacit consent and conspiration: the same thing usually happens in the naturall worke of the philosophers, which is equally governed by nature in the conformation of it, as an infant in the mothers wombe; and though a father and mother, and more ~~these~~ be by way of similitude ascribed to it, yet it is not more artificiall, than the generation of every animals: two seeds are by a certaine pleasant artifice joyned together by animals, and both humans ~~seeds~~, which being united doe by successive alteration produce an Embryo, which growes and is increased, acquiring life and motion, and then is nourished by milke: but necessary it is for the woman in time of conception and impregnation to carry herselfe temperately in heat, meat and drinke, motion and rest, and other circumstances, otherwise an abortion will follow, and destruction of the conceived Embryo, which observation in <term>six things not naturall</term>, because it is prescribed by physitians according to their art, is also artificiall. After the same manner if the seeds be not joyned together in the philosophicall worke, they ought to be joyned: but if they should be found somewhere joyned together, as the food of the Cocke and Henne are had in an egge subsisting together in one continent, then would the philosophicall worke be yet more naturall, than the generation of animals. But let us grant, as the philosophers doe assert, that one comes from the east and another from the west, and are made one; what more is administred to them, than <insert>union</insert> ~~mixtion~~ in their owne vessell, temperate heat and nutriment: the vessell is indeed artificiall, but herein is noe difference, whether the nest be made by the <insert>Henne</insert> ~~country dame~~ herselfe, or disposed by <insert>some</insert> ~~a rustick~~ in <del>some</del> ~~certaine~~ place; as common it is, the generation of the eggs, and the hatching of chickens from them will be the same. Next is a naturall thing, whether it proceed from the temperate fire of furnace, and putrefaction of dung, or from the Sunne and aire, or bowels of the mother, or elsewhere: thus the Egyptian doth by art administer

natural heat by his furnaces for the hatching of eggs: the seeds of silke-

by earth alone to the <term>philosophicall babe</term>, the name of Nurse is not
without reason to be ascribed to earth: but a thing contrary to the other
kinds of milke, which are converted and doe not convert, happens in this
admirable terrestriall juice; it by reason of its most efficacious virtue
much altering the nature of the thing nourished, even as the milke of
the wolfe is believed to have disposed the body of Romulus into a mag-
nanimous nature, and propense to warre.

### Embleme 3. Of the secrets of nature.

->Goe to the woman washing cloaths, and doe you after the same manner.<-

### Epigramme 3.

> If you delight to dive in things abstruse;
> Be vigilant, observe what may conduce:
> Doe you not see; how women wash and boyle
> Their cloaths in water hott, if they gett soyle!
> pursue their steps, you cannot goe astray,
> For water washeth any filth away.

If ~~some~~ <insert>any</insert> ~~it detains~~ impuritys happen to linnen cloaths, whereby they are spot-
ted and blacked, as with earthy excrements, they are washed away by the moist -
Element, namely water, and the cloaths exposed to the aire, that the moisture -
together with the faces may be drawn out by the heat of the sunne; as by -
fire, the fourth Element, if this be often done, the cloaths, which before were -
foule and unclean, doe become pure, and <insert>free</insert> ~~purged~~ from spots. This is the art of -
women, which they learned from Nature herselfe: for wee see, that if the bones of -
animals, which at first are blacke and filthy, be exposed to the aire, and often -
washed with raine, and dryed agayne by the heat of the sunne often coming upon -
them, they will, as Isaac notes, be reduced to perfect whiteness. The same -
alsoe we observe in the philosophicall subject, whatsoever crudityes and fa-
ces appear, they are purged and taken away by ~~this~~ water powred on, and the -
body ~~is~~ brought to great clarity and perfection. For all Chymicall operations, -
as Calcination, Sublimation, Solution, Distillation, Descension, Coagulation, Sepa-
tion, and the rest, are perfected by ablution alone: For hee that washeth an -
impure thing with water, performs the same thing, as is accomplished by soo -
many forms of operating: For, as the philosophers Rosary sayth, the linnen of -
prime descent being defiled by sweat, must be washed by fire; and burned -
by water: by which fire and water seem to have given one another mutual qua-
lityes, or that the philosophicall fire is not of the same kind with common fire, and -
the same must be supposed of the water. As for Calx vive, and ignis Graecus wee -
know that they are kindled by water, and noe wise extinguished, contrary to -
the nature of other things that are subject to take fire: For they affirme, that -
Camphire being kindled before burns in water: and the stone called Gagates (as -
Anselm de Bood attest) being kindled is more easily extinguished by oyle -
than ~~by~~ water: for Oyle is ~~not~~ mixed with it, and suffocates the fires body, -
water not being able to mix itselfe with fattnesse, gives place to the body -
being fired, unless it altogether cover and overwhelme it: but that can-
not easily be done, because it is a stone, and swims upon the top of the -
water, as all oyle. For Naphta, petroleum, and the like being kindled doe -
not much fear water. Some write of the subterranean coals in the dominion -
of Liege, that being kindled in the earth, they cannot be extinguished by -
water, but onely by throwing and heaping the dust of the earth upon them: -
as for Tacitus declares of the like kind of fire, which cannot be quenched -
by water, but onely by clubs, and garments taken off from the body: there -
is therefore a great diversity of fires, and <gloss>1</gloss> no <gloss>2 both 4</gloss> <gloss>3</gloss> <gloss>4</gloss> lesse of liquours, in exciting -

parison with fire: nor this onely, but also the matter itselfe doth some-
times sustaine fire; as those fine Byssinian cloaths, formerly used and -
esteemed by rich men, which were washed, or reduced to former cleanness, -

vainly talke of the haires of the reptile Salamander, as if incombustible -

Elychnia <insert>or lamp-wickes</insert> made thereof, is not to be regarded: Some there be who of a
truth affirme that in times past a vesture was prepared of <term>Talke</term>, <term>plumous Alum</term>,

combustible matters: The <term>philosophicall Subject</term> must be considered according
to all these differences, if at any time it be prepared: for fire, water, and matter
itselfe is here not common: for the philosophers fire is water, and water fire
their water doth at once both wash and calcine; soe alsoe their fire: and the
cloaths, which are to be washed, have the nature of the <term>byssinean vesture</term>, or
prepared Talke, the temperament of which, and art of preparation is alsoe
not obvious to all men: To wash them, they make a <term>Lixivium</term>, or Lye, not
with the ashes, or salt of Oake, but metalline, which is more durable than
the other, nor with common water, but that, which hath been congealed into
Ice and snow under the signe <term>Aquary</term>, as that which is of thinner parts, than
standing waters of ditches or marshes, and therefore can better penetrate into
the more internall parts of the blacke and unclean philosophicall body, in
order to the washing and purging thereof.

### Embleme 4. Of the secrets of Nature.

->Joyne the Brother with the Sister and giue them a cup of loue.<-

### Epigramme 4.

To multiply the world with humane race;
The brother <gloss>did</gloss> must<note>The word 'must' is struck through in the manuscript with a horizontal line.</note> his sister close embrace:
Let therefore one the other friendly wed,
That they may act as man and wife in bed:
First to invite, prepare a cup o’th best,
And then they both will freely doe the rest.

<vocab>Embleme, Epigramme, secrets of Nature, humane race, cup of loue, alchemical marriage</vocab>

--- Page 32 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>Discourse. 4.</header>
<meta>This page contains the "Discourse" for Emblem 4 of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens," explaining the alchemical concept of the marriage between brother and sister through a natural philosophy lens, referencing the lineage of Adam and the medical theories of Levinus Lemnius.</meta>

The divine and politicall lawes doe both forbid those people to marry, who are by nature ioyned in too near a degree <gloss>directly</gloss> of consanguinity, such namely as ascends and descends in a genealogicall line, and that for very good considerations; but when the philosophers speake of the mother to be ioyned with the sonne, or father with the daughter, or of the brother with the sister, they neither say or doe anything contrary to the sayd lawes: because subjects distinguish attributes and causes effects; for the persons of philosophers are <gloss>say</gloss> free from those differences of <gloss>among</gloss><note>The word 'among' is struck through with a horizontal line.</note> the sonnes and daughters of Adam, who marryed one with another without the imputation of any crime: but the principall reason seems to be, that mankind might be more firmly united and linked in affinity and friendship, and not <gloss>be</gloss> divided into inimicall and hereditary factions of familyes: the same reason remaining firme and entire in the first sonnes and daughters of Adam, did not at all debarre them from the <gloss>right</gloss><note>The word 'right' is struck through with a horizontal line.</note> <gloss>rites</gloss> of matrimony: for mankind did then exist in them alone, and no other were living besides them, and their parents, and therefore as allyes in blood, soe necessarily were they ioyned in affinity: but a multitude of men increasing, and being distributed into innumerable families, this was found a true and iust cause why brothers should not marry sisters: The philosophers have another reason, why sisters may marry brothers, namely the similitude of substance, that it may be ioyned to the like: but in this kind there are only two like one another as to species, different as to sex, whereof one is saluted by the name of brother, the other of sister: they therefore may, by the same both liberty and condition, yea and inevitable necessity, as the first consanguine men, be lawfully committed to one matrimony: the brother is hott and dry, and therefore very cholerick; the sister is cold and moist, having much phlegmatick matter in her: which natures soe differing in temper doe conioinly best agree in love and fruitfulness, and the propagation of children: for as fire, which may be propagated, is not easily struck out of the most hard body steel and steel, nor out of the brittle flint and flint, but out of hard and brittle, that is, steel and flint, soe a lively offspring is not produced from a hott man and fiery woman, as neither from both cold (for the coldness of the man is barrenness) but from him hott, her more cold, for in the latitude of humane temperament, the hottest woman is concluded colder than the coldest man of his sex, if sound, as Lev. Lemnius in lib. de occ. nat. mirat. proves: the sister therefore and brother are rightly ioyned together by the philosophers: If a man desires offspring from a henne, bitch, shee goat, ewe, or other brutes, hee ioynes a <gloss>dunghill</gloss><note>The word 'dunghill' is struck through with a horizontal line.</note> cocke, dogge, hee goat, ramme, every animale, to its species, to which it is most like, and soe is not frustrated of his desire; nor indeed doth hee respect the consanguinity of brutes, but onely the generosity of every one, and the congruity of nature: soe may it be sayd of the body of a tree, and the slippe to be therein grafted, yea of the metalls to-

<vocab>consanguinity, philosophers, Adam, affinity, matrimony, species, cholerick, phlegmatick, Lev. Lemnius, alchemy</vocab>

<header>Discourse 4.</header>
<meta>This page continues the discourse for Emblem 4 of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens." It elaborates on the necessity of a "philothesium" (cup of love) to facilitate the union between the alchemical brother and sister, using medical and biblical analogies (specifically Lot) to explain how natural barriers to such a union are overcome to ensure fruitfulness.</meta>

nature, which notwithstanding as it hath of all things the greatest
similitude and homogeneity of substance, doth soe require, where it
desires a thing to be joyned with it. But brother and sister being joyned
together, doe not become fruitfull, nor persist long in love, except a philo-
thesium, or cup of love be administred to them like an amorous potion:
for their minds are hereby soe quieted and united, that (laying aside mo-
desty) they doe as it were drunke (like Lot) goe together, and procreate
an offspring (not spurious but) legitimate: For who knowes not that man-
kind is very much obliged to medicine, because many thousands of men
are now in the world, which would not have been, if their fathers or fore-
fathers had not by the means and benefit thereof been freed and delivered
from the defect of sterility, or their mothers <gloss>preserved</gloss> from abortion, by removing
and taking away the cause, or the nearest and remote impediment? This
cup of love is therefore given to this new marryed couple for the same reasons,
which any man may hereby plainly understand are three, that is to say, -
constancy of love, remotion of sterility, and prevention of abortion.

<vocab>philothesium, cup of love, amorous potion, Lot, homogeneity of substance, sterility, abortion, remotion</vocab>

### Embleme 5. Of the Secrets of nature.

->Put a Toad <gloss>to</gloss> ~~in~~ the womans breasts, that shee may giue it sucke,<-
->and the ~~woman~~ <gloss>herselfe</gloss> dye, and the Toad <gloss>grow fatt</gloss> ~~be fed~~ with milke.<-

### Epigramme 5.

> Lay the vild Toad to th' womans milky breast,
> That as an infant hee himselfe may feast,
> And thereby grow in bignesse and in strength,
> Till hee hath killd his dry-suckt nurse at length:
> An antidote from thence prepare, the heart
> Intoxicated cure, and purge with art.

<header>Discourse 5.</header>
<meta>This page contains "Discourse 5" from a 17th-century manuscript translation of Michael Maier’s "Atalanta Fugiens." The text provides an alchemical commentary on the allegory of a woman nursing a toad. It includes several contemporary interlinear corrections and a marginal reference to "Theophilus in Turba" (referencing the Turba Philosophorum). The handwriting is a clear English secretary hand with italic characteristics.</meta>

The whole troop of ~~the~~ philosophers doe herein consent, that their worke is
nothing else, but man and wife: the man's part is indeed to generate, and governe,
the wife, her to conceive, be impregnated, bring forth, give sucke, and educate
the offspring, and be subject to the mans comands: for as shee sustaines and
nourisheth the conceived Embryo, before it be brought to light, with blood, soo
being brought forth with milke. To this end nature hath prepared, for the
tender infant a digestible and proportioned food in the mothers breast, which
waits his coming, as the first provision and viaticum in ~~this~~ <gloss>the</gloss> race of the world:
by milke therefore hee is nourished, growes, and is increased, till hee be furnished
with instruments to chew bread, that is, both when <gloss>hee</gloss> is deservedly weaned, be-
cause nature hath provided him other nutriment more solid: But here the phi-
losophers say, that a Toad must be put to the womans breasts, that shee may
nourish it, as an infant, with her milke: This is a miserable and horrible spectacle,
and a wicked thing, that milke designed for an infant should be given to a
Toad, being a venomous beast, and contrary to the nature of man: Wee have
heard and read of Serpents and Dragons sucking the dugs of Cowes: Toads might
perhaps desire the same thing, if occasion should be offered in beasts. There is a
noted story of a Toad, who seated himselfe upon the mouth, and inside of the
lipps of a certaine countryman being asleep, soo that hee could not be driven off
by any contrivance, unlesse by violence, which being accompany'd with danger of
death, by the spitting of poyson (which hee is sayd to use for defending or offending
weapon) was therefore not to be attempted; but a remedy being found for the
miserable man by antipathy, namely, of a very great Spider and the Toad,
who hate one another mortally; hee was therefore carryed with the Toad di-
rectly to the place, where an ~~over~~ <gloss>grown</gloss> Spider had made its web, which is
seeing the Toad, presently came downe upon the backe of the Toad, and
pricked him with its sting: but hee having received noe harme thereby, it
descended a second time, and stucke him againe more strongly, where-
upon the Toad began imediately to swell, and fell downe dead from the
mans mouth, without any hurt to him. But here a thing contrary happens, be-
cause the Toad seiseth not the mouth but the breast of the woman, by whose milke hee
increaseth soo as to be of great magnitude and strength, but the woman consumes
and dyes her spirits being taken away: for poyson is easily comunicated by the pec-
<margin>Theophilus in Turba makes mention of a Dragon joynd to a woman.</margin>
torale veins to the heart, and corrupts and destroyes it, as it is evident in the case of
Cleopatra, who applyed vipers to her breasts, being willing to be prevented by vo-
luntary death from being led alive into the hand and triumph of the con-
querour: But lest any man should thinke the philosophers soo cruell, as to en-
joyne a venomous reptile to be put to a woman, hee must know that this Toad
is the offspring or sonne of the same woman, produced by a monstrous birth,
and therefore by natural right ought to be fed and nourished by the mothers
milke, but it is not in the will of the sonne that the mother should dye: for
neither could hee infect his mother, who was coagulated in her bowells, and
increased by blood even to birth. It is indeed a thing ominous for a Toad
to be borne of a woman, which in our knowledge hath happened elsewhere:

<header>Discourse 5.</header>

Guilielmus Neobrigensis, an English writer, in his commentarys sayth (how 
truely let others judge) that in a certaine stone-quarry in the Dioces of the 
Bishop of Winchester, a great stone being divided, there was within found a living 
Toade with a golden chaine, and it was by the Bishops comand, hidden in the 
same place, and bury'd in perpetuale darknesse, lest it might bear an ill omen 
with it: such is also this Toade: for it is apparelled with gold, though not extrin=
secally, with the artificiale worke of a chaine, yet intrinsecally with a natural, 
namely of a stone, which some call <term>Borax</term>, <term>Chelonitis</term>, <term>Batrachites</term>, <term>Crapaudina</term>, 
or <term>Garatronium</term>: for this farre exceeds gold in virtue against all poyson whatsoe=
ver of animals, and is commonly sett in gold, as a case or cover, that it may 
not be hurt or lost. But it ought to be legitimate, <gloss>being</gloss> had out of <strike>the</strike> <insert>an</insert> animal, but if 
it be picked out of subterranean trenches, as commonly it is, the stone taken up 
may be made neat in the forme of it, and used in stead thereof with the best 
mineralls, relieving and comforting the heart: for in this <insert>the philosophicall</insert> 
Toade <strike>is</strike> <gloss>really</gloss> found, not in a stone-quarry (as that fabler affirms) and hath 
gold in him, not outwardly for pompe: for to what end should a Toad adorne 
himselfe, lurking in darknesse and secret places? that hee might perhaps 
be magnificently saluted by a Beetle, if it mett him in the twilight! what 
subterranean Goldsmith should make him a golden chaine? the father 
perhaps of the green boyes, which came out of the land of St martin, yea 
out of the earth itselfe, as also two doggs out of the stone-quarry, asserted 
by the same Author.

<header>Discourse 6.</header>
<meta>catchword: gold</meta>

<margin>x Chymistry is exactly parallel to agriculture, and its deputy, which in all things it professeth its office, but under a more abstruse allegory.</margin>

A City sayth plato subsists not by physitian and physitian, but by physitian and husbandman, that is, men of divers functions, but hee mentions the husbandman and physitian first, because their works are very conspicuous in the imitation, emendation, and perfection of nature: for they both take a naturall subject, in which they according to art add some certaine things necessary being wanting or also remove those things which are superfluous; soe that the art of both may (as medicine by Hippocrates) bee defined by the addition of defect, and subtraction of superfluity: for what dos the husbandman more than add ploughing, ridging furrowing, harrowing, dunging or manuring, sowing, etc to land left by nature, that is, commit the increase and good successe to nature, which administreth the heat of the sunne, and raine, and multiplyes the seeds by them, and promotes them to standing corne soon after to be reaped: in the meane time whilst the herbe is springing the husbandman weeds out thistles and all impediments, gathers the corne being ripe, takes away things superfluous from it being reaped, namely straw, chaffe, and such other things: soe also a physitian (as also a <term>Chymist</term> in a different respect) laboureth how to preserve present health to mans body, being wanting to recover it by severall remedyes, takes away the morbificke cause, cures the disease, asswageth symptoms, abates superfluous blood by opening a veyne, being wanting restores it by appointing good order of dyet, evacuates noxious humors by purgation, and soe imitates, supplyes, and amends nature a thousand ways by the works of the understanding and art, which being notorious, Chymicall things are rather to be considered by us: for Chymistry doth first declare the operations of agriculture by its secret bounds and ways of operating: Husbandmen have land, wherein to sow seed, soe also the Chymists: they have dung wherewith to manure their ground, soe also those, without which nothing could be done, nor any successe expected: They have seed, the multiplication of which they desire: if Chymists had not the like, they would (as Tully sayth) imitate the painter, that endeavours to expresse the face of a certaine man, whom hee has never seen, nor the image of him: Husbandmen expect raine, and heat of the Sunne, soe also doe the Chymists truly administer heat convenient to their works, and raine: I need say noe more: from hence the Antients produced Ceres, Triptolemus, Osiris, Dionysus, the golden Godds, as relating to Chymistry, teaching as it were men how to cast the seeds of fruits into their earth, showing tillage, with the propagation of vines, and use of wine, all which things the ignorant have interpreted to rusticall operation, but erroneously: for those things, which are absconded from the vulgar, and declared to the learned under these veils of agriculture, are most abstruse mysteryes of nature: To this end say the philosophers that their gold must be sowed upon <term>white foliated earth</term>, as if they meant that the sowing of corne should be observed and imitated for example, which the author of the treatise of corne and Jodocus Greverus have most excellently performed in their descriptions, for they have both most elegantly accommodated every operation of agriculture in the producing of corne to the semination of gold, or generation of <unclear>it</unclear>.

<margin>For this reason sayth Ovid. Philyrides made the youth Achilles an excellent Harper . . .</margin>
the tincture: White earth, because sandy, yeilds little fruit to the countrymen, by 
whome that which is blacke and fatt is more desired, but the white principally 
conduceth to the philosophy, if it be foliated, that is well prepared, because 
they know how to enrich it with their dung, the other not at all: Now sowing is 
the propagation of the world, whereby that, which cannot long endure in an 
individuale, is caused to persist in the species: This is in men, animals, and 
plants in their hermaphroditicey, in them under two distinct sexes: but in 
metales farr otherwise: for in them a line is made of the flux of a point, a 
superficies of the flux of a line, a body of the flux of a superficies: But 
the stary produces that point before the line, superficies, and body, because 
it is the principle of them all: Nature adds the flux a long time after, that is, 
the celestiall sunne did in the earth generate a sonne, whome Mercury committes 
to Vulcan and Chiron to be educated, that is, manuale artifice to be instructor, 
even as they write of Achilles, who was hole, and hardened in fire by his mother 
Thetis: hee, amongst other things, learned Musicke, and the art of playing on the 
harp from Chiron: but Achilles being noe other than the philosophicall subject 
whose sonne was Pyrrhus with red haire, without which two Troy could not be 
taken and subdued, as wee have abundantly demonstrated in our Hieroglyphick 
booke 6.) therefore doe wee not without reason touch (though cursorily) upon musicke 
in this our worke, where wee describe Achilles, and his heroicke virtues and en- 
terprises. For if musicke adornes soe great a heroe, why may it not also 
make this our worke more compleat and acceptable: For the Angells sing (as the 
sacred scriptures attest, the heavens sing, as Pythagoras affirmed, and, as the 
psalmist sayth, declare the glory of God, the Muses and Apollo sing, as 
the poetts, men even infants sing, birds sing, sheep and geese sing in 
musicall instruments, if therefore wee also sing, there is reason for it . . .

<header>Discourse 7.</header>

What Hippocrates, the defender and propugnator of physitians, affirms of hu-
mors, that they are different and many in man, and not one onely, otherwise
various diseases could not arise; that was perceive to be true of the elements
of the world: for if there was but one onely element, there could be noo-
mutation of it into another, noo generation nor corruption, yea all things
would be one immutable thing, and noo mettals, minerals, plants, and
animals could be naturally from thence produced. For this reason the su-
preme Creator composed the whole systeme of the world of divers and
contrary natures, namely of light and heavy, hott and cold, moist and dry,
that one might by affinity passe into the other, and soo a composition be
made of bodyes, which might be very different one from another in essence,
qualityes, virtues, and effects: for in things perfectly mixed are the light ele-
ments, as fire and aire, and also the heavy, as earth and water, which are soo
equally tempered together, that one recedes not from the other, but suffers itselfe
to be easily taken and held by the other, as neighbour by neighbour: Earth and aire
are contrary one to the other, soo fire and water, yet notwithstanding fire main-
taines friendship with aire by heat common to both, with earth by siccity: soo
aire with water, and water with earth: by which means they are all joyned
by bonds of affinity, or rather consanguinity, and doe remayne together in
one composition, which, if it abounds with the light element, elevates the
heavy with it, if with the heavy, weighs downe the light with it: This is decla-
red by the two Eagles, one with wings, the other without, of which that
which endeavours to fly is restrained by the <gloss>other</gloss> an example hereof is evident
in the fight of the Falcon and Heron, for the Falcon soaring higher in
the aire by his speedy flying, takes and bears the Heron with his talons, by
whose weight both fall to the ground. The contrary appears in the
artificiall doue or Automa of Archytas, where heavy things were lifted up
by light, that is, the wooden body of it caryed into the aire by the
spirit included within: In the philosophicall subject the light things doe
first predominate the heavy, as to plenty, but they are overcome by vir-
tue of the heavy things, and in processe of time the Eagles wings are cut
off, and one very great bird (namely an Ostrich) is made of two, which
can consume iron, and which being impeded by weight runns more upon
the earth, than flyes in the aire, though it hath goodly wings: Concerning
this or the like Hermes (as the Author of Aurora chapt. 5. affirms) writes thus:

> I have considered a bird worthy to be had in estimation by the wise, which
> flyes when it is in Aries, Cancer, Libra, or Capricorne, and you will never
> fayle of it in pure Mynes, and rocky mountains: Concerning the same Se-

nior in Tabula, where two are seen, one volatile the other without wings,
whereof one holds the other fast by it beake, that they cannot easily be
separated: For this is the devise and machination of universall nature,
alwayes to rayse heavy things by light, and likewise to depresse light
things by heavy, as the Author of the perfect Magistery declareth, who
constitutes seven minerals spirit, as it were created to starry, and

<header>Discourse 7.</header>

Soo many metallicke bodyes, as fixed, starry, and enjoying those, to be
wedded to these: from whence also Aristotle the chymist, the spirit, sayth he,
having dissolved the body and soule, soo as to exist in their forms, doth not
remaine a fixed thing, unless you operate upon it: now operation is to joyne
it with the body, from which you prepared it at first: because the spirit is
thereby prevented from flight being mixed with the superficies of the
body; the light element, namely aire and fire, as a learned man declares,
doe in Camphire oversway the heavy, and therefore it is sayd wholly to ex-
hale and evaporate into aire. In Argent vive, the flowers of Sulphur, Anti-
mony, the salt of harts blood, Sal Armoniacke, and such others, the earth
flyes with the aire into the Alembecke, and is not separated from it. In Gold,
glasse, Diamond, the stone Smyris, Granats, and the like, the elements
doe notwithstanding fire, remayne along time joyned without detriment,
and the earth retaines those things which are left in it selfe. In other com-
bustibles division and separation of one thing from another is effected, soo
that the ashes are left in the bottome, the water, aire, and fire fly up-
wards: wee must not therefore have respect to the unequale composition of
these last, being not soo strongly mixed, nor to the commixtion of the first,
though more durable, yet volatile, but to the solidity, constancy, and fixity
of the middle: for soo the bird without wings will detaine that which hath,
and the fixed substance will fix the volatile, which must necessarily be
done.

## Embleme 8. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Take an egge and smite it with a fiery sword.<-

> Rest not till you have found that egge soe pure;

<header>Discourse 8.</header>

Many and severall sorts of birds there are, which as they are indefinite in number, soe are they unknowne to us by names: They tell us of a very great bird appearing at a certaine time of the year in a small Isle of the Sea called Rut, because it can lift up an Elephant with it into the aire. India and America doe yeild parrotts of various colour, crows, and the like: but it is not the philosophicall intention to inquire after the eggs of these birds: The Egyptians doe yearly infest, and as it were with a publicke warre denounce & prosecute the Eggs of Crocodiles with weapons of iron; the philosophers doe burne their eggs with fire, not to mortify and destroy it, but that it may receive life and growth; for when a chicken is from thence produced, animated and living, it cannot be sayd to be the corruption but generation of it: it ceaseth indeed to be an egge by the privation of the <term>ovall</term> forme, and begins to be an animale with two feet and volatile by the introduction of a more noble forme: In an egge are the seeds of both male and female joyned together under one cover or shell: The yolke constitutes the chicken, and the radicale part and bowels thereof, the seed of the male being the former, and internale efficient: The white yeilds matter, or thread and increment to the rudiment as it were to the warp of the chicken: External heat is the first mover, which doth by a certaine circulation of the Elements, together with the instinct and guidance of nature introduce a new forme by changing one thing into another: for water turnes into aire, aire into fire, and fire into earth, upon the conjunction of which, a specificall forme being transmitted by the Starry, an individuall is made of a certaine kind of bird, namely of that, whose egge it was, and seed infused in it. It is indeed sayd to be smitten with a fiery sword, whilst <term>Vulcan</term> officiating the duty of a midwife gives passage or egresse to the chicken by his axe, as to <term>Pallas</term> out of the brains of <term>Jupiter</term>: This is that which <term>Basile Valentine</term> affirms, that <term>Mercury</term> was imprisoned by <term>Vulcan</term> at the command of <term>Mars</term>, and not released, before hee was wholly putrefy'd and dead: but this death is to him the begining of a new life, as the corruption or death of an egge brings new generation and life to the chicken, soe an Embryo being freed from that humane vegetable life (which alone hee enjoyes in his mothers wombe, another more perfect is obtained by coming into the light of this world, as by nativity: You alsoe who being deprived of this present life, which was bad, another most perfect and eternale is at hand: Thus <term>Lully</term> doth in many places call this fiery sword a sharp spear; because fire doth as a sharp spear or sword, perforate bodyes, and makes them porous and pervious, that water can penetrate to dissolve and reduce them from hardnesse to softness and tractability. In the stomachs of a Cormorant, which is of all birds most voracious, are found long and round wormes alive; which are as the instrument of heat, they forthwith invading, and as most sharp needles perforating (as wee have sometimes observed) and soe in a very short time consuming the Eeles and other fish taken by it, with a wonderfull operation of nature: as therefore heat pricketts, soe a thing pricking sometimes supplys the

But what bird was the egge? <term>Muséty</term> in the same place; now I say, sayth hee, that no instruments are made, except of our white, Starry, splendid powder, and of the white Stone, of which powder are made fitt instruments for the egge; yet they have not named the egge, nor what bird the egge was.

### Embleme 9. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Shut up the tree with the old man in the house of dew, and eating<-
->of the fruit thereof hee will become young.<-

---

### Epigramme 9.

The Sophi have a tree with golden fruit,
And an old Senior of noe mean repute;
For which you must provide a house of glasse
Replete with dew, therein some time to passe:
That hee, solacing nature with that fare,
May shake of age, and be as young men are.

<vocab>Sophi, Senior, golden fruit, house of glasse, dew, solacing nature, alchemy, emblem, epigram</vocab>

--- Page 47 ---
<lang>en</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>Discourse 9.</header>

All things whatsoever doe grow in length, breadth, and depth, that is, are
propagated, nourished, and augmented to maturity, the same things doo all-
so decrease, that is, are diminished in facultyes, dye, and are wholly demo-
lished, as is manifestly evident in all vegetables and animals: wherefore man
also having arrived at full growth admits of decrement, that is, old age, whereby
his strength is gradually diminished, even till the approach of death: now the
cause of old age is the same as that of a lampe decaying, and shining obscurely
the oyle being almost consumed: For as there are three things in a lampe, the
match, fattnesse, and flame, soo in man the vitall parts, bowells, and ligaments
are the match, fattnesse the radicale moisture, flame the naturall heat; all
the difference is that the flame of a lampe shines, the naturall heat not at all,
it not being fire, but onely heat, and that fattnesse is oyly, the radicale mois-
ture viscous, as being of a seminale principle: As also a lampe is extinguished
through want of oyle, soo man by old age, without any other disease, falls
into a marasme, and aged consumption, and lastly death. They relate
of the Eagle, that in old age being hindered by a crooked beake it would
be famished to death, if it were not restored by nature as it were to
youth by casting the beake: Soe Staggs seem to grow young againe by
throwing of their hornes, Serpents their skinns, and crabs their shells;
not indeed really, because the consumed radicale moisture is not restored
to them, but as to appearance: That which can make a man grow young
againe is nothing, but death itselfe, and the begining of eternall life
ensuing: as to externall forme, and the restauration of strength in
some measure, together with the taking away of wrinkles and gray-
haires, some there are that say a remedy may be found, which Lully
affirms of the Quintessence, Arnold of the prepared gold: But here
the philosophers say, that an old man, to become young, must be
shutt up with a certaine tree in a house of dew, and then hee will
eat of the fruit of the tree, and soe recover youth: It is scarce be-
lived by the vulgar that such trees are now in the nature of things.
Of Myrobalans, the fruit of a tree, physitians write wonders, as if
they could performe such a thing, take away gray haires, purify the
blood, and prolong life: but these things are doubted by many men,
unlesse they are sayd to effect it by accident, as also other things, which
purge the masse of blood from faeces mixt with it, and dye gray hairs
with blacknesse, with which Myrobalans are reported to colour the
haire and ball of the eyes. Marsilius Ficinus in his booke of preser-
ving the life of students, writes, that in order to attaine to long life
it is comodious for a man dayly to sucke the milke of some certaine
beautifull and young woman, instead of which others doo comend
the eating of vipers flesh: but indeed these means are themselves

<header>Discourse 9.</header>

ldus more rigid than old age, and scarce to be used by one of a thousand,
though they should not be without certaine effect. It is written by para=
celsus in his booke of long life, that a sicke man may attract to him =
selfe the health of another by imagination alone, as also an old man
youth, but this Author seems in this to use his imagination onely, not
experience: It is a thing certaine of the Psylli who have two pupills of the
eye; and of witches, that they bewitch children and cattle by aspect alone;
from whence that of Virgile: Nescio quis teneros oculus mihi fascinat agnos:
but these things are done without contact, whereby the tree restores the
old man to youth: for this tree hath sweet fruit, ripe and red, which doe
easily turne into pure blood, being of easy digestion and excellent nutri=
ment, soe as to leave nothing superfluous or feculent in the body: but
the old man abounds with white phlegme, and is of a white colour and
hairy, which humor, colour, and haire are changed by eating these
fruit, and become red, as these are of young men. For this reason say the
philosophers, the stone is first old, that is white, then young, that is, red, because
this is the colour of youth, as that of old age: It is added, that the old man
ought to be shutt up with the tree; not in the open aire, but in a house; not
dry, but moist with dew. It is accounted a miracle for trees to spring or vegetate
in a close place; but if it be moist, there is noe doubt of their long duration:
for the nutriment of a tree is moisture; and airy earth, that is, fatt, which
can ascend into the trunk and boughes, and there produce leaves, flowers,
and fruit: in which naturall works all the Elements doe concurr, fire
giving the first motion, as the efficient, aire tenuity and penetrability, water
lubricity, earth coagulation: for aire returns into water, and water into
earth, if any of their superfluity ascends: By fire I meane the naturall
heat, which being propagated with the food doth by the power of the Starres
as it were a Smith fabricate and forme fruit like to <gloss>onely</gloss> those things from whence
the food ariseth: But a dewy evaporation is not good and expedient to moisten
the tree, that it may more easily yield fruit, but also the old man, that by
these fruits hee may become young againe; that is to say, the dewy evapo=
ration mollifying, filling up, and restoring his wrinkles, and dry skinne
with temperate heat and moisture: for physitians doe advisedly and with
great utility enjoyne and prescribe warme baths in the marasme and
aged consumption: but if the thing be well considered that tree is the
daughter of the old man, which as Daphne is transmuted into a vegetable
of the like sort, and therefore the old man doth not without reason expect
youth from it, which causes it to be effected.

## Embleme 10. Of the secrets of Nature.

->Giue fire to fire, Mercury to Mercury, and you haue enough.<-

---

## Epigramme 10.

> The worlds whole frame depends upon this chaine,
> Like to its like, as shepheardesse to swaine:
> These are the bounds of art, proceed noe higher,
> Hermes to Hermes ioyne, and fire to fire.
> Vulcan makes Hermes fly, but Cynthia must
> Bring him to stay, then yeilds to her brothers lust.

<vocab>Alchemy, Hermes, Vulcan, Cynthia, Mercury, secrets of Nature</vocab>

--- Page 50 ---
<lang>en</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>Discourse. 10.</header>

If this saying be taken liberally, it onely increaseth the quantity of fire and Mercury, and introduceth noe new quality in the subject: for every like being added to its like makes a similar augmentation: and therefore physitians affirme that contrary diseases are cured and taken away by contrary medicines: For wee see that fire is extinguished by water, but sustained by adding fire: The poet is of the same opinion saying: Venus rageth in wine, fire in fire: but it may be answered, that fire and fire, Mercury and Mercury are very different one from another: for there are many kinds of fires with y^e philosophers, many also of Mercury: Moreover the same heat and cold being distant onely in place or situation, differs from another of its owne kind, soe as to attract to it that which is like to itsselfe, that heat fixed in any part is attracted and drawne forth by the same heat, and that the joynts being stupefied with y^e cold of winter, and almost dead with cold water, are restored if they be put into it, not by externall heat immediately administred: as indeed a greater light obscures the lesse; soe also greater heat the lesse, and greater cold the lesse: nevertheless it is requisite for the externall heat and cold to be lesse, than that which was before fixed or imprinted on the part or joynt, otherwise the same impression would be made as before, and the like would be much more increased, than drawne out, by the like: for y^e drawing out of cold by cold water, and of fiery heat by heat is agreeable to nature, because every sudden mutation into a contrary is dangerous to it, and lesse acceptable, but that which is by little and little, and as it were by degrees is tolerable: Soe wee say there is one internall fire, and is allready impressed and essentiall in y^e philosophicall subject, another externall, which may also be understood of Mercury: that is an internall fire equivocally because of it fiery qualities, virtues, and operations, externall univocally: Externall fire therefore must be given to the internall, and the like Mercury to Mercury, that the intention of y^e art may be completed: To mollify or mature any hard, or crude thing by coction, wee use fire and water: for water dissolves hardness, and penetrates into y^e compact parts, heat adding strength and motion to it; as appears by example in y^e coction of peas, which are of themselves sound and hard, but doe swell, are broken, and reduced to a pulp by water, y^e heat of fire rarefying y^e water by ebullition, and making it of a more thinne or almost airy substance: Soe the heat of fire, resolves the crude parts of fruit or flesh into water, and causeth them to evaporate with it into aire: after y^e same manner fire and Mercury here are fire and water, and the same; fire and Mercury are the mature and crude parts, of which the crude are to be matured by coction, or the mature purged from superfluities by y^e ministery of fire and water: Now that these are two fires, and two Mercuryes principally and solely necessary to the art, wee will here briefly demonstrate: Empedocles determines two principles of all things, love, and variance, or friendship and dis-

tors. that by variance, corruptions were made, generation by love: such vari=

porate; if it can, water on the contrary extinguishing fire; if admitted to it:
but for all that it is conspicuous that generations may be made of the same

of heaven, and of all corporale things: Fire, as the forme; moves and in=

<margin>NB</margin> or Sulphur, the forme: which two, that they may come to operation, and
move themselves mutually by solution, coagulation, alteration, tinction,
and perfection, have need of externall helpes, as instruments, without
which noe effect could follow: For as a Smith effects nothing without
hammer and fire, soe neither the philosopher without his instruments,
which are water and fire: For this water is called by some the water of
clouds, as this fire occasions fire: It is without doubt called the water of
clouds, because it is distilled, as the dew of May, of most thinne parts: for
as the dew of the sayd month being included in an eggshell is affirmed to
rayse the egge, or its continent, high by the heat of the sunne; soe also
this water of the clouds, or dew makes the <term>philosophers egge</term> ascend, that
is, <term>sublime</term>, <term>exalt</term>, and perfect it: The same water is also most sharp
vinegar, which hath made a body a mere Spirit: for as vinegar is of dif=
ferent qualityes, namely to penetrate to the bottome, and astringe, soe
this water dissolves and coagulates, but is not coagulated, because it is
not of a proper subject. Now this water is had from the fountaine of
<term>Parnassus</term>, which contrary to the nature of other fountaines is at the top
<margin>+ the..</margin> of a mountaine, made by the hoofe of the flying horse <term>Pegasus</term>.
There must also be actuall fire, which notwithstanding must be go=
verned and qualified by its degrees, as by birthes. For as the sunne
proceeding from <term>Aries</term> into <term>Leo</term> gradually increaseth heat to things
growing, by approaching allways nearer, soe is it here necessary to be
done: for the philosophers infant must be nourished by fire; as milk;
the more hee grows, the more fruitfull.

## Embleme 11. Of the Secrets of Nature.
### Whiten Latona and tear your books.

## Epigramme 11.
->Ancestors (of truth noe doubt) so us relate<-
->Latona's twinns produc'd from Jove the great.<-
->Some say shee's Sol with fair Diana mixt,<-
->Having a face most white; some blacke betwixt:<-
->Study my dear! to wash these spots away,<-
->Then burne those books which led your thoughts astray.<-

<vocab>Latona, Sol, Diana, Jove, Alchemy, Embleme</vocab>

--- Page 53 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>Discourse 11.</header>

Soo great is the diversity of Authors in writing, that the exploratory of
truth doe almost despaire of finding the end of art: for Allegoricall Speeches
being of themselves difficult to be understood, and the causes of many errors,
specially then, if the same words be apply’d to different things, and diffe-
rent words to the same things: out of which if a man would extricate him-
selfe, hee must either be of a divine genius, to perceive the truth invol-
ved in soo great darkenesse, or of endlesse pains and charge in proving what
is true, what not: But the philosophers say one is not sufficient without the
other, an acute genius without hand-labour, or this without that, as neither
the theory without the practise, and contrarywise: for noe man is of soo
discreet an understanding as to avoyd a hundred thousand circumvagations,
errors, falsityes as to the word, digressions, and ambiguityes, and persist in the
true path of nature: wherefore say the philosophers, hee that hath not as
yet erred, hath not yet begun, and that errors are the informers of those
things which ought to be done or not: Soo the same doe inculcate, that a
man may spend his whole life; though hee should live a thousand years, in
distilling and redistilling, before hee can attaine to truth by experimentation
alone: That little benefit can be had without study and reading of au-
thors, the corrector of fools signifyes, saying: Study removeth ignorance,
and reduceth a man genius to true understanding, and the knowledge of
every thing: It is therefore principally necessary to acquire the knowledge of
this delectable worke by study, and to sharpen the witt by philosophicall
sayings, the knowledge of truth being contain’d in them: If therefore opera-
tors despise not study, they will sweetly relish the fruit from thence pro-
ceding: but they that abhorre study, and yet are willing to operate, let them
see whether that art be the imitation of nature herselfe, which the art of
her desires to amend: because it is impossible for it to prepare the philoso-
phers secrets to a perfect end: Of those men say the Sophi, that they runne to
practise as an asse to hay, not knowing what to lay his nose to, onely soo far
as the outward senses doe without understanding lead him to see by sight
and tast: these are his words: But lest a man should macerate himselfe with
overmuch study, which is an inexhaustible Sea and of immense profundity
and endeavour (though in vaine) to bring every letter, agreeing also with ma-
ny other things, into act, in the meane time consuming or diminishing his
strength, time, reputation, wealth and riches, the philosophers doe use by
Emblematicall Speeche, that Latona must be whitened, and those books
torne, lest their hearts be broken, for there are many books soo obscurely
written, as to be understood by their owne Authors onely: not a few left out
of envy to seduce others, or rather to retard them, that they may not attain
to their end without difficulty, or to obscure those things written before by
themselves: But what is the meaning of whitening Latona, this is the
worke this the labour to inquire: Clangor bucina affirms Latona to be an
<margin>!!</margin>

<header>Discourse 11.</header>

imperfect body, compounder of Sol and Luna. The most antient poets and
writers affirme Latona to be the mother of Sol and Luna, or Apollo and
Diana, others the Nurse; and that Diana was first borne (for Luna and
whitenesse first appear) and did then performe the office of a midwife
the same day in bringing forth her brother Apollo. Latona was indeed one
<margin>|</margin> of the twelve divine hieroglyphicks of the Egyptians, by whom those
and other Allegoryes were propagated amongst other nations, very few of
the Egyptians priests, and those onely understanding the true meaning
and sense of them, all the rest referring them to other subjects, which were
not in the nature of things, namely various Gods, Goddesses, and the like:
whereupon Latona had there the most sumptuose Temple next to Vulcan, towe-
red and adorned with gold, as shee that was the mother of the philosophicall
Apollo and Diana: but this Latona is browne and blackish, and hath many
moles and blemishes in her face, which must be taken away by art, namely
by dealbation: Some doe make dealbations of Ceruse, Mercury Sublimate,
Talke, reduced into oyle, and the like, with which they incrust, cover, and
soe whiten the outside of the skinne; but these whitening incrustations
fall of with every wine or liquor, because they doe not penetrate to the more
inward parts: But the philosophers care not for such colorations, which are
us’d more to deceive the eyes, than to tinge the internall substance of the
body: For they would have the face of Latona whitened thoroughly, and
the slime itselfe altered, that is, truly, and not superficially: a Question
might be asked, how it can be? I answer, Latona must be first sought out
and known, which though shee be taken out of a vile place, must not=
withstanding be sublimed to a more worthy, but if shee be taken out
of a more worthy place, shee must be throwne into a place more vile,
namely into dung: for there will shee become truly white, and be
made white-lead, which being had, there is noe need to doubt of suc=
cesse, or red lead, which is the begining and end of the worke.

## Embleme 12. Of the secrets of Nature.

### Epigramme 12.

->Know would you why the poets soe recount<-
->the fame of Helicon, the Muses mount?<-
->That monumentall stone is plac'd thereon,<-
->Which Saturne gorg'd instead of Jove his sonne.<-
->Be wise; the sence agrees not with the sound,<-
->For Saturns stone in Chymistry is found.<-

<lang>English</lang>

<header>Discourse 12.</header>
<meta>catchword: is</meta>

The Allegory of Saturne was fine to be taken divers wayes: for the Astro-
nomers repute him the highest of the planetts in order; the young students
of Chymistry the meanest of mettalls, namely lead. The heathen poets con-
ceive him to be the father of Jupiter, sonne of Coelum; the mythologists time:
But though all these may seem to have thought aright in their sense; yet
they will not declare those things which are elswhere spoken of Saturne,
why hee devoures and vomits up his sonne, and a stone instead of Jupiter,
why hee is the discoverer of truth, why appearing with a sieth, a serpent,
blacknesse; morosity, and distorted foot: wherein the last thinke themselves to
interpret best, saying, that time reveals and manifests truth, glides away as a ser-
pent, puts all things downe with death as by a sieth, devoures its children, that is,
all those things which it hath generated, digests hard stones, or cannot consume
them, and therefore doe as it were vomit them up againe: These things doo indeed
agree in part, but are not consonant to the truth of the thing, and all circum-
stances. But the chymical philosophers doo say, that Saturne is first in their
works, which if hee be really present, they cannot erre, truth is discovered in
darknesse: but there is nothing besides blacknesse: wherefore they say in
*Turba philosophorum*, whatsoever colour comes after blacknesse is laudable.
and when you see your matter blacke, rejoyce, because it is the begining of
the worke: And, when it is first blacke, wee say it is the key of the worke,
because it is not effected without blacknesse, as the Rosary hath it, with Arnols,
and the Speculum: when you are operating, indeavour to obtaine first a
blacke colour, and then will you be certaine that you putrefy, and proceed
the right way. And a little after: that blacknesse is called earth, which is made
by light decoction soe often repeated, till blacknesse be most eminent. For
this reason say the same, that Saturne possesseth the earth, Mercury water,
Jupiter aire, Sol fire. Saturne therefore is blacknesse, the discoverer of truth,
who followes a stone instead of Jupiter. For blacknesse, that is, a darke cloud
covers the stone at first, that it cannot be seen: Therefore sayth Morienus
every body without a soule is darke and obscure: And Hermes: Take his body
dissolve it with the sharpest vinegar, or the urine of children, till it be made
darke: & by being performed too long in putrefaction, and the darke cloud which
were upon him, and in his body, before hee dyed, are returned. The stone is
againe disgorged by Saturne; when it becomes white; and then is it sett upon
the top of Helicon for a monument to men, as Hesiodus writes: for whitenesse is
really comprehended under blacknesse, which is drawne out of its bowelles, that
is, out of the stomacke of Saturne. therefore sayth Democritus; purify him
with a peculiar ablution, draw its blacknesse and obscurity from it, and the
whitenesse of it will appeare. And in Turba it is sayd: Joyne the dry with
the moist, that is, the blacke earth with its water, and boil, till it be whitened.
This Arnols in Novum lumen chapt: 4. excellently declares, saying; That
moisture therefore which causes blacknesse in decoction, shows itselfe to be
dryed, when the white colour begins to appear. And a little after: And
my Master sayd to mee, that that purity ascends, because the whitenesse

the successive mutation of all perfect colours: The stone cast out by Saturne is
reported to be placed at the top of the mountains as a monument to men, which
is a thing most fine.
