<header>Discourse 13.</header>

That Naaman the Syrian betooke himselfe to Judaea at the persuasion of the prophet, and wash'd himselfe seven times in the river Jordan, is to be ascribed to his confidence in the prophets words, but that hee was freed from the Leprosy by that washing, is a miracle of divine omnipotence: For the Leprosy which being seated in the blood, and radicale part of mans body, is as it were an universale <term>Cancer</term>, cannot be cured or taken away by any externale washings, much lesse of cold water, such as was of Jordan. After the same manner that the <term>philosophers brasse</term> diseased with an <term>Hydropsy</term>, should be freed from it by the washings of water, and that being imperfect it should be reduced to perfection, and being sicke to health, soe as to be able to comunicate the same to other sicke bodys, is next to a miracle. For such an example is not elsewhere extant in nature, nor is it the ordinary way of nature to produce the philosophers most absolute tincture, unlesse it be governed by art, and fitt subjects administred to it with the externale efficient. Soe the restitution of luxation or dislocation is not peculiar to nature, but art: Nevertheless the <term>Os Sacrum</term> opens itselfe miraculously in the birth of a child, that the infant may thereby have egresse, as by a door, and herein the most great and mercifull God operates by nature above nature: Soe that the stone should be perfected, may seeme to be a thing supernaturale, though really it is naturale: Wherefore sayth a philosopher in the <term>Rosary</term>,

> You must know that our stone is airy and volatile, externally cold and moist, internally hott and dry: and that redness and moisture which is externale, is a watry fume, corrupting, blasting, and destroying itselfe and all things, and flyes from fire: and that heat and siccity which is internale, is gold hott and dry, and is a most pure oyle, penetrating bodys, and is not fugitive, because the heat and <term>siccity</term> of <term>phlegmy</term> tingeth, and nothing els: Cause therefore the externale redness and watry moisture, to be like to the internale heat and siccity, soe that they may agree and be united together, and all at once be made one penetrating, tinging, and fixing body: but these moistures must be destroyed by fire, and degree of fire with soft temperament, and a congruous and moderate digestion.

<margin>NB</margin> These words: But if true, how can it be freed from water by water? but it may be answered, that some certaine waters are hott and dry in quality, such as are many baths, in which it must be philosophically washed: For this is that which they say, wash with fire, and burne with water; for that fire, which washeth, and that water which burneth, differ one from the other in name onely, but agree in effect and operation: That brasse therefore must be washed, that is, dryed from its superfluous moisture, by this water, or this fire: Wee have knowne experimently of hydropicall bodys cured by six months abstinence from any drinke, by being buried in hott sand, or horse dung, by being put in a hott furnace, and sweating, and innumerable other helps, you also by drying baths, such as are at <term>Carolina</term> and <term>Wisbadenses</term> not farre from Mentz: By the same ways must thy patient be cured, sometimes by waters, o

sometimes by the hott aire of furnaces, sometimes by dung, sometimes sand, and by abstaining from drinke: for these are most effectuall remedyes in both cases, how and where to be used: but in alle these things heat is the operator, which drawes forth and consumes the superfluous water by the Emunctoryes or also pores of the body: For externale heat quickens the internale, that is, the vitall Spirit, to expelle that moisture which is noxious to it, as an unprofittable excrement, by which the naturall heat was before suppressd as by an enemy: In this cure there is need of great diligence and precaution, lest whilst one bowell is helpt, another be hurt: In a <term>Quartan</term> (the <term>platonicks</term> probation of a physitian) wee find, that thicke and viscous humors, like the gumme or glew of trees, being gathered together from all the veins and masse of blood, doe descend through the hollow or great veines even to the bottome of the backe, where obstructing the emulgent veins, which draw the serous humor out of the blood, or the passages of them, they are lesse able to operate, and more serous liquor remaines in the body, and soe in a short time, if care be not taken, a <term>Dropsy</term> may happen, the other bowels being also untainted at first: Diureticks doe here little or noe good, purgatives yet lesse, unlesse diminution and reduction be made in some certaine series of time: Sudorificks are also manifestly hurtfull, because they draw out the more subtile part, leaving the thicker, and being continued debilitate the body: for the custome of nature is to find that way to evacuate serosityes through the pores when shee is obstructed about the bladder: This therefore is <term>Scylla</term>, the other <term>Charybdis</term>, both which hee that has a mind to preserve himselfe ought to avoid: But that <term>Dropsy</term> which proceeds from an impaired Liver or Spleen, if it be confirmed, is of most difficult cure: Now in the <term>philosophicale brasse</term> the cure is not impossible, the distemper being rather by accident, and secondary, than essentiale or primary, provided it be cautiously begun, as wee have sayd concerning the plenty of <term>serum</term> in a <term>Quartan</term>, lest that it may not by overmuch exiccation fall into a consumption, or by humectation into a <term>Dropsy</term> difficult to be cured.

<vocab>Quartan, platonicks, physitian, emulgent veins, serous humor, Dropsy, Diureticks, purgatives, Sudorificks, serosityes, Scylla, Charybdis, philosophicale brasse, serum, exiccation, humectation</vocab>

## Embleme 14. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->This is the Dragon which devours his tayle.<-

---

## Epigramme 14.

> Hunger taught polypus his flesh to eat,
> And man with man his appetite to treat:
> The hungry Dragon feeding on his tayle;
> His fainting soule revives, which els would fayle;
> Make him so hard meat, till himselfe hee doo
> Eat and devoure, kill and revive anew.

<vocab>Embleme, Secrets of Nature, Dragon, Epigramme, polypus, alchemy</vocab>

--- Page 62 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>Discourse 14.</header>

It is a saying of the Antients, that a Serpent, which hath devoured a serpent, becomes a Dragon: for it is cruell to its kind, as a thiefe and murderer to his. It is evident that such are in Africa, of soe great bignesse and plenty, that they destroyed a great part of Alexanders army: Amongst the Asachaei a people of Ethiopia are very great producers, which being wrapped together after the manner of hurdles doe with their heads lifted up make way to better pasture: They report that the Kings of India maintains two Dragons, one of eighty, and the other of ninety cubits in bignesse: by the observation of later writers and now adayes it is known, that near Angola are found such as are equall to the great masts of ships: Soe they report that in certaine mountains of India and Africa there is great store of gold, but kept by Dragons, lest any one come thither and take it away: For Dragons doe resort to fountains or rivers, which fall from the mountains, and soe are sayd to watch the gold by accident: for this reason doe the philosophers ascribe soe many Dragons or serpents to their treasures, as to the golden fleece, the garden of the Hesperides, and to other persons or <term>Chymicall subjects</term>, to Cadmus, Saturne, Aesculapius, Mercury, whose wand is begirt with two serpents, male and female: but doe they means nothing else by Dragons, but Chymicale subjects? thereupon say they, the Dragons yeild mountains, and the earth fountains to Study, and denoting the extreme hunger of the dragon in devouring his tayle: which though others may interpret of the year returning into itselfe, and resembling a circle, yet it was accomodated by the philosophers to their things, who by this dragon would have a Serpent understood, which devouours another of its owne kind, and this is properly called <term>Sulphur</term>, as they all attest in innumerable places: Lully in his little booke chaph: 31. This is, sayth hee, Sulphur, my Sonne! and this the serpent and dragon devouring his tayle, the roaring lyon, and sharp sword, cutting, mortifying, and daring all things. And the <term>Rosary</term>: The Dragon dyes not, unlesse hee be killed with his brother and sister. And a little after: The Dragon is <term>Argent vive</term> extracted from bodys, having in it a body, soule, and spirit: This is in the same place called by another name ffatid water, which is produced after the separation of the Elements: Now the Dragon is sayd to devour his tayle, in consuming the voluble, venomous, and moist part, that after without a tayle hee may seem more corpulent and of lesse speed, for the motion and volubility of him consisteth cheifely in his tayle: Other animalls depend upon feet, but dragons, serpents, and wormes of that kind doe use constriction and explication of body in stead of feet, and as it were water poured out doe divide into certaine courses, inclining themselves sometimes this way sometimes that, as you may see in almost all rivers, how they turne their courses like serpents, and runne obliquely in circuits: They did not therefore without reason call <term>Argent vive</term> a Serpent, and add serpents to Mercury, it also drawing as it were a tayle, and running sometimes this sometimes that way with a voluble poise: For as a Serpent creeps soe also Mercury, who hath therefore wings upon his feet and head.

<vocab>Dragon, Serpent, Alexander, Asachaei, Ethiopia, India, Angola, Hesperides, Cadmus, Saturne, Aesculapius, Mercury, Chymicall, Sulphur, Lully, Rosary, Argent vive, ffatid water</vocab>

<lang>English</lang>

<header>Discourse 14.</header>

In Affrica they say are flying serpents, which would waste and depopu-
late all things, if they were not destroyed by the bird called Ibis: For which
cause Ibis is erected amongst the sacred Egyptian images, both for the mani-
fest utility, which it performes to the whole country, and for an occult
reason, which very few men have understood. The dragon biting of his
tayle, and casting his old skinne is sayd to acquire a new one together with
youth, as nature hath granted longer life not onely to crows, Ravens, Eagles,
and Hartes, but also to the serpentine kind: The Ant growing old acquireth
wings, soe also many wormes: Man growing old is put into the earth, but
being revived from the earth will be consecrated to eternall life. Of every
serpent burned is made a powder, which is taken safely against all poy-
sons with great effect: Such an <term>Alexipharmacum</term> ought also to be made
of this dragon (who hath already devoured his tayle, which is usually putt
of in vipers) a most effectuall remedy against the adversitys of for-
tune and body.

### Embleme 15. Of the Secrets of nature.

->Let the worke of the potter, consisting in siccity and moisture,<-
->instruct you.<-

### Epigramme 15.

->Water the potter mixing with his clay,<-
->Makes vessells shape, by wheele without delay:<-
->This thriftly doe by aime and art expresse;<-
->The thirsty earth with liquor to refresh:<-
->Thy road is safe, proceed, and doe not burne<-
->Drowne not your earth, nor suffer it to burne.<-

<vocab>siccity, moisture, potter, liquor, Embleme, Epigramme, alchemy</vocab>

--- Page 65 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>51</page-num>
<header>Discourse 15.</header>

As this orbe is by the connexion of earth and water conglobated into one
round body, soo alsoe the potters worke seems to be compounded of the
same elements particularly, namely of siccity and moisture, that one
may regulate the other: for if the earth should be without water, nei-
ther the Ocean, Sea, lakes, rivers or fountains would be near the earth,
it could bear nothing of itselfe, but remains barren: soe if water should
not be received into the cavitys of the earth, but stand about, it would
easily cover the whole earth, and soo the same remaine inhabitable:
but one entring amicably into the other, and water moderating the
siccity of the earth, the earth the moisture of that, by their mutuall
comixtion, hereby is the fertility and utility of both elements conspi-
cuous: In like manner the potter mixeth clay with water, to make
thereof a tractable masse, which hee shapes by his wheele, and setts in
the warme aire, that it may be gradually dryed; then adds violence of
fire, that his vessells may be well hardened, and condensed into a durable
stone, which can resist both water and fire: Soe alsoe must wee proceed
in the naturall worke as the philosophers attest, and therefore doe affirme
that example ought to be taken from the pottery. For as to siccity and
moisture, that is, earth and water, wee doubt not that there is much
affinity in both, but much difference in the way of portion, and in the
matter and forme of compounding the elements: For pottery vessells have
an artificiall forme, the philosophicall tincture a forme altogether natu-
rall, and soo much more noble than they, by how much the more excel-
lent the matter of this is than theirs: either is indeed a worke of earth, but
in the philosophicall there is nothing sayd to be, which hath not ascended
and attained to the heaven of aire, in that a thicke and feculent earth is
predominant: The effect of both is a stone, the one a common, the other a philo-
sophicall: by which name a certaine person being seduced put a great
number of artificiall stones or beryls into one chest, and the whiter sort
of flint into another, using Diabolicall conjurations from above, by
which the last were at a certaine time to be converted into silver, the
first into pure gold: but they being supposed to be the philosophers
stones, and therefore a great summe of mony accrewing to purchase se-
verall things, when the new gold and silver might be expected, at a
certaine time, in which it not appearing, nor the stones converted into
gold, as was desired, death put an end to the folly and shame: For gold
or silver ought not to be expected from that thing, in which it is not na-
turally; diabolicall Magick having noe place in these divine works, but
is as farre different from them, as the author of it from a devout and
pious man, hell from heaven: Soe though it may be esteemed the true
philosophicall stone, yet let not any man perswade himselfe, that im-
possible things can be performed by it, as Isaac admonisheth: noe man
being bound either by the laws of nature, or polity to things im=

possible: as for the transmutation of gemms by it, and performing the
malleability of glass: any man may thereby perceive, whether these
things are possible, and congruous to thy nature, or noe: Geber affirms
that the philosopher speaks many things allegorically, as also of him-
selfe, that where hee spoke clearly and plainly, hee sayd nothing, but
where figuratively, there hee absconded the truth, as wheat under
chaffe: These things which a man sowes, the same also will hee reap, which takes
place in vegetables and animals, though different species may sometimes proceed
from the things sowed: whether these things ought to be applyed to metals,
which are not propagated by seed, as these, must be considered: In these the
parts onely are homogeneouy, Sulphur, and Argent vive, in those they are hete-
rogeneous or organicall: In these are noe receptables of food, but in those:
In those there is not found any nutrition, augmentation, or extension in all
dimensions, in these very much: Yea these are elemented, waighty admitting
mixtion alone, those also besides mixtion have either a vegetive or sensitive
soule: Nevertheless it is without doubt true that there is something in places
under the earth, which is not gold now, but will, after a thousand years be
gold by nature: Who will deny this to be the analogicall food of gold? both
gold and the aurifick nature being of one originall and radix, though
this of the more noble form: and therefore the food of gold being known,
this also will be manifest: The philosopher affirms purity and mois-
ture or Sulphur and Argent vive to be that, which being most pure must
be extracted out of two mountains.

# Embleme 16. Of the Secrets of Nature.
## One Lyon hath wings, the other none.

### Epigramme 16.

The Lyon prince of beasts in might and sway
Fights without fear, and scornes to runne away:
Give him a spouse with wings of his owne race;
To mount him with herselfe from place to place:
But hee immovee stands, and nulls her force;
By Emblems to demonstrate Natures course.

<vocab>Embleme, Secrets of Nature, Lyon, Epigramme, beasts, immovee, Natures course</vocab>

--- Page 68 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>Discourse 16.</header>

It is a thing knowne by experience that a Lyon excells other animalls not soe much in bignesse and strength of body, as generosity: if hee be seen in hunting, being ashamed to runne away, hee retyreth himselfe by degrees, if hee be oppressed by a multitude: being removed from the view of the hunters, hee makes hast away, thinking it a dishonour to be expulst by ab-sconding: hee desires a wood in flight, which hee useth, when anything pursues: his bones are solid and not at all empty, which are sayd to be soe hard, that being shakt together they yield fire as out of a steel and flint. Hee fears fire above all things. Hee seems to derive his substance from the nature of the sunne, for in force and heat hee excells other animalls, as the sunne doth the starry: Besides hee is allwayes beheld with fiery and open eyes, as the Sunne beholds the earth with an open and fiery eye: a Lioness fighting for her whelps fixeth her eyes upon the ground, that shee may not be afraid of the hunters staffs and spears. when a Lyon perceives the coition of a Leopard, hee takes revenge upon the Lioness for adultery, and inflicts severe punishment; The Lioness therefore washeth away the offense and sent in a river, or being conscious of the trespasse doth also accompany the adulterer flying for fear of the male: Which wonderfull nature of this beast the philosophers contemplating, have produced severall allegories of the Lyon, as it were hieroglyphicall writings testifying of their secret works, observing the Lyon to be a constant, firme animale, without deceit and suspition, they assimilated the better part of their phi-losophicall composition to him: for as hee flyes not, soe neither they: hee with solid bones, soe they fixed and not to be overcome: and as the Lioness is not allwayes innocent and free from adultery, soe is neither Luna nor Mercury without some blemish, but is joyned by the ignorant sometimes to this, sometimes to that matter, where-by an adulterous conjunction of things discrepant and different in nature, rather than true matrimony is established: for the products of a Leopard and Lioness have noe specious and comely manes about the necke and shoulders, with which ensignes those generated by the Lyon are alone adorned. The philosophicall Lioness must therefore be joyned to her proper mate or male, and a genuine and generous whelp will from them spring, which may easily be knowne by his paw. But the Lioness must not be any or what you please, but winged, which may be able to fight and contest with the Lyon, depending upon the swiftnesse of her plumes, lest (to witt) shee should be oppressed by his extraordi-nary wrath, but to thinke upon escape, if at any time hee fall into fury without cause, for hee when hee stops her flying

is united with greater love towards her, friendship being made after discord.
But you will say, who ever saw a Lioness with wings, or what can the plumes
of a Lioness avail? There is a deep valley near the mountain Cytheron,
in which are seen none but flying Lionesses, and at the top of the same
mountains resort a red Lyon of that kind, which ravously kills. The
Lyon therefore must be taken, and brought into the valley, and forthwith
coupled to the winged Lioness: she also will easily suffer herself to
be overcome, as like by like: afterwards they must be both advanced
out of the sayd valley to the top of the mountains, and henceforth they
will never desert one another, but always remaines together in inviolable
amity. The taking of Lyons, I confess, is not easy, but obnoxious to many
dangers, nevertheless it must be attempted: a Lyon feeds not with the
Lioness, but strays apart, as story relates, therefore they are to be found and
hunted severally: But if the Lyons whelps be obtained in their infancy, when
they first begin to goe, which is at two moneths age, and be joyned, as afore-
said, being come to good growth and stature, the thing will be free from
any danger. Moreover they breed in the spring time, which must be well
observed: but Lyons after whelping using crosse and winding wayes, to
prevent the discovery of their denns, therefore must they be sought and
their whelps taken away with very great diligence and care.

### Embleme 17. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Fower orbes governe this worke of fire.<-

### Epigramme 17.

> Consult with Nature, and four orbes acquire,
> Excited with a soft internall fire:
> The first and lowest Vulcan doth declare,
> Hermes the next, the third is Luna's sphere,
> Phœbus the fourth is thine and Natures fire:
> By these young Students may to truth aspire.

<vocab>Embleme, Epigramme, Secrets of Nature, Vulcan, Hermes, Luna, Phœbus, orbes, internall fire</vocab>

--- Page 71 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>Discourse 17.</header>
<meta>catchword: not</meta>

The philosophers have in many places made mention of four sorts of fire necessary to the naturall works; namely Lully, the Author of Scala, Ripley, and many others: and so this one Raymund speaking of fires (sayth Scala). You must note, that here are contrary operation: because as the fire against nature dissolves the spirit of a fixed body into the water of a cloud, and binds the body of a volatile spirit into a congealed earth, soe contrarywise the fire of nature congeales the dissolved spirit of a fixed body into a globous earth, and resolves the fixed body of a volatile spirit by fire against nature, not into the water of a cloud, but into philosophicall water. Ripley speaks more clearly of those fires in gate 3. thus: There are four sorts of fires, which you ought to know, the naturall, unnaturall, fire against nature, and elementall, which kindleth wood: These fires were used, and noe more. Fire against nature ought to excruciate bodys, that is the dragon, as I told you, violently burning, as the fire of hell. The fire of nature is the third menstruum, that fire is naturally in every thing: Occasioned fire wee call unnaturall, as the heat of ashes and baths to putrefy: Without these fires you can bring nothing to putrefaction, whereby your matter may be separated, that it may at once be proportioned for a new conjunction: Make therefore a fire within in your glass, which can burne bodys more effectually, than elementall fire: these are their sayings. They are indeed called fires, because they have a fiery virtue, the naturall in coagulating, the unnaturall in dissolving, the fire against nature in corrupting, the elementall in administring the first heat and motion: And there is a concatenat order observed in them, that the second may be incited by the first, the third by the second, the fourth by the third, and first together to action, soe as that one is the agent, and the other the patient, and the same both agent and patient in different respect. That which is observed in iron rings or pillars held together by a Magnet, and joyned by mutuall contact, the same in these fires: for the elementall doth like the magnet immit its virtue through the second and third even to the fourth, and joynes one to another by mutual operations, and causes them to stick together, till internall action be effected amongst the uppermost: The first is elementall fire indeed and name, the second is aire or airy, the third watery or of a Lunar nature, the fourth earthy: It is needless to say any thing of the first, because it is knowne to every mans eye and touch: The three other are dragons, menstrues, watery, Sulphury, and Mercurys: Dragons, because being participants of venonosity, they devoure serpents of their owne kind, soft and alter, that is, dissolve and coagulate bodys incorporated and mixed with them: They are called Menstrues, because the philosophicall infant is produced from them, and nourished therewith even to its nativity: Lully in his booke of the Quintessence, dist. 3. hath two

<vocab>Lully, Scala, Ripley, Raymund, dragon, menstruum, sulfur, mercury, quintessence, elementall fire, philosophicall water, venonosity, putrefaction</vocab>

<header>Discourse 17.</header>
<meta>catchword: contrary</meta>

<term>menstruum</term>, the vegetable, and minerall; <term>Ripley</term> in his preface of the gates hath three, which are really one and doe agree: for the generation of the infant is made of them all together, and a white water preceds the birth of it, which is not of the substance but superfluity of the infant, and therefore to be separated: They are watery, because in fire they show a watery nature; that is, flowing and liquidity, which agrees with water: Manifest it is that the propertys of watery are different and wonderfull, whereas some doe putrefy, being coagulated into very hard stones fitt for building: Very like these are the philosophers minerale waters, which doe condense, and turne into a stony consistence: They are alsoe called <term>Sulphurs</term> because of the virtue of <term>Sulphur</term>, which they comprehend in them, for the sulphur of nature is mixed and made one with the other sulphur, and the two <term>Sulphurs</term> are dissolved by one; and one is separated from two, and the <term>Sulphurs</term> are preserved and contained by <term>Sulphur</term>, as <term>Eximiding</term> in <term>Turba</term> sayth: Now what <term>Sulphurs</term> are <term>Dardary</term> there declares in these words: <term>Sulphurs</term> are souls hidden in the fower elements, which being extracted by art doe naturally containe one another and are joyned together: if you can by water governe and purify well that which is hidden in the belly of <term>Sulphur</term>, that hidden thing meeting with its owne nature rejoyceth, even as water with its like. I will now tell you (sayth <term>Mosing</term> also) what it is: the first indeed, which is fiery <term>Argent vive</term>, the second, the body compounded in it, and the third is the water of <term>Sulphur</term>, by which the first is washed, and dilatated, and governed, till the worke be accomplished: That which is sayd of <term>Sulphurs</term>, the same may be understood of soe many <term>Mercurys</term>: For thus saith the same <term>Mosing</term> afterwards: <term>Argent vive</term> <term>Lambar</term> is the <term>Magnesia</term>, but <term>Argent vive</term> or <term>Auripigment</term> is that <term>Sulphur</term> which ascends from a mixed compound: But I will omitt the inference of more testimonys, they being infinite and obvious to every man: These foure fires are included as it were in spheres and orbes, that is, every one hath a particular center from which or to which their motion tends, but nevertheless they are kept soe bound together partly by nature, partly by art, that one can operate little or nothing without another, yea the action of one is the passion of another, and on the contrary —

## Embleme 18. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Fire loues to burne, not to aurify, but gold.<-

### Epigramme 18.

> All natures agents doe their seed diffuse,
> That thus they may their species produce:
> Soe fire makes all things fire, nor ever was
> A true effect without its proper cause:
> Gold generates noe fire, fire noe gold indeed;
> All things are propagated by their seed.

<vocab>Nature, Fire, Gold, Seed, Species, Aurify, propagated</vocab>

--- Page 74 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>Discourse 18.</header>

Natures way of operating in all individually of the universe is to use one single processe to complete and perfect one single motion, as appeares in the Anatomy of mans body, in which one muscle serves onely for one motion, that is, the attractive; but for the expansive another opposite to the first, soe that if any part or member be to be brought into a circular forme, it must be effected by various muscles placed circle-wise: Soe the operation of fire is one and single, that is to say, to heat and fire, yea assimilate to itselfe, and burns all things to which it is applyed, if they be combustible: Hereupon sayth <term>Avicenna</term> in his booke of the <term>congelation of stones</term>: That which falls into salt pitty, becomes salt, and that which into fire, becomes fire, but somethings sooner, some slower, according to the power of active, and the resistence of passive: And there is a place in Arabia, which coloureth all bodyes in it of its owne colour: Soe every naturall thing possesseth the virtue infused into it by nature, by which it acts upon things mixt or applyed to it, assimilating and altering their nature and forme: That which is generation in vegetables and animals by the propagation of seed, is the infusion of virtue and assimilation in single bodyes or singly mixt: the Sunne, the light of heaven, projects its rayes upon the earth, which, being gathered together in concavous or burning glasses, doe demonstrate themselves to be produced from such a cause, as to appear to be the projectible formes of the Sunne: Whereby it is manifest that the rayes of the Sunne are nothing else, but a fiery flame extended and dispersed into an ample latitude, which being dilated and againe contracted by concavous, diaphanous and circular or proportioned instruments, doth in concavous and steele mirrors shine as a flame, and burns things obvious to it: after the same manner, a certaine virtue as a vapour dispersed in a certaine elementar body, which, if it be gathered together, and attracted into one, turns into water, and that water into earth: For the reason sayth <term>Avicenna</term> in the booke before alledged, Earth is made of water, when the qualityes of earth overcome it, and soe on the contrary: But there is a certaine thing, which some ingenious men doe use when they have a mind to coagulate a dry substance, which is compounded of two watery, and is called <term>Lac Virginis</term>, which is the most certaine effect of it: thus saith hee: Some <unclear>xxxx</unclear> there are that assure themselves able to increase or multiply virtues in the <term>Load-stone</term>, as the like wee have seen felt in silver, which being scarce one pound weight attracts and holds up an iron anchor of 28 pounds, which would have been impossible, if the virtue had not been increased and made stronger in it, which without doubt was done by revocation of the dispersed virtues as it were into one point or pole, or by the attraction of the same out of a great body into a lesser: Others are found, who say that a <term>plumbifying stone</term> may be made by the <term>sulphurous breath of Saturne</term>, infused and retained, till it be thereby converted into <term>common Mercury</term>, which continually converts <term>common Mercury</term> into lead. Some boast that they can by <term>Antimony</term>, or the <term>stellated Regulus</term> thereof make Copper from the fume of Copper, in that time wherein a man may eat an egge, yea that they have made all metalls: but I will not detract from their reputation, though to mee it seems not

likely to be true: I know not whether they are more confident or 
successfull, who endeavour to deduce gold from gold, according to the 
saying of the golden poet:
> Hee that desires barley, sows only barley; in gold is the seed of gold:
Every naturall thing hath indeed a certaine 
virtue of multiplying itselfe; but these things are brought into action 
in vegetables and animals only, not at all in metals, minerals, things 
dugge out of the earth, or meteors: Some plants sprung from a small 
graine of seed doe oftentimes yield a thousand or more grains of seed, 
and soe multiply and propagate themselves, and that yearly; Ani=
malls alsoe have their products of greater or lesse number, according 
to the nature of every one: But gold, silver, lead, tinne, iron, copper, or 
<term>Argent vive</term> are never found to have multiplyed themselves or their species 
after that manner, though one may be very often found commuted and 
nobilitated into another: Nevertheless the philosophers affirme that the 
principle of ignifying is in fire, soe of <term>aurifying</term> in gold: but where is 
the <term>tincture</term>, by which gold must be made? This must be sought in 
its owne principles and generation, not in things of another nature: For 
if fire produceth fire, a peare a peare, a horse a horse, then lead must 
generate lead, and not silver, gold gold and not the tincture: Moreover 
the philosophers have a peculiar gold, which they doe not only not 
deny that it ought (but alsoe affirme it necessarily requisite) to be 
added to the <term>aurifick Stone</term> as a <term>ferment</term> at the end of the worke. The 
ferment converting the thing fermented into its owne nature, without 
which the whole composition would never come to perfection.

## Embleme 19. Of the secrets of Nature.

->If you kill one of the fower, they will all suddenly dye.<-

### Epigramme 19.

> Beholde foure brethren how together stand,
> One having earth, water the next in hand,
> The third holds aire, and fire the fourth alone.
> If you woulde all destroy, kill onely one,
> And they will all forthwith resigne to death,
> Their lives depending joyntly on one breath.

<header>Discourse 19.</header>

The poetts doe feigne Geryon king of Spayne to have been tricorporall,
who had oxen of a purple colour, which a dogge with two heads, and a Dragon
with seven were charg’d with as warders: and the same Geryon is reported to be
the sonne of Chrysaor sprung from the blood of Medusa, and that Dragon the
sonne of Typhon, and Echidna: which things agreeing with noe hystory, or truth
of the matter, as it sounds, and making a noyse in all <term>Chymicall Allegoryes</term>,
they are not without cause hereto by us: But by the three bodyes of Goryon wee
meane three faces beholds in one father, according to the sense of <term>Hermes</term>, or, as
others would have it, foure; having regard to soo many <term>Elements</term>: For of a Qua=
drangle ought a triangle to be made, as that made of a circle, soo this to re=
turne into a circle: for the consanguinity and naturall conjunction of the
bodyes of Goryon, or the Elements is soe great, that one being overcome or
kills, the rest alsoe dye of themselves and putrefy, without the administration
of any manuall force: As to things with two bodyes it is knowne, that one being
dead, the other alsoe wastes and consumes: as in Italy wee have seen a boy
foure years of age with two bodys, who <unclear>absteeded</unclear> the head of his brother, bearing
to him, within his owne body, the other party hung downe to the navile, where
they were bound or joynd together: But if hee, that hung downe, and being
much lesser, was carryed by the other, should be toucht about the hands or
feet too hard, the bigger did hereby feele paine, yea alsoe hunger, if the lesser
wanted food: And this is the combination and sympathy of nature, whereby
the members and parts of one body, or joynd with another are mutually
moved and affected together; whereof if one be sound and unhurt, yet
the other doe not thereby necessarily continue sound and unhurt, but if
greivously hurt, the rest doe alsoe sympathize, and perish by the same
malady: soo if a neighbour gaine much, noe profit thereby accrues to his
neighbour, but if hee suffer loss by fire, his neighbour receives much
damage: for your affaires are in danger when your neighbours house hath
taken fire: It is not therefore a thing contradictory to truth, for the other
three to dye, by the destruction of one of these foure brethren, seing that
it may happen divers wayes, either by finding the same ends as beginnings
of life being borne at one birth both by father and mother, as wee
have read has happened to some, perhaps by the inclination of the
Starrs, or by being joyned together not onely in soules, but alsoe ligaments
of the body, or by confederation of mind, by strong imagination in time
of pestilence, or by the vow of a league: A certaine heathenish people
live by the name of <term>pythagoreans</term> in the Indyes under the govern=
ment of the great <term>Mogulle</term> (who now reignes there the minorit of the
Successor of <term>Tamberlan</term>) amongst whome this antient custome is hither=
to observed, that if the husband dye, his wife is burnt with fire, or as
now, lives perpetually in the highest infamy, deserted by all, and es=
teemed as a dead woman, which was therefore done, that the wives
might not kill themsolves by poyson for their husbands, unless alsoe

<header>Discourse 19.</header>

they were willing to dye: soo in the philosophicale worke, one of the bre=
thren being dead, the other doe allsoe perish by fire, not compulsively, but
voluntarily, that they may not survive in sorrow and infamy: or if one be
assaulted with a club, sword, or stone, hee will commence civill warre with
his brethren, as appears in the Gyants sprung from the teeth of a Dragon
opposing Jason, and elsewhere allso Cadmus, and soo they will all fall by
mutuall destruction: For touch or hurt him that carryes aire, and hee will
insurge against those two together which are nearest to him, namely him
that carryes water, and him that carryes fire, and those will on both sides
oppose themselves against him that carryes earth, and him that first promo=
ted the quarrell, till they have given and received mutuall wounds, of
which they dye: For it is thus decreed amongst the brethren, that the more
earnestly and vehemently they love one another, if once they begin to hate,
they are not to be reconciled, but pursue one another to death, as the most
bitter vinegar is generated from the sweetest honey in a stomacke too hott, or the
Liver being corrupted. Kill therefore him that is alive, but soo, as being dead,
to quicken him againe, otherwise the death of him killed avayles you nothing;
for death will manifest him, that is to say, after resurrection, and death,
darknesse, and the sea will fly from him, as <term>Hermes</term> asserts, and the Dragon
which observes the holes will avoyd the rayes of the sunne, and our dead
<margin>Chapt 9.</margin> Sonne liveth, and the King cometh by fire: <term>Bolinus</term> in his Metaphor in the
Rosary signifyes the same thing, saying: And let this be done, when you
have drawne mee partly from my nature, and my wife partly from her
nature, you must then allso kill the natures, and wee are quickened by
a new and spirituall resurrection, soo that afterwards wee cannot dye.

## Embleme 20. Of the secrets of Nature.

### Nature teacheth nature how to subdue fire.

## Epigramme 20.

Fire that insulting dragon is prepar'd
To scorch the virgins beauty sans regard:
Distressed for help shee <insert>spyes</insert> <unclear>lookes for</unclear> a man of warre
Equipp'd, all, cap a pe; as such men are;
Who, shielding her from harme; assaults the flames,
And by this president her fear proclaims.

<vocab>Embleme, Nature, fire, dragon, man of warre, president, cap a pe</vocab>

--- Page 80 ---
<lang>en</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>Discourse 20.</header>

The common token and symbole, by which the philosophers know one another, is, that nature is guided, taught, governed, subdued and overcome by nature, as a young scholar by a mistresse, a waiting mayd by her Lady, a subject by a Queen, yea a daughter by the mother, and kinswoman by kinswoman: how true it is, appears by dayly experience, in the education of youth amongst men, and other actions, as literary discipline or institution, government, and the like. Pliny writes of the Nightingale, that one teacheth, attends, observes, imitates, overcomes another in singing, or being overcome grieves, and that sometimes being outdone and vanquished in the conflict, her throat being broken, it perisheth, and in the midst of singing falls downe dead: Wee see also how all sorts of birds doe begin to accustome and instruct their young being yet unfethered, and tender for flying, that not onely nature, but also art or use hath begotten in them the habit of flying, though nature alone gave power and organs to exercise that action, without which neither institution or art can find foundation or place: soe colts are taught to runne by the mare, whelps to barke by the bitch, and young foxes to craft by the dam, nor is there any animate and sensitive nature or species of nature found, which guides, instructs, and governs not another nature, as it minor, or suffers it selfe to be overcome by another, as a parent: Wee doe not perceive such discipline in vegetables, yet notwithstanding the use is observed in them, and that many hands are of much efficacy; for whilst corne is in the blade, it may be cleansed from incommodious thistles and tares, whilst a tree is yet a twig it may be bent, and made to grow as you please: soe in metals and philosophicall subjects one nature keeps, preserves, and defends another nature in fire, as is knowne to founders and lesters, but especially to the masters of naturall things: Iron is very helpfull and as a midwife to silver and gold, being yet very tender and spirituall, as they call it, mixed in its myne with Cadmia, Arsenick, or Antimony the thistle, if it be cast into mynes to be burned in a fire of furnaces: after the same manner iron it selfe, if it be intended to be changed into steele, is saved from burning by white stones found at the sea shores; some doe cast the powder of crystall glasse, or the gall of glasse upon metallick powders which are to be dissolved, that they may not perish by overmuch fire: Instead of this gall of glasse the philosophers doe use their Eudica, which Morienus say is also the gall of glasse, and to be had in glassie vessells: for the heat of fire consumes the body with hasty burning: but Eudica applied will free bodies, & changes into earth from any burning: for bodies, when now they doe not retaine their soules, are soon burned: Eudica (the fax of glasse) is indeed very agreeable to all bodies, for it vivifyes and prepares them, and defends them from all burning: These are the sayings of Morienus the Roman: This therefore is the nature, which teacheth another nature to resist fire, and be inured to it, which is the Mistresse instructing the

power into action, but not perfect all things in a moment, but one thing
after another: nor this alone; but shee constitutes allso her deputy in the
first place, to which shee leaves the power of life and death, that is, of
forming other things, as for example, in the generation of man shee ~
useth a long processe, as of tenne months, where first according to the
opinion of Aristotle, shee fabricates the heart, as the vicar, deputy, or
principale bowell, and then the heart delineates, formes, and perfects
the other parts necessary to nutrition, the life, senses, and generative
power, and comunicates to them life and vivificke spirits by systole ~
and diastole; or dilatation and compression of the artery-es, soe long ~
as it is not impeded by diseases and violence: And thus one nature ~
begeteth another, which you must observe and follow for an example
of the philosophicall worke, as a thing most clear and evident. . .

<lang>English</lang>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>Discourse 21.</header>

It is the doctrine of that most famous philosopher plato that knowledge is as
it were actually engraven and imprinted in the mind of man, which is as the
foundation of all arts and sciences, by the remembrance and repetition of
which all learning may be knowne and apprehended by any man; which
to prove; hee introduceth a youth yet very young, ignorant, and illiterate,
whome hee instructes first in geometricale interrogations; soe, that the
youth was observed to answer directly to all questions, and, not by usery, or
without consideration to have attained to the very marrow of soe intricate
a science: From whence hee concludes that noe doctrine and discipline is
learned or understood at the first, but onely by remembrance and repeti-
tion recalled and revolved into the mind by children, alluding to his An-
nus magnus, wherein before the solary of 48 thousand years, the same
persons, things and actions were sayd by him to have been before the re-
volution of the heaven, after the same manner, as now: But those things
every man understands are like dreams without any foundation of truth:
That there are some certain Sparks of knowledge naturally radicated in
us, and more power, to be reduced into action by use and instruction, wee
doe not deny, but wee deny that they are soe great and of such quality,
as to be the seminaryes of arts and sciences without any precedent
praparation: From whence therefore came Sciences and arts, if men in-
vented them not, others may aske the question, whether they were first
taught by heaven, or by the Gods of the heathen? I answer, it is one
thing to say, that burning coales are covered under ashes in soe great plenty,
that if they may onely appear by removing the ashes, they are sufficient
to boyle meat, or warme our cold limbs; it is another thing to affirme, that
small sparks onely doe there lye hid, which, before they can be usefull
for boyling or warming, must by the industry, art, and care of man be
stir'd up, quickened, and nourished with fuell, otherwise they may easily
be extinguished, and wholy reduced into cold ashes: The last is the asser-
tion of the Aristotelians, the first of the platonists: With the last agrees
reason and experience, with the first onely phantasy or imagination: Where-
upon it might be demanded, why plato wrote upon the doore of his Schoole,
that hee that was ignorant of Geometry should not be admitted into it, hee
also having affirmed that children actually know it! are men more unlear-
ned than boyes? or have those of full age forgotten those things which
boyes know? that cannot be supposed: because wee see that a brute doth
by the instinct of nature abhorre and avoid the danger of fire, water,
sudden chance, and the like, though newly come into the world, yet that
an infant knows not or shunns such things, except hurt, or his finger being
a little burnt at the flame of a candle like a Fire-flye, which burns it
wings and falls downe: why doe not the young bee, the flye and gnatt
fly directly into fire, they not knowing by experience the danger from
thence arising? because nature hath taught them, but not a child now

<header>Discourse. 21.</header>

borne. If Geometry be soe naturale and easy to children, how comes it to passe
that the square of a circle was not knowne to plato himselfe, soe that Aris-
totle the schollar of plato affirmes it possible to be knowne, but not as yet
knowne; but it is evident that it was not unknowne to the naturale philoso-
phers, because they command a circle to be converted into a quadrangle; and
this, by a triangle, againe into a circle: by which they meane a most simple
body without angles, as by a quadrangle, the foure Elements, as if they
should say, the most simple corporale figure, that can be found, must be
taken, and that divides into four elementale colours, and to be a quadrangle
equilaterale. Now that this quadration is physicall, and agreable to nature,
every man understands; by which farre more utility accrues to a Commonwealth
as alsoe more illustration to the mind of man, than by that mathematicale
and merely theoricale or from an abstracted matter: To learne that perfect-
ly, a Geometritian acting about solid bodyes must inquire what depth of a
solid figure, for example, of a sphere and Cube can be knowne, and transferre
to manuale use or practise: If the capacity or circumference of a sphere be
of 32 foot, how much one of the sides of the Cube will be, to equalize the
capacity of this sphere; on the contrary, if a Sphere containe 32 measures
in soe great a circumference, how much will one side of the Cube be, to
containe soe much, or by looking backe from the measures, which the sphere
or cube containes, to the feet of every circumference: In like manner the
philosophers would have the quadrangle reduced into a triangle, that is, into
body, Spirit, and Soule, which three doe appear in three previous colours
before rednesse; for example, the body or earth in the blacknesse of Saturn,
the Spirit in a lunar whitenesse, as water, the Soule or aire in a Solar
citrinity: then will the triangle be perfect, but this likewise must be
changed into a circle, that is, into an invariable rednesse: By which ope-
ration the woman is converted into the man, and made one with him,
and the senary the first number of the perfect compleated by one, two, three,
having returned againe to an unitt, in which is eternale rest and peace.

<header>Discourse 22.</header>

As Mercuriall Statues are comonly placed in roads where three wayes meet, to-
gether with a title and insription, to guide doubtful travellers into the right
way: soe also are certaine acute sentences inserted in many of their books
and volums of the philosophers, here and there allegoricall and dubious, that
the investigator of truth may thereby be admonished, and led as it were by
the hand into the right path: of these this emblematicall inscription is one,
whereof this is accounted the meaning, that lead must be made of the phi-
losophicall brasse, and tinne of lead, which by Geber is called white lead,
who teacheth also, which way Saturne may by washing with Mercury be
converted into Jupiter; wherefore this index, though the speech of Battus,
must be credited, if at any time hee discover the philosophicall open,
what place they frequent, saying they were in the mountains, and were
under those mountains: for many men, as Arnolde in **Novum lumen** Chap: 1.
affirms, wandring in the mountains doe not know these animalls: but they
are sold openly at a very small price: In the summer also Snow is some-
times observed on the highest mountains, and clouds most comonly, by
which as by a vapour and water lead being blacke is washed, and turned in-
to whiteness: but their crystals are found congealed and hardened by ice
in the lowest valleys and mynes, together with **Lapis specularis**, and Talke
tremendous for making the face white and beautifull, if an oyle be made
thereof: But this soft, clear and running Mercury, which being well prepared
rectifyes the blemishes of Saturne, and advanceth him into the throne of
Jupiter: but they ought not to be meant vulgar Saturne and Jupiter, be-
cause vulgar metalls enter not into the physicall worke, but as they are purged
and made physicall by long preparation: Saturne is the father of all the
gentiles, or rather golden things, and the first gate of secrecy: With this, saith
Rhasy in his Epistle, are the gates of Science opened: This Sonne Jupiter
succeeds him, who expells his father out of his kingdome, and dismembers him,
that hee might not beget more Sonnes; but Venus the most beautifull of wo-
men sprung from his testicles being cast into the Sea: From Jupiter, who is
hott lead prepared, are produced the other planets, as Mars from Juno, mer-
cury from Maia, the daughter of Atlas a mountaine of Mauritania,
Luna and Sol from Latona: which four are brought into the world by co-
ction alone or womens worke: but by coction, maturation and the dispersion
of the more crude parts is signified, which is performed by Vulcan in philo-
sophicall vessells: for it is not to be supposed, that it is vulgar coction, as to
the way of operating, but it agrees with it as to the end: For as a woman ma-
tures fish in water, that is, softens, boyles and seethes them, all the superfluous mois-
ture being resolved from them into water and aire; Soe the philosopher handles
his subject, macerating, melting, dissolving, coagulating and fixing it in a pecu-
liar water, which is stronger than the sharpest vinegar, in the vessells of Her-
mes, the joynts of which are most exquisitely closed, as is requisite, that the wa-
ter may not exhale, and that, which is in the vesselle, be burned; This is

in which the old man sweats: some there are, who boyle fish, Lobsters, crabs,
or green peas in a double, soe that the sayd things are in the upper pott, and
water onely in the lower, and the potts are placed one above the other with
orbes, that the vapour may not goe forth: by which means the vapour of
the water ascending onely penetrates and matures the things contained,
and makes them much more tender and soft, than if they had boyled in
water: This is the most laudable way of the philosophers, whereby they
mollify that which is hard, dissolve that which is compact, and rarefy that
which is thicke: For the aire or insensible vapour is that which matures,
cooks, and perfectly finisheth in seede, not crude and cold water, as such: The
aire is also that which tingeth and coloureth the golden apples in the Or-
chards of the Hesperides: For, if it was well considered, the ebullition of
water, whereby raw flesh is sod till it be fitt to eat, is nothing els, but a ra-
refaction and transmutation of the water into an airy vapour, the bubbles
being aire containd within the water, which doe easily vanish away, the
aire betaking itselfe from the water to its owne sphere, and the water
settling to its owne center.

<vocab>philosophers pott, Laconian bath, double, orbes, Hesperides, ebullition, rarefaction, transmutation, sphere</vocab>

## Embleme 23. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->When Pallas was borne, and Sol lay with Venus, it rained gold<-
->at Rhodes.<-

### Epigramme 23.

> Thy prodigy seems more than strange to us,
> Yet Grave for truth hath it affirmed thus:
> The clouds did twice at Rhodes their joy declare,
> Showring downe gold through the yielding aire;
> When Sol to Venus pays the dues of love,
> And Pallas sprung out of the braine of Jove.

<vocab>Embleme, Secrets of Nature, Pallas, Sol, Venus, gold, Rhodes, Epigramme, Jove, yielding air</vocab>

--- Page 89 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>Discourse 23.</header>

It would be madnesse to affirme that gold ever rained upon the earth, except
it was allegorically understood: for there are noe golden rivers, or veynes of
gold being in the clouds, that it could be sayd to be there generated, nor is gold of
soe little weight, as to be thought to be attracted with vapours: but a Trope ad-
mitts and resolveth all these things; for soe surely as pallas actually sprung
from Jupiters braine, and Sol committed adultery with Venus; soe surely also
fell golden raine: not as if wee doubt both these things to have happened,
but to take away the vulgar and literall sense from things allegorically
spoken: for if wee follow the bare words of this Embleme, there is nothing
more absurd than them, if the meaning, nothing more true. Now Rhodes
is an Island, first called Ophiusa by reason of the multitude of Serpents,
after that Rhodes from the garden of roses there flourishing, lastly also
Colossia, from the Colosso of the Sunne, which being there was esteemed
among the seven miracles of the world: Whereupon the antient philoso-
phers, their crude Mercuriall matter showing it selfe like a Serpent, but
after preparation and coction assuming the purple colour of a rose:
tooke certaine similitudes from the Island Rhodes: & the same also was
the cause, why they ascribe a golden shower to it, Sol or Apollo having
coition with Venus: This thing first tropically spoken gave occasion to
the Rhodians (growing more proud, because soe great Gods had begotten
children among them) to erect as it were an idole of the Sunne of
stupendous magnitude and charge: for that Colosso, as hystories re-
late, was 70 cubits high, soe placed, that shipps might passe be-
tween the leggs of it with full sayles: the fingers were as bigge as
ordinary statues, and few men could graspe the thumb: the Artist
was Chares Lyndius the scholar of Lysippus, by whome it was
made in twelve years: after the fifty sixth year being overturned
by an earthquake, it was also a miracle lying: & the Soldan of
Ægypt, winning and possessing Rhodes, is reported to have laden
900 Camels with the brasse of this statue: That which Sol is
among the planets, sayth a philosopher, that is gold amongst metales;
and this is chiefly appropriated to the sunne in respect of heat, colour,
virtue and essence: for this reason is a golden rayne ascribed to the-
generation of Sol: when little Sunns are conceived by Venus: For Ve-
nus hath a rosy colour in her face, and if infused into the seed of
Sol, the offspring from hence produced, must really be a Rhodian:
Hee is beautifull, like roses, the Sonne of the philosophers, who
drawes and allures the eyes and minds of all men to him, and de-
serves love, at whose birth it is noe strange thing if miracles hap-
pen: for hee in all his actions will be miraculous, and excite a
golden rayne: This is the Brother of Augias the Sonne of Sol,

who had Oxen for a patrimony, the dung whereof Hercules purged away
in one day: soe also is the brother of Aeta, who possessed the golden
fleece obtained by Jason. Of pallas proceeding from the braine of Jupiter,
without a mother, they report, that shee was brought forth at the river
Triton, and therefore called Tritonia: shee is feigned to be the goddesse
of wisdome, who springing from the braine, the seat of wisdome, is not
without reason soe called: a golden raine did also adorne her nativity at
Rhodes, that after birth shee might remaine in the memory of men. In
publicke or solemne joy, as at the coronation of a King, or also the birth
of the kings heire peeces of gold are sometimes throwne amongst the -
common people, as golden raine: soe did the same happen at the birth of
pallas. For pallas is wisdome, who carryes health in her right hand, and
riches in the left, and provides for mens health and welfare: To her per-
sons brought the saxifying head of Medusa, terrible with serpentine and vi-
perous haire, which shee uses in a sheild against her enemyes, a rude and bar-
barous people, transmuting them into stones. And indeed naturall wisdome
or philosophy makes those incredulous and envious despisers stupid and
voyd of sense and understanding by that very thing, (from which Chrysaor
was produced, who was the father of the tricorporeall Geryon aforesayd)
that is, the petrifying Gorgonian blood, which is nothing else, but the
tincture of the philosophicall stone.

## Embleme 24. Of the Secrets of Nature.
## A wolfe devoured the King, and being burned restored him to life againe.

---

### Epigramme 24.

Take the rapacious wolfe, and let him feast
His hungry stomacke on the King deceast:
Let Vulcan then blow up a fire, that hee
By th’ flames to ashes may reduced be;
Repeat this method, which at length will bring
Life and a Lyons courage to the King.

<vocab>Alchemy, Embleme, Secrets of Nature, wolfe, King, Vulcan, Lyons courage, Michael Maier</vocab>

--- Page 92 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>Discourse 24.</header>

<margin><unclear>with the P. of G. are extreame hungry</unclear></margin>
It is a thing commonly knowne, how great the hunger and voracity of a wolfe is: for when hee wants food, in extreame hunger hee feeds upon the earth: which also hee is sayd to disgorge, if hee come amongst great herds of beasts; that being made more heavy with that part as it were; hee cannot be soe easily shaked off, but resists more strongly and furiously. Having entred into the folds, hee not onely kills what is sufficient for his belly, but also through greedinesse destroyes the whole flocke on every side. Hee is consecrated to Apollo and Latona, because hee stood by her when shee was in labour: for neither could Latona have been delivered without the presence of the wolfe: and therefore the wolfe is not without reason reported to be acceptable to Apollo, whose birth: day hee celebrates; as also because his eyes doe shine and cast forth light by night. The breathless body of the King is cast to him being extreme hungry, not that hee should wholy consume and annihilate it, but to restore life and strength to it by his owne death: For there is I know not what amatory virtue in the wolfes tayle, which is infused into the King being halfe dead, which makes him very desirable to all men, hee restoring pristine health and beauty. The Hyrcanians brought up doggs for noe other use, than to devoure the bodyes of dead men cast to them, as Cicero relates: Soe also the Massagete doe give men that dye of diseases as a prey to doggs: But the philosophers expose their King to a wolfe: nor indeede did the customes of the Sabeans please them, in carrying out the corps of the deceased after the same manner as dung, who also threw their Kings amongst dung hills, nor of the Troglodytes, who tye the necke of a dead man to their foot, and hurrye him along with jests and laughter, and without any consideration of the place burye him: But they had rather herein follow the customs of the Magi, who did not bury their bodyes, before they were torne to peices by wild beasts: Or of the Indians, who causes themselves to be buryed alive, with Crownes on singing the praises of the Gods, lest old age should prevent: but this custome was fatally imposed upon them all without any hope of resurrection, or renovation of life: the philosopher determine farre otherwise: for they have most certaine knowledge, that their dead King devoured by the wolfe will appeare with life, strength, and youth, and the wolfe must be burned in his stead: For the wolfe may easily be put to death if hee hath soe gorged his belly, but the King, though dead, hath notwithstanding a Martiall or Swan:like virtue soe as not to be wounded nor consumed: But where must this wolfe be sought for, and from whence must the King be had? The philosophers answer, that the wolfe wanders every where in mountains and vallyes, to take his prey, who must be drawne out of his harbour, and preservd for use: The King also coming from the East wearyed with a long and tedious journey at length falls downe, whose death also griefe accelerates, seeing himselfe in noe honour, and soe little esteemd amongst strangers and forreigners, as to be sold into servi-

tude for a little mony: But the wolfe must be had out of a colder region, for
those that are bred in a cold climate are more feirce, than in Africa or Egypt,
by reason of greater hunger proceeding from externall cold: who devoures the King &

thren, that y, the youngest of all, yet will hee after many tribulations and
miseries at length attaine to a most powerfull kingdome: whereupon sayth

y moved from master to master, in whose beginning will be misery and
sharpnesse, but in the end joy and gladnesse: and Alanus the same:
<margin>N.B.</margin>
One thing y to be chosen and preferred before all, which y of a livid colour,
having a clear and liquid metallic species, and y a thing hott and moist
watry and adustive, and y a living oyle and a living tincture, a mine-
rall stone and aqua vitae of wonderfull efficacy. It y not allwayes safe for
Kings to travaile out of the confines of their kingdome, for if they abscond
and be discovered, they are by their adversaryes taken for Spyes and im-
prisoned; if also being detected they proceed without an army, they are
after the same manner in danger: soo also hath it happened, or would have
happened to this Indian King, death not being prevented. This y the first
sublimation, lotion, and nobilitation, which the philosophers use,
that the second and third may be performed with the greater successe.
for those without this are not of any efficacy, the King being as yet
pusillanimous, somnolent, and sicke. For hee must of necessity first
require subsidies and tribute from his subjects, whereby hee may pur-
chase himselfe garments and other necessaryes, and afterwards hee
will be rich enough, soo as to be able to bestow new garments upon all
his subjects, soo often as hee please: for great things for the most part
proceeding from small beginnings may then advance small things, or
also suppresse great things, if they will: as for example, small cityes
at the beginning, which have afterwards governed great things, and
of villages have made spatious and populous towns.

# Embleme 25. Of the secrets of Nature.

->The Dragon dyes not, except hee be killd by his brother and sister,<-
->which are Sol and Luna.<-

## Epigramme 25.

> To kill the dragon will much art require,
> Soo sure as never after to respire:
> Brother and sister joyntly must invade
> his life, cause nothing else can him degrade;
> Luna his sister y, who Orion slew,
> his brother phebus python overthrew.

<vocab>Embleme, Dragon, Sol, Luna, Epigramme, Orion, phebus, python</vocab>

--- Page 95 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>Discourse 25.</header>
<meta>17th-century manuscript in a neat secretary/italic hybrid hand. Significant bleed-through is visible from the reverse of the leaf. The scribe uses a character 'y' with a dot as shorthand for 'is', which has been transcribed as 'y'. Hyphens at line breaks are represented by '='.</meta>

In acquiring the golden fleece the Dragon was first of all to be killed, bicause the labour being attempted by many men, they were overcome by the Dragon and destroyed with deadly poyson: the reason was, bicause they were not sufficiently armed against the Dragons poyson, nor instructed, by what engines hee might be slayne: but Jason (the physitian) neglected noe manner of reme=dies, whereof hee received severale from Medea (the advise of the mind) and amongst those the images of Sol and Luna, which using hee successfully ob=tained the victory with the reward, that y, the Golden Fleece. The Dragon there=fore was slayne by Sol and Luna, or the images of them, which the philosophers doe in many places inculcate: for an Author of the Rosary amongst others hath it thus: 

> Hermes; The Dragon dyes not, except hee be killed by his brother and sister, not by one alone but by both together, to witt, by Sol and Luna. 

The philoso=phers Mercury never dyes, except hee be killd by his sister, that y, hee must be congealed by Luna or Sol. Note, that the Dragon y Argent vive extracted from bo=dyes, having in it body, soule, and spirit, whereupon hee sayth: 

> The Dragon dyes not, except by his brother and sister, that y, Sol and Luna, that y, Sulphur ex=tracted, containing in it the nature of moistnes and coldnes according to Luna, 

by those the dragon dyes, that y, Argent vive extracted from the same bodyes at first, which y the philosophers Aqua permanens, which y made after putre=faction, and after separation of the Elements, and that water y by another name called, Aqua foetida: those are his words: with whome all the rest doe agree, soo that there y noe need to alledge the authority of every one: the Epidaury worshipped the Dragon in the Temple of Apollo in commemoration of python being slayne: Warre and hatred y genuine to the dragon with the Ele=phant, whose eyes and throat hee strikes at, till the Elephant falling on the ground kills the dragon with himselfe by the weight of his body, by which means Dragons blood y imported into our countryes: The Dragon hath eyes of the va=luation of gemms: his sight y very acute and clear, and therefore hee y much used for the keeping of treasures, as the apples of the Hesperides, and the gol=den fleece at Colchos aforesaid: The Antients ascribed a dragon to Esculapius as a Hieroglyphicke: But the Chymists doe appropriate Dragons to their opera=tions, not indeed, really, but allegorically: for a dragon, whether hee be fixed or volatile, allwayes denotes Mercury: for this reason Mercury y to be seen with two serpents twisted about his wand (the Dragon being a great serpent) and Sa=turne with one, which devoureth his tayle, as also Janus: A serpent y conse=erated to Esculapius the sonne of Apollo, the inventor of medicine (philo=sophical medicine) in which forme hee y reported to be brought from Epi=daury to Rome, and there allwayes worshipped for the cessation of the pestilence caused by him, as was supposed: But the philosophers dragon y allwayes most vigilant and lively, nor easily to be wounded, both by rea=son of the thicknesse of his skinne, and the sharpnesse of his teeth and poyson, wherewith hee y armed: For though common dragons are sayd to be voyd of poyson, yet this y not without it, venting it upon any one

in his way, if hee be not warily managed: for hee can therefore be rarely over-

the poet says truely, It is a safe and usuall way to deceive by the name

fell in love with a marriageable and beautifull maide, and lay with her in bed,
and that **Tiberius** the Emperour delighted in another, which hee commonly
fed with his owne hand: Soo also the philosophicall dragon, if hee be
rightly handled, leaves his fiercenesse, and becomes a friend to man, though
hee be malicious, if otherwise. **Xanthus** the historian is a witnesse with
**pliny**, that the young of a dragon being killed was restored by the parent
to life with the herbe, which hee calls **Balin**: which notwithstanding, I as-
cribe to a <term>philosophicall allegory</term>, rather than true history; for life is re-
stored to a dead dragon, and death to a living by courses in <term>Chymicall processes</term> only.
But some man may enquire, where and how the dragon must be taken?
The philosophers answer by a verse most concisely,

> ->Dant vobis montes, dracones, terrae, fontes:<-

But you may see Easily of the way of taking him, with what care and
industry many men watched to take a mighty dragon observed in **Africa**,
and bring him to the Emperour **Tiberius**, namely the dragons accusto-
med way amongst stones being enclosed and by degrees straightened, or
where hee was stopt by netts and cords, and at length made tame by clubs
and stripes, and as a burthen of many carts brought to a ship, in which hee
was carryed to **Rome**.

### Embleme 26. Of the Secrets of Nature.
->The tree of life is the fruit of humane wisedome.<-

### Epigramme 26.
> To greater wisedome man cannot attaine,
> Than health with wealth to acquire, if not in vaine:
> Shee in her right hand length of dayes extends,
> Her left vast heaps of treasure comprehends;
> To him that shall espouse her as a wife
> Shee (as the tree) will yeild the fruit of life.

Tully hath excellently described the essentialle difference of man, whereby hee differs from all other animalls, after this manner: As a bird is intended by nature for flying, a horse for <gloss>running</gloss> ~~practising~~, soe man for reasoning: For as Lyons, bears, and Tygers doe exercise and delight themselves in fiercenesse, Elephants and bulls in strength of body, Eagles, Falcons, and other hawkes in the prey of birds, and swiftnesse of wings; soe man excells these and all others in reason, discourse of mind and understanding: Soe that there is noe fiercenesse, noe corporall strength, noe celerity, or such like thing in beasts soe great, as not to be mitigated, subdued, and prevented by reason alone: For reason is not a thing humane, or proceeding from the earth, but as the poet sayth, a particle of divine breath, sent from heaven into the body of man: but it is sometimes called memory, sometimes the intellectuall vertue, to which if use or experience be added, wisdome springs from thence, which is most pretious to man: For Use is reported to be as the father, and reason or memory as the mother of soe generous an offspring: From whence that common saying: Use begat mee, and my mother-memory brought mee forth: but the question is, what is true wisdome, and most worthy of many inquisition, there being infinite opinions concerning it, every man interpreting it according to his owne imagination? It may be answered, that wisdome in humane things (allwayes to be excepted in divine things relating to the welfare of the soule) consists not in sophisticale arguments, not in rhetoricale and garnished discourses, not in the poeticale sounde of verse, not in the criticale subtleties of Gramarians, not in wilfullnesse and obstinacy, in deceipt and perjury, in æquivocations and lyes, in oppression and sweat of the poor, the craft of heaping up wealth and riches, nor in any thing els, but the true knowledge of Chymistry, together with practise, most remedious to mankind: This is wisdome surpassing all things, which on the right hand goes to the East, on the left to the west, and comprehends the whole earth: Salomon in his booke of Wisdome reasoneth distinctly of wisdome, saying Chapt. 8. v. 9. 14. They that are acquainted with wisdome have eternall perseverance, and they that are her friends sincere pleasure, and hee that diligently enquireth after her, shall receive much joy, for there is noe unpleasantness in her conversation, but mirth and joy, and though wine and music doe alsoe chear the heart of man, yet wisdome is more pleasant than them: for shee is a tree of life to them that lay hold on her, and blessed are they that retaine her: Sustanting fully her for the aliment of the soules: The wise shall inherit glory, and hee that esteems wisdome shall be exalted and honoured by her: for wisdome advanceth her children, and hee that firmly adheres to her shall be promoted to honour: An immortale name alsoe proceeds from her age after age: Shee is more powerfull than all things, and comforts a wise man more, than ten mighty princes that are in the citty: To this wisdome of the world may alsoe be applyed that which is spoken by the prophet Baruch: Learne true wisdome, and you will find who hee is that gives long life, wealth, joy, and peace: And in the 9th Chapter v. 17. of Wisdome it is affirmed, that Wisdome

as are health, the good things of fortune and mind: for without y^se a
man even living is dead, and not much unlike to a brute; though out=
wardly hee represent him y^t hee ought to be, and is not in his better

## Embleme 27. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Hee that endeavours to enter into the Philosophers Rosary without<-
->a key, is like him that would walke without feet.<-

## Epigramme 27.

->Roses in wisdoms garden doe abound,<-
->But alwayes under locks and bolts is found:<-
->One onely key it hath, of meane esteeme,<-

Concerning Erichthonius they write, that hee sprung from the seed of Vulcan cast upon the earth striving with Pallas the Goddesse of wisdome; with serpentine and without humane feet: Very like him are those, who, depending upon the meanes of Vulcan alone, without the wisdome of Pallas, doe begett monstrous children, namely, without feet and abortive, which can neither sustaine themselves, nor profit others: It is a miserable thing for a man to goe on all foure, that is, hands and feet together, but much more miserable to be altogether destitute of feet, and to use armes instead thereof: for they seeme to have degenerated to the nature of wormes, who goe after the manner of creeping wormes: But the two leggs are the organs to walk in man, without which hee cannot truly walke, as neither see without eyes, or touch things tangible without hands: Soe likewise Medicine and every operative art is supposed to have two leggs, whereon to stand, namely, experience and reason, without either of which that art is lame, and imperfect in its tradition and practery, nor correspondent to the proposed end: Chymistry indeed chiefly delights in two subjects (as leggs) whereof one is y<sup>e</sup> key, the other y<sup>e</sup> string of the barre; by these y<sup>e</sup> philosophicall Rosary looks on all sides is opened, and passage admitted into it to those that have right to enter: But if one be wanting to him that is about to enter herein it will be the same thing, as for a man whose feet are bound, to endeavour to outrunne a hare: For that entry into the garden, which is every way enclosed or fenced, without a key, imitates a theife, who coming in a darke night can discerne nothing that growes in the garden, nor enjoy y<sup>e</sup> things stolen away. But the Key is a thing of very little esteeme or value, which is properly called a stone knowne in the chapter, and is the root of Rhodes, without which a twig is not put forth, nor doth the bud swell, nor the Rose become green, or discover multitude of leaves: But a question might be asked, where must this Key be sought for? I answer with the oracle, that it must be there investigated, where the bones of Orestes are sayd to be found, namely, where winds, the striker, that which is beaten backe, and the destruction of men are found together, that is, as Lythas hath interpreted it, in y<sup>e</sup> Brasiers shop or workhouse: For the oracle would have bellowes understood by winds; a hammer by y<sup>e</sup> striker, an anvile by that which is beaten backe; by y<sup>e</sup> destruction of men Iron: if a man knowes how to number well and distinguish y<sup>e</sup> signs, hee will certainly find y<sup>e</sup> Key in the northerne hemisphere of the Zodiacke, and y<sup>e</sup> string of y<sup>e</sup> barre in the southerne: Which having obtained, it will be easy to open y<sup>e</sup> dore and enter and in y<sup>e</sup> very entrance hee will see Venus with her paramour Adonij: for shee hath tinged y<sup>e</sup> white roses of a purple colour with her blood: In the same place also a Dragon is observed, as in the Hesperian gardens, which watcheth to preserve the roses: The scent of y<sup>e</sup> roses is sayd to be increased by garlicke planted near, and that by reason of an exceeding degree of heat, which appears in garlicke, in which respect it is a remedy against cold poysons: for Roses

onely once, twice, or thrice, whereas being frustrated in their understanding and
whole study, they also (which is worst) loose their mony, pains, and time, if
they have spent any in this art. And a little after, For when a man thinks hee
hath done, hath the worke, hee will find nothing in his hands.

### Embleme 28. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->The King sitting in a vaporous bath is bathed, and freed from<-
->melancholy by Pharut.<-

### Epigramme 28.

> Prince Duenech, armed with the Lyons strength,
> Grew melancholick and morose at length:
> Pharut is sent for to continue a cure,
> Who by a vaporous bath doth health assure;
> Which means by frequent use wrought good effect,
> And his distempers wholy did correct.

<vocab>Embleme, Secrets of Nature, Prince Duenech, Pharut, vaporous bath, melancholy, Epigramme</vocab>
