<lang>English</lang>
<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>Discourse 28.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

As there are three concoctions in man, the first in the stomacke, the second in the liver, the third in the veins, soe also soe many are the universall evacuations of the excrements, which are correspondent to them, and daily carry of their superfluities, namely, the first by stools, which is proper to the first concoction, the second by urine, which appertaines to the second, the third by expiration through the pores of the whole body, or by sweat, which is peculiar to the third: In the first the chylo, in the next the chyme, in the last a dew or dewy substance is elaborated, and applyed to every part of the body: The excrements or faeces of the first are thicke, bilious, and fatt, which are carryed through the bowells to the backe dore, and if they be at any time obstructed, they are moderately or strongly expelled by gentle purgatives; the excrements of the second are liquid, more thinne, bilious, and saltish, which are brought out of the veins by the kidnyes and bladder, as conduits, and the sediment of these is the signe or touchstone of urine: The superfluities of the third are yet more thinne, and therefore doe for the most part expire of themselves through the smallest pores, or are carryed out together with the scum of the humors, as sweat: These are helped by sudorificks, as antecedent by diureticks: To evacuate these last faeces the ancient Greeks and Romans were very solicitous: For to this end were soe many sports and exercises used by them, as chafing of all the parts in the morning, anointing with oyle, and wrastling, fencing, the exercise of running, of hand-ball, tennis, daily washing and bathing in rivers or artificiall baths, for the conveniency of which were soe many magnificent structures built at Rome, that wee can rather admire than imitate them: As for example the baths of Diocletian, which for the most part are yet remaining, and (if I be not mistaken) portrayed with Archangels, soe high, sumptuos, and splendid were the works: The sorts of concoctions in the elaboration of metalls are almost the same, as those aforesaid: for the first is in its way effected in <term>annus magnus</term>, that is, the revolution of the highest sphere, the second in the revolution of the lowest, the third in the revolution of the middle sphere: But the philosophers to draw forth this masse of excrementitious superfluities more commodiously by the ministery of art, doe invent also severall ways, as ablutions, purgations, balneations and laconicks, by which they have performed that in the philosophicall worke, which physitians have in mans body: Duenech therefore is introduced into the bath by Pharut, there to sweat, and evacuate the faeces of the third concoction through the pores: Now this Kings distemper is melancholick or atrabilary, whereby hee is of lesse authority and esteeme than all other princes, being dazed with the morosity of Saturne, and the choleric passion of Mars: hee hath therefore a desire either to dye or be cured, if it be possible: Amongst many physitians one being found, who undertooke this charge

upon himselfe, hee was brought with entreatyes and rewards: And this Allegory

tion: lest this should happen, the artist must be carefull in the first place
to choose the true offspring of the king, which though hee be not in splen-
did and golden attire, but in despicable and meane cloathing, as also of a
livid and melancholicke complexion, let him not therefore reject him, or
take another in his roome; for if hee be very well washed, his royall ger-
miny will soone appear, as in Cyrus, Paris, Romulus, who were educated
amongst rusticks: But by way of observation the bath must be a Laconicu<gloss>m</gloss>,
that is, vaporous and sudorificke, that the water may not burne the tender flesh, or
obstruct the pores, whereby more damage than utility would ensue, and his malady
not be cured: As for the Kings garments to be put on after bathing let noe man
be solicitous; for as the daughter of King Alcinous did in times past contribute
cloaths to Ulysses being shipwracked and naked, soe will there be one that will
lend him most pretious robes, that hee may deservedly be acknowledged the
Sonne of Sol.

## Embleme the 29. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->As the Salamander lives in fire soe allso the Stone.<-

### Epigramme 29.

> The Salamander sports in scorching heats,
> And, Vulcan! bids defyance to your threats;
> The Stone soe allso being thereto inured,
> Withstands the strongest flames ever to endure;
> That vermine cold and moist congeals the fire,
> Thy Stone with heat dos as it like conspire.

<vocab>Salamander, Vulcan, alchemy, nature, fire, stone, epigram, Atalanta Fugiens</vocab>

--- Page 107 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>Discourse 29.</header>

Two Elements there are, in which animals live, water and aire, and as many in which noe animate thing can live, earth and fire. For as these are of a midle and temperate complexion in the first and second qualityes, soe those are extreme; bodys either too thicke or too subtile, soe thicke as not to admit other bodys, soe subtile as indeed to admit, but to penetrate and burne them. But the reason why men doe live in vaults and subterranean mines is because of the aire descending thitherto, and supplying the vacuity of those places: But here wee speake of every Element apart: In water fish doe live in incredible number, variety, and fertility, yea the greatest of all animalls; In the aire men, four-footed beasts, birds, wormes, and insects: Whatsoever is sayd of Spirits wandring in the lowest parts of the earth, is another thing, for neither are they animalls: But they say the Salamander alone lives in fire: Now the Salamander is a creeping vermine, not much unlike to a Lizard, but of slower pace, bigger head, and different colour, such as I remember I have seen in the Alps, or neere the Spluga, coming out of the caverns of rocks after thunder and raine, and staying in the way, a countryman of that place advertising that it was called ein Molch: And it has about it a clammy and viscous moistness, by the virtue of which it passeth safely through fire without harme: But the philosophers salamander is much different, though it be assimilated to this: For that is bred in fire, this not; but if it falls into fire it is not presently burned, by reason of its exceeding coldness and moistness, but can freely pass through the flame: That is hott and dry, this cold and moist: For every thing participates of the nature of the mothers wombe, or resembles the place and country. Fire produceth nothing but that which is hott and dry, as itselfe; and on the contrary the moist and cold caverns of rocks, being full of water, doe bring forth this cold and moist vermyne. That doth by the similitude of nature rejoyce in fire, this by contrariety extinguisheth it, or repells it force for some time from it. They say that the flye, called pyrausta is generated in fire, and flys out of the brasse furnaces of Cyprus: but noe man hath beheld this true, except by an allegory: For fire, if it be continued, destroyes and corrupts the bodys of all animals whatsoever, it also burning the earth into glasse, and the most solid timber into ashes, and all other compounded things, a few excepted, as mercurialls, which doe either wholly remayne, or altogether flye from fire, noe separation of the parts being made in them. Vulcan is a most cruell murtherer, who calls all things mixed with the Elements to his examination, some being excepted from his judgement seat as the privileges and prerogatives of the Empresse Nature: Upon these hee obtaines noe right alone, unless hee joyns to himselfe other

judges, and Salamanders are above by violence, which they doe not fear:
Avicenna in porta reckons up the various temperaments of bodys, which are
all unaquall, and therefore corruptible by fire and other injurys: but one
hee affirms to be exactly equall, which hath soe much heat, as cold, soe
much siccity, as moisture, not as to weight, but justice, as the physicians
terme it, and this is that which is more patient, than agent; in which if
fire endeavours to dissolve water it contrary into aire its familiar, the
earth admits not this resolution, because it is incorporated with water:
and the internall fire of the compound doth by consent approve this
sentiment of the earth, because it is intimate friend to the earth.
The judgement therefore of Vulcan ceaseth, who useth yet another an=
tique: striving to burne the earth into ashes, as his custome is, but the wa=
ter adhering to it prevales against expephory, which shows itselfe uni=
ted to the earth, and the aire to it, and fire to the earth on the other
side; hee therefore that would incinerate the earth, must also incine=
rate the other Elements: And soe Vulcan being disapointed suspends
his judgement, lest hee should be derided by all men. This body is like
the reall Salamander, in which the Elements are exactly equalized in vir=
tue: Of it sayth the Rosary out of Geber, That philosopher would have
the substance of Mercury mortifyed, but naturally by Mercury is in that
venerable Stone, as is manifest to all men: therefore &c: Also that phi=
losopher would have the substance of Mercury fixed, as is evident, be=
cause hee set forth the wayes of fixing with many cautions and devices,
but who doubts the substance of that pretious stone to be fixed; cer=
tainly noe man that knows it. By which it is demonstrated, that the
Stone must be reduced to the nature of the Salamander by fixa=
tion; that is, absolute fixity, which declines not, or refuses fire.
For neither can it be the Salamander, before it hath learned to en=
dure fire most patiently, which must of necessity be effected in long
process of time. Hereafter in the 35 emblematicall discourse wee will
treat of Achilles and Triptolemus, placed in the night time under embers
of fire, till they could endure most vehement heat, who also attained to a
Salamandrine property by custome and habit: for custome is a second
nature, which notwithstanding must communicate power, and as a mis=
tress begin alteration, otherwise custome will be able to effect little or
nothing; that is the reason why it is impossible to fix ice at fire, but
cristale possible, because nature hath begun this: the same must be
supposed of watery and volatile Mercury, which in its owne nature
cannot be fixed, except by marriage and coition with Sulphur,
which is the philosopher's tincture, and fixeth all volatile Spi=
rits. . .

## Embleme 30. Of the secrets of Nature.

->Luna is as requisite to Sol as a henne to a cocke.<-

### Epigramme 30.

What can you doe deare Sol alone, if I
Joyne not as henne with cocke to multiply?
Or can I Luna Natures gifts expresse
Without your ayde? noe, grant mee some redresse.
Hee's but a foole those to confine apart
Whome Nature would have joynd in mind and heart.

<vocab>Sol, Luna, nature, alchemy, emblem, epigram</vocab>

<header>Discourse 30.</header>

Avicenne in his booke of the Soule divers times admonisheth, that noe
eggs are to be taken for art, but onely of those henns, which had a cocke:
that is, the feminine subject avayles nothing without the masculine
virtue, and on the contrary, nor a cocke profitable without a henne:
for both these sexes must be joynd together in the philosophicall coupe,
that multiplication may be made: The philosophers doe indeed use
the similitude taken from a cocke, because in the first place hee is
more correspondent to the power of Sulphur, than any other male of
bird, one being able to serve many henns, and not easily suffering a
rivall in the same dunghill, thinking himselfe convenient and suffi=
cient for everyone. Hee is the bird of Mars, transformd, as the poets
fable, from the youth Gallus, who was entrusted to observe Sol, that
hee might not discover the adultery of Mars with Venus, and very
Martiall in fight, contending with his adversary even to death: In
the philosophicall worke hee represents Sol, as the henne doth Luna:
there is therefore the same necessity of joyning Sol with Luna, as a
cocke with a henne: The cocke is also consecrate to Sol, at whose
approach hee rejoiseth, and goes to sleep with him: hee often looks up to
heaven, and erects his <unclear>houkes</unclear>, tayle upright: hee fights against serpents in
defense of his henns: hee is an acceptable messenger of light and Latona,
being present at her labour: Latona indeed brought forth Sol and Luna,
and therefore hee is appropriate to the mother and sonne: But Sol,
Luna, and Latona doe in Chymicall subjects agree, soe also the cocke
and henne: but these also proceede from an egge, and againe doe
lay eggs for the production of young chickens; soe also doe the philoso=
phers eggs turne into birds of the same kind, provided they be kept
warme with temperate heat, as is the heat of a brooding henne, which
endures continually: For whereas amongst other birds the male also
sitts upon the eggs, the cocke alone shows himselfe free from this
office or burthen, and therefore all the care and pains of hatching the
eggs, and producing chickens lyes upon the henne; where her sedulity
and diligence may be observed, how hastily shee eats and drinks, and
unloads her belly, and presently runs backe to her eggs, before they be
cold: Then with what force and violence shee defends her chickens,
with what tendernesse shee receives and covers them being naked under
her wings, with what a voyce, as with a bell, shee calls and keeps them
together, with what study shee breaks the harder grains or seeds for
them, and presents them with her beake, as on a knife, this worke of
nature is worthy of admiration: And all those things are, that man
may neither want eggs nor chickens for aliment: After the same man=
ner the philosopher or artist doth most diligently and providently

<header>Discourse 30.</header>

proceed in all his operations; For her picks up eggs there where a cock
hath been, trying them whether they be adds, after that her cleanseth
prepareth, and disposeth them in his vessel, as nests, and administreth due
heat, whereby the subjects might together daily act and suffer one by
another, till after a long time passing through various colours they come
to one colour and essence: In which operation Solution, coagulation,
Sublimation, ascension, descension, distillation, calcination and fixion
as intermediates are perfected: for a hard and compact thing cannot
be altered, therefore solution is premises, that it may be made liquid
and soft: and where a thing is dissolved, it is convenient also to be
coagulated, not to its former hardness but a honyish tractability;
Sublimation separateth the pure from the impure, and makes a thing
vile more worthy, the inferiour superiour; and wherefore why nei=
ther ought to be wanting, for it is as the mistress and directress
of all: when sublimation is made, some party ascends higher, which
is ascension, and other again descends, and soe descension is made;
Distillation also clarifyeth the whole being often interposed, and that
which remayneth in the bottome is calcined: then both are fixed, and
soe the worke is compleated. But if a man gather all these parti=
cular operations under a general, which is <term>coction</term>, her will not
erre much from the purpose: for as soe many chickens as there are,
which runne here and there dispersedly, doe resort to one mother or
nurse the henne, soe doe soe many severall wayes of operating con=
curre to the womanly worke, that is, coction alone: It is <term>Luna</term> which
comes to be exalted to the sublimity of <term>Sol</term>, for whose sake all these
things are done, that is, durable matrimony is intended between <term>Sol</term>
and <term>Luna</term>, which if effected, all <term>ligations</term>, <term>desponsations</term>, <term>copulation</term>,
<term>dubitation</term>, etc: will cease, and the body and flesh of both will be one,
mutuall and constant love, eternall peace, and an indissoluble alli=
ance: <term>Sol</term> without <term>Luna</term> is of noe great esteeme, and <term>Luna</term> without
<term>Sol</term> of meane and sordid condition: But shee receiving Splendour, and
dignity, with strength or constancy of mind and body from <term>Sol</term> her
husband, and <term>Sol</term> from <term>Luna</term> multiplication of offspring, and pro=
pagation of kind: whereupon sayth the <term>Rosary</term>. If onely one of them
should be in our Stone, the medicine would never flow easily, nor
yield the tincture, and if it should yield, it would not tinge, unless
for soe much as also the remainder would be, and the Mercury would
evaporate in fume, because the receptacle of the tincture would not
be in it: And <term>Geber</term> in his booke of examinations proves, that if <term>Sol</term>
and <term>Luna</term> be incorporated together with art, they are not easily
separated.

## Embleme 31. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->The King swimming in the Sea cryes out with a loud voyce, hee that<-
->will deliver mee shall haue great reward.<-

### Epigramme 31.

> The King oppressed with a weighty Crowne,
> Floting ith Sea, and being like to drowne,
> Cryes out for helpe; O helpe without delay,
> And I with thanks your seruice will repay:
> Be wise, and to my kingdome mee restore;
> That I may fauour show to rich and poore.

<vocab>Embleme, Epigramme, King, Sea, Secrets of Nature, alchemy</vocab>

--- Page 113 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>Discourse 31.</header>

The first rudiment of all discipline were antiently the knowledge of 
swimming, and instruction in learning: hervpon became the proverb of an 
ignorant and unpolisht man, that hee could neither swimme nor read: 
For the Antients considers swimming as a meanes oftentimes to save and 
deliver the body from the danger of water, and the knowledge of learning 
the mind from the waves of fortune: Moreover swimming is very ser-
viceable in time of warre, the knowledge of learning in time of peace at 
home: And as wee observe that brutes have weaponry in readinesse, pro-
vided by nature, man hands and witt administred in stead thereof against 
externall violence, witt to contrive and invent weapons, hands to make and 
use them, Soe is the faculty of swimming naturally impressed in the same 
beasts, but not in man: they even young doe often escape out of waters by 
swimming, in which a man though <insert>ere soe</insert> never soe strong is suffocated; 
Necessary therefore it was to enjoyne the exercise of swimming to children, be-
ing often times most conducible to preserve a manly life, that that which was 
wanting by nature, might be supplyed by the use of art: The same exercise 
have Noblemen, princes, and Kings used for the safeguard of their body, for 
these descended from noble blood are not wholy exempt from the chance of 
fortune, but exposed to them as well as other men: If Dionysius had known nei-
ther swimming nor learning, when hee was driven out of the Kingdome of Si-
cily as a Tyrant, hee had perished by the waves of the Sea being shipwrecked in 
the Corinthian gulph, but escaping the danger by swimming, hee went to 
Corinth, where hee sett up a schoole to teach boyes, and professe humane 
learning, of a King being made a schoole master, and holding a ferur in stead 
of a scepter, from whence that jeering proverb, Dionysius of Corinth: In 
like manner if the royall Sonne of the philosopher had not been able 
to swimme, noe man would have heard his voyce, nor perceive him, being a 
long time drowned in the watery: Swimming therefore is necessary and 
remediory to all degrees of men: for though it cannot deliver a man presently 
from the surges of the vast ocean, yet it gives time of life, whereby hee 
may be saved by others: But the sage King sustaines himselfe a long 
time, and cryes out hither, though hee be heard or seen by very few by 
reason of the largenesse of the Sea, and remotenesse of it: for by chance hee 
hath touched upon a rocke or great stone in swimming, by which to be pre-
served, if the waves prevaile: But the question is what is the Sea? I an-
swer, the Erythraean or red sea, subject to the Tropicke of Cancer, in 
which, there being great store of Magnetts in the bottome, it is not safe for 
shipps compassed or laden with iron to sayle: for they might easily be 
drawne to the bottome by the virtue of the Magnetts: which the sage King 
being ignorant of, and the rest perishing the ship being sunke, hee alone 
escapes by swimming: His Crowne shining with orient Rubyes continues 
with him, by which hee might easily be knowne, and restored to his

<header>Discourse 31.</header>

Kingdome. But what are those good things, which thy royalle Sonne is able or 
willing to conferre on him, by whome hee should be restored, to his owne King-
dome? certainly not such, as Ptolomy the last King of Egypt bestowed on 
Pompey, by whome his father was restored to the Kingdome of Egypt, to witt, 
perfidiousness and death, but health, the removale of disease, the preser-
vation of life from them, the use of things necessary, and the horne of 
plenty, honour and love; which not being vulgar or meane things, but 
the cheife viatick and ornaments of this life, who, but a man of bras, 
would not desire them? who would not stretch forth his hand, and draw 
him into the boat? but hee must see that in helping him his Diademe fall 
not into the sea: for then hee could scarce be knowne for the King, nor 
received by his subjects, because the venerable pyropus would perish, be-
cause the <term>Bezoar stone</term>, assuring health to all men, would be lost. The Ro-
sary alluding hereto cites Aristotle in these words: Chose you a stone, by 
which Kings are revered in their Diadems, and by which physitians can 
cure their patients, because it is near to fire: for without a medicinall 
vertue a Crowne would be of noe value. But what must be done to 
the King now delivered? hee must be freed from the water which hee 
received in by <term>sudorificks</term>, from cold by the heat of fire; from num-
nesse and stupefaction of the party by baths moderately hott, from 
hunger and want of food by the administration of convenient dyet, and 
from other externall maladys by their contrarys even wholesome reme-
dyes: Then hee must be provided of royalle matrimony, and in due 
time a most hopefull heir will be generated by him, most acceptable to 
all men, most beautifull and most fruitfull, who will exceede all his An-
cestors in power, Kingdome, dominion, people, wealth and riches, and will 
subject his enemyes to himselfe, not by warre, but humanity, not by 
tyranny, but clemency, which is genuine and peculiar to him.

## Embleme 32. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->As Corall growes under water, and is hardened by the aire,<-
->soe allso is the Stone.<-

### Epigramme 32.

In the Sicilian waves a Sea-plant growes,
Shooting forth many moist and plyant boughs:
Corall it is by name; whose nature's such,
That, if exposd to th'aire, it alters much,
~~And~~ A blood-red rock becoming: which alone
Exactly represents the Sophick Stone.

<vocab>Corall, Sophick Stone, Sicilian waves, Sea-plant, nature</vocab>

--- Page 116 ---
<lang>en</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>Discourse 32.</header>

The philosophers call their Stone vegetable, because it vegetates, grows, is increas'd, and multiplys as a plant: which indeed to the ignorant seems strange and contrary to truth; it being manifest that stones doe neither vegetate, nor grow after y<sup>s</sup> manner, nor any thing at all relate to liquable metals: but they are deceived in their judgment: for y<sup>t</sup> which is unknown to y<sup>m</sup> they suppose is not in rerum natura, measuring the immensity of the Universe by their capacity. For who could ever have believed that a stone should grow under water, or a plant there generated become a stone, if experience together with the credible testimony of writers had not confirmed it? where is y<sup>t</sup> petrefying, where y<sup>t</sup> tinging virtue, which hardeneth and tingeth Corall, whether in the water, or in the aire, or in the earth? wee may reasonably believe it to be, as they affirme, a soft and flexible plant, whilst it is under water, yet of a very terrestriall nature, which, when it is out of and exposed to the cold wind, becomes hard, and acquires a stony tangibility, the watery part abounding being dryed up by the cold and dry aire (for those South winds blowing doe introduce humidity) and the terrestriall body remaining congealed with cold, and finally by <gloss>y<sup>e</sup></gloss> quality of the earth: for constriction appertains to the earth alone, not to the water, nor aire, as the proper and genuine virtue of either. Besides the Sea in other places yields three medicinal stones deduced partly from the vegetable kingm, partly from the animal, or rather partly from the secrets of nature; as pearls, Ambar, and Ambar-grise: the production of pearls, and the way of taking them have been known to us, but not of the rest. Ambar is found and gathered on the Sea coasts of Sudavia, on the shores, after a most vehement South or westerly wind, which without doubt boyles out of the veins of the earth into the Sea, or is washed out by the water, and driven by the waves to the shores: For wee have seen some veins of iron and silver swimming with the Ambar, which could not be done but in <gloss>y<sup>e</sup></gloss> earth. But as to flyes, gnatts, spiders, Butterflyes, froggs, Serpents, which are seen in some pieces (as wee have had 120 beads turn'd out of Ambar, which did every one containe some flyes, gnatts, spiders, butterflyes, and <gloss>one</gloss> particularly, not without a singular miracle of nature) y<sup>t</sup> happens by the influence and imagination of the heaven; as wee have elsewhere demonstrated. That Ambar grise is found after the same manner on the shores of the east and west Indyes, cannot be denyed, and though some declare it to be the juice or gumme of trees, as the Ambar aforesaid, yet they y<sup>t</sup> conceive it to proceed out of veins of the earth doe judge more probably: For fruitfull trees of Ambar have been in no place seen, which notwithstanding, if there be, doe most certainly grow out of the water in the open aire: wee

<vocab>philosophers Stone, vegetable stone, petrifaction, Corall, Ambar, Ambar-grise, Sudavia, rerum natura, alchemy</vocab>

<header>Discourse 32.</header>

y<sup>e</sup>refore ascribe both sorts of Ambar to subterranean veins or stones, as
pearls to Zoophytes (y<sup>t</sup> is, a middle nature between animals and plants)
and Corall to vegetables. y<sup>e</sup> philosophers stone is indeed like to y<sup>e</sup> stones
and especially to Corall: for as Corall grows in water, and draws nutriment
from y<sup>e</sup> earth; soe also y<sup>e</sup> philosophicall stone grows in mercuriall
water, and from it receives whatsoever is earthy, for y<sup>e</sup> augmentation of it-
selfe, y<sup>e</sup> superfluous moisture expiring. A red colour is also fixed in Corall,
by coagulation, w<sup>ch</sup> modern authors call y<sup>e</sup> tincture of Corall, nor other-
wise than as in y<sup>e</sup> physicall stone, w<sup>ch</sup> becomes red in y<sup>e</sup> last coagu-
lation of it, and appears like y<sup>e</sup> reddest Corall, w<sup>ch</sup> is y<sup>e</sup> tincture:
but as Corall becomes hard by cold and fixity, soe y<sup>t</sup> by heat and humi-
ity, w<sup>ch</sup> being augmented it likewise dissolves, contrary to y<sup>e</sup> nature of
other stones, w<sup>ch</sup> doe indeed dissolve, but turns into glasse, w<sup>ch</sup> thing
is nowise agreeable to y<sup>is</sup>: And as Corall is prepared into severall medi-
cines of great virtue, soe also hath y<sup>e</sup> philosophicall Corall, transferred
y<sup>e</sup> virtues of all herbs into itselfe, soe y<sup>t</sup> it alone is able to performe
as much, as all y<sup>e</sup> medicines of all vegetables: For y<sup>e</sup> celestiall Sunne,
w<sup>ch</sup> infuseth medicinale virtue and efficacy into vegetables, contributes
to y<sup>is</sup> it seems and terrestriall douply more, than to all others: y<sup>is</sup> is
y<sup>e</sup> philosophicall vegetable, animalle, and minerale Corall, w<sup>ch</sup> lyes
occult in y<sup>e</sup> vast ocean, and is not knowne, except it be exposed to y<sup>e</sup>
eyes and put into y<sup>e</sup> hands of y<sup>e</sup> ignorant: But it must be put out of in
y<sup>e</sup> water with very great caution, lest it loose its juice and blood, and
nothing remains but a terrestriall chaos without its true forme: For
herein consists all y<sup>e</sup> difficulty of taking Corall, besides w<sup>ch</sup> there is
scarce any: y<sup>e</sup> superfluous moisture, I mean, w<sup>ch</sup> ties y<sup>e</sup> stone,
unless it be separated, w<sup>ch</sup> suffers not y<sup>e</sup> coralline redness to
appear, w<sup>ch</sup> admits not coagulation, whilst it is therein.

## Embleme 33. of the Secrets of Nature.

->The Hermaphrodite lying like a dead man in darknesse, wants fire.<-

### Epigramme 33.

> See how the cold and dry Hermaphrodite,
> Appears as dismalle as a ghost by night:
> Fire is the thing hee wants, if that you give,
> His spirit feeling warmth will soone revive:
> the stones whole virtue in God Vulcan lyes,
> Sulphur in Sol, in Luna Mercury.

<vocab>Hermaphrodite, Vulcan, Sulphur, Sol, Luna, Mercury</vocab>

--- Page 119 ---
<lang>en</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>Discourse 33.</header>

It is one of the secrets of nature, that in the cold winter Froggs and toads lye as it were dead under water, and in the spring new-heat of the sunne operating recover sense and motion, or performe the actions of a sensitive life: But if they be found in the watery in the winter time, and brought into the aire or a warme house, they presently begin to move themselves, as in the Summer: From whence it appears, that they want nothing but externall heat, to excite the naturall internall heat, and bring it to action: After the same manner doe the philosophers speake of their Hermaphrodite, who, if hee appear dead lying in darknesse, requiring the heat of fire: Now hee is sayd to lye in darknesse, being lost in a darke and most cold wintry night, that is, remaining in blacknesse, which is a signe of coldnesse, from which hee ought to be brought to whitenesse by a greater intension of fire; and by yet a greater, to rednesse: For, as Bodillus in Turba sayth, nothing is generated without heat, and a bath of vehement heat causeth a body to perish, if it be cold it drives it away, but if it be temperate, it becomes agreeable and pleasant to the body. Bonelly in the same sayth, All things that live doe also (God so pleasing) dye: that nature therefore, which is deprived of moisture, being exposed by night, seems like a dead man, and then that nature requires fire, till the body and spirit of it be turned into earth, and then it becomes dust like to a dead man in his tombe: these things being accomplished God restores a spirit and soule to it, and all infirmity being taken away our nature is comforted and amended: It is therefore requisite to burne that matter without fear or. Fire therefore, which destroyes all other things, repairs this, that which yeilds death to others gives life to this. One onely phœnix there is, which is restored by fire, which is renewed by flames, and revives out of ashes, and this being knowne to the philosopher onely is burnt and restored to life, whatsoever other men dreame of a bird, I know not what, that flyeth, residing nowhere, nor seen, but as a fable. This Hermaphrodite, of which the philosopher speakes, is of a mixt nature, male and female, whereof one passeth into another by the operation of heat; for the female is converted into a male, which ought not to seem so strange in the philosophers worke, it being found, if wee may believe historyes, that many men have also changd their sex: The poets make mention of Coneus, Iphis, and Tiresias, as Pontany in the Starry.

> Poeniteat generis tamen et se Caenea malint,
> Aut in fœmineam penitus transire figuram: And Ausonius:
> Mutat in antiquam Caeneus revocata figuram.

Soe in the Consulship of Quintus Crassus, and Caius Cassius Longinus

at Casinum a boy was made of a virgin: And Licinius Mutianus in pli-
ny relates, that hee had seen one Aristontes at Argos, called also by the
name of Aristusa, who was marryd, and soone after discovered a beard
and virility, and marryd a wife: Pliny himselfe sayth that in Africa
hee saw Lucius Cossitium a citizen of Thritano chang'd into a woman
on the day of mariage: certaine it is that those things are true, and
might be proved by many other examples, if need were: but the geni-
tale or virile parts are thrust out of the body by the increase of heat:
For a woman being much colder than a man, and having inwardly
those things absconded, which a man hath outwardly, as physitians
say, hereupon nature being dubious, whether shee should generate a
man or a woman, hath expressed a woman outwardly, though inwardly
shee conceives a man: for which reason heat and motion increasing
through age the hidden parts breake forth, and showe themselves visibly
to all men: After the same manner with the philosophers the woman is
turned into a man heat being increased, that is, the Hermaphrodite loo-
seth the female sex, and becomes a man stout and grave, having
nothing in him of effeminacy or levity. For wee some time since
saw a noble youth an Hermaphrodite <note>The word "changed" is struck through here.</note> or rather promoted by
the industry of the chirurgicale art performe by that most famous
physitian Caspar Tagliatony of Bononia into a perfect man, fit (as
was desired) for the generating of an offspring, a new hole being made
through the yard, which was wanting there: and the lower as appertai-
ning to the woman being stops up. The philosophers are not without
all these manuale operations: for when the coldness and moisture of
Luna appears, that they call the woman, when the heat and siccity of
Sol, the man; <gloss>when</gloss> all these four qualityes together, <note>The word "enter" is struck through here.</note> their body or Her-
maphrodite; And this conversion of the woman may easily be made,
that is, the mutation of coldness and moisture, into the man, which is
done by the sole heat of fire, as hath been sayd; for heat sequesters,
and separates the superfluityes of moisture, and will establish the
idea of itselfe in the philosophicale subject, which is the tincture..

<lang>en</lang>

<header>Discourse 34.</header>

The opinion or flattery of men attributs wonderfull births to some persons above others, but certainly, fabulous, as to Alexander the great, begotten not by philip king of Macedonia, but Jupiter Hammon; to Romulus and Remus, by Mars; to plato, borne of the virgin perictione violated by the image of Apollo: for the heathen would demonstrate themselves to proceed from the Gods, as also Thessalus the Sonne of Hippo=crates the physitian doth amongst other things endeavour to perswade himselfe to descend from Apollo at Athens: but wee cannot easily believe them, who know, that they were neither men nor Gods, to whome they referre the originall of their linage: And if there were amongst men as divine, wee conceive all those magnificent actions boasted in the worke, through the adulation of subjects, disciples, or others, were things fained of them, and insinuated into the opinion of the vulgar by forged writings: The thing is different, that the philosophy ascribes an unusu=all conception and nativity to their Sonne; for hee hath something peculiar above all other things, borne in the world, hee being conceive in baths, and borne in the aire. Wee know that women being barren through overmuch coldnesse and siccity may be much helped by hott baths, soe as to be made able and fitt for conception, but that it should be done in baths is a thing unheard of: that seems to appertaine to him alone, by reason of the won=derfull power of nature farre different from other things. They elsewhere say, that hee must be conceive in the bottome of the vessell, and borne in the Alembic: which opinion is more conspicuous: For the watery of baths, if there be any, will not be at the top, nor in the midle, but in the bottome of the vessell, and in the Alembic are airy vapours: where therefore con=ception is made, hee ascends into the Alembic, and by birth appears in a white colour: Blacknesse rules in the bottome, concerning which sayth the Rosary; Conception is, when the earth is dissolved into a blacke powder, and begins to retaine somewhat of the Mercury; for then the male acts upon the female, that is, <term>Azoth</term> upon the earth. And a little after: Conception and desponsation is made in putrefaction in the bottome of the vessell, and the generation of things generated will be made in the aire, so will, in the head of the vessell, that is, the Alembic: And conception in baths is nothing else but putrefaction in dung: for soe the same Rosary continueth: The body dos nothing, unlesse it be putrefied, and it cannot be putrefied, except with Mercury: And soone after: putrefaction must be effected with a most gentle heat of warme and moist dung, and by noe other thing, soe that nothing ascends, because if anything shoulde ascend, there would be a separation of the party, which ought not to be, till the male and female be perfectly joynd together, and one re=ceive the other, the signe of which perfect solution is blacknesse in the superfices. His birth is white, which is consummated at the top

of mountains, that is, in the aire or Alembike; which is by Rosinus to

by mountains hee signifies Cucurbits, and by the tops of mountains
Alembicks: and to send is according to similitude to receive the water
of them by an Alembike in a Receiver: and to reduce to the roots, is to
that, from which they proceed: and hee names Cucurbits mountains,
because Sol and Luna are found in mountains: for also in the moun=

are his words: Afterwards hee becomes red, and begins to goe upon the
waters, that is, upon metalls melted by fire, which stand in the forme
of a mercuriall water: For hee is the lord of waters, upon which
hee exerciseth authority, as Neptune, and is the King of the Sea,
and the possessor of mountains. Of Xerxes King of persia they
report, that being upon an expedition into Greece, hee sent an
embassy to the Sea, and to the mountaine Athos, that they would
doe him noe wrong, that by the force of waves, this of fire, other=
wise that hee would take revenge upon them both: But the sub=
jects, to them that were deafe, and the Sea drowned some of his
ships, and Athos destroys not a few by fire. Whereupon the King be=
ing angry, did, as lord of the Sea and mountains, command a cer=
taine number of stripes to be inflicted upon the first, and a great
part of the mountaine hee cast into the sea: but these things doe
demonstrate the rashnesse rather than the prudence of soe great a
King: Hee, of whome wee speake, purgeth all waters from obstacles,
and impuritys, not by edict, but fact, and freely passeth through them,
which also (which is more) hee congealeth, soe as by their hardnesse to
bear his chariot, in which ships sailed before: Hee makes mountains
equall with valleys, and fears not the flames of fire, and therefore
goes freely wherefoever hee please, from the pillars of Herculis in
the farthest part of India, to the pillars of Dionysius.

<lang>en</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>Discourse 35.</header>

Lycurgus that Law-maker of the Spartans showed to the people in
an assembly in the Theater by an ocular example, how farre custome
and use whether good or evill may prevayle, hee bringing in two whelps
produced at one litter, and between both setting a pott full of meat,
and a hare; one of which whelps seeing the food and the hare, left
the food and pursues the hare, because hee was soe accustomed; but
the other not regarding the hare, emptyes the pott, hee being also
soe accustomed: which being done, beholde, sayth hee, here you see by
little ones, how farre education and custome may prevayle, even in those,
which nature hath produced exactly alike, and of the same litter: After
this manner therefore is it convenient to rectify or direct nature to bet-
ter things, which is as wax, easily flexible to vice or virtue. That
which hath been demonstrated to be true in politicks, the philosophers
doe agree to be also soe in physicks. How effectuall custome is in
men and brute, daily examples are seen throughout the whole world,
in vegetables also are many obvious, but in minerals and metallick
bodyes wee have not soe much experience: yet nevertheless they pre-
pare their stone by custome in fire agreeable to it, as they in innumerable
places doe signify. For it must bee nourished by fire, as a child by milke
upon its mothers breast: whereupon sayth Emiganus; beholde the babe
sucking, and hinder him not: And Bodillus, the infant borne is nou-
rished by milke and fire alone, and by little and little whilst hee
is very young, and the more hee is burned, his bones being comforted,
hee is brought to youth, to which attaining, hee is able to provide for
himselfe: And Arnold in the Rosary, booke 2. chapt 7. Yet the medicine
must be a long time roasted by fire, and nourished as a child
with the breast. These same things have the most antient philo-
sophers been pleased to demonstrate by the allegoryes of Triptole-
mus and Achilles accustomed to lye and being hardened in fire, they
both denoting nothing else but the Chymicall subject, besides which
the fable would be insignificant and absurd, unworthy either to be
appropriated to other morale things, or inculcated to the eare of the
learned: Ceres did as a Nurse feed Triptolemus in the day time
with milke, and in the night laid him in fire, whereupon the
youth being very well nourished, his father Eleusius at length
tooke notice of it: hereupon Ceres killed Eleusius, and gave the
youth Triptolemus a chariott drawn with winged Dragons,
whereby hee passed through the aire into all parts of the world,
and taught men how to sow corne: But this Triptolemus is the
tincture nourished by fire after a philosophicall manner, which
being drawne by Serpents, that is, Mercury, taught men how the

<header>Discourse 35.</header>

philosophers seeds are to be cast into their earth. & these same things
are ascribed to Osiris, who to that end tooke a view of the whole world,
as wee have elsewhere demonstrated, and to Dionysius, who travelled
throughout the world teaching men the use of wine: Now these three
Triptolemus, Osiris, and Dionysius are of one intention and office;
yea one thing; as also Achilles, who being most valiant was to be
sent to the Trojan warre; his father was Peleus, that is, earth, or the
mountaine Peleus, his mother Thetis goddess of the sea or water; from
those Achilles descended: But at their nuptials the golden apple of Eris
was produced, the first cause of the Trojan warre, wherefore pro-
ceding from those nuptials reason there was that hee should determine
it: Achilles is sayd to be hardened by his mother after the same manner
as the aforesaid Triptolemus by Ceres, concerning whome wee have
sayd enough in the 6th booke of Hieroglyphicks, which here to repeat
wee thinke superfluous. The nutriment therefore of the stone is fire;
but it is not thereby (as some may falsely imagine) extended into lon-
gitude, latitude, and profundity, nor doth it receive increment in
weight; because it acquires onely virtue, maturation, and colour from
fire, carrying all the rest with it as viatic provision: For when it
parts are from severall places congested, purged, and prepared, it hath in
it all things requisite for it: whereupon sayth a philosopher in the Ro-
sary.

> Omnia fert secum quibus indiget haec aqua factum.

Nor indeed is any strange thing added to it from the beginning to the
end, except it be homogenous, nor separated, if not heterogeneous;
But every man ought to consider, before hee begin any thing, how hee
may come to the knowledge of those Dragons that are to be joyned to
the chariot of Triptolemus, which are winged and volatile, which
if you desire to know, you will find them in the philosophicall dung:
for they are dung, and are generated from dung, and are that vessell,
which Mary sayth, is not negromanticale, but the regiment of your
fire, without which you will effect nothing: I have revealed to you
the truth, which by incredible pains I acquired from the monuments
of the Antients, not without the expense of many years.

## Embleme 36. Of the secrets of Nature.

->The Stone, that is, Mercury, is cast upon the earth, and exalted on<-
->mountains, and resides in the aire, and is nourished in water.:-:<-

## Epigramme 36.

> The Stone, as refuse, is by some supposd,
> Throwne upo' th' earth, to rich and poore disclosd:
> Some say 'tis on the highest mountains place,
> Some say ith' aire, in water some at last;
> What they all say is true, but I do haue you
> That noble prize in mountains to pursue.

<vocab>Mercury, Stone, Nature, mountains, aire, water, Epigramme</vocab>

--- Page 128 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>Discourse 36.</header>
<meta>Catchword: As in</meta>

All men, that have but once heard the name and power of the Stone, unless they be such as are altogether incredulous, doe commonly aske where it is to be found, that they may runne as it were by a straight line to it? The philosophers answer in two places: first, that Adam brought it with him out of paradise, that it is in you, in mee, and in every man, and that birds flying carry it with them out of farre countryes: Secondly, that it may be found in the earth, mountains, aire, and water. Which therefore must we adhere to, whether to this, or that? To both, in my opinion, but in a peculiar respect, though the last is more plausible, and seems to us more safe. It is sayd to be throwne upon the earth, because the Element of earth doth first appear in a darke and blacke body: Secondly, because it is a thing of meane and small esteeme, which is trodden upon in the wayes of travellers, and in dung itselfe; Whereupon sayth Rosarius, if I should name it, fooles would not believe that to be it: And Calid asking Morienus, and trying to get much out of him, had answer from him thus: This, as a wise man sayth, hath both the rich and the poore, both the liberall and the cowboy, going also and sitting. For it is throwne in the wayes, and trampled upon in dunghills, and many men have already digged in dunghills for it, and herein have been deceived. Mundus also in Turba sayth, if the sellers knew it, they would not sell it at so mean a rate. And Arnold affirms, that the Stone may be had gratis, as much as a man will, and for that reason noe man ought to be interrogated. All which things are true. For who, but an inhumane wretch, will deny water and earth to him that desires it? The most antient Cimbri having desired and not able to obtaine the same gift from the Romans, entring Italy with a great army slew many thousands of the Romans with their Consuls, as historyes declare. For earth, as the mother of all things, is most pretious, as the last matter of things putrefyes, most despicable; Nothing more vile than clay or dirt, which notwithstanding is nothing else, but earth mixt with water. What more common than a clodd of earth? Yet Euripylus the sonne of Neptune offered it as a present to the heroicke Argonauts, by which not refused, but thankfully accepted, afterwards being dissolved in water Medea divines many things. For earth must be dissolved in water, otherwise neither this, nor that avayles any thing: After this manner is the Stone cast upon the earth, in which notwithstanding it doth not continue abject and contemptible, but is exalted to the mountains, as the flammivomous Athos, Vesuvius, Etna, and the like, whereof many such are seen in every part of the world: For a perpetuall fire burnes in them, which sublimeth the Stone, and rayseth it to the highest dignity. As in

mountains it growes in a rude forme out of Sulphur and Argent-vive, for
it is matured and perfected at the top of the mountains, where also
is an herbe produced, without which fire cannot be regulated, because
being cold and moist if it be cast into fire, the vehemency of it is re-
strained by its contrary. From the mountains it passeth to the aire,
where it finds habitation. For the aire becomes a house to it, wherein it
is enclosed, which is nothing else, but to be carryed in the belly of
wind, and borne in the aire, of which thing wee have spoken before.
At length it is nourished in rivers, that is, Mercury in water: For that
reason the Athenians in honour thereof instituted their hydrophoryes, be-
cause the matter of the philosophers stone is water, as Rosarius sayth, and
is understood of the water of these three. For which reason Mercury was also
so sayd to have three heads, as being marine, celestiall, and terrestriall,
because hee resides in the water, earth, and aire. He is reported to be
educated by Vulcan, notorious for theivery, because Mercury is sayd to be
onward to fire, which is volatile, and carryes away with it that wherewith
it is mixed. Mercury in the dayes of old gave lawes and discipline to
the Egyptians, yea religion also to the Thebane priests, and so a
great part of the world, because the Egyptians received polity, and sa-
cred things from Chymicale institution, from them the Greeks, from
them the Romans, from those many other nations, as hath been elsewhere
more at large demonstrated. Argos hee slew with a Stone, and conver-
ted Battus into a touch-stone called Index. What need many words? All
the volumes of the Chymists doe inculcate nothing else but Mercury, and
sufficiently confirme his power in this one versicle: What wise men seeke
in Hermes it contains. Hee therefore must be sought, till hee be found,
whether his harbour be in the aire, fire, water, or earth. For hee is in
perpetuall motion, running sometimes here sometimes there for the
service of the Chymicale Gods, as their foot-man, which his office is de-
noted, some men ascribing to him a daughter named Angelia . .

### Embleme 37. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Three things are sufficient for the Magistery, the white fume, that<-
->is, water, the green Lyon, that is, the brasse of Hermes, and aqua<-
->fatida.<-

### Epigramme 37.

The Lyon green, white fume, and stinking juice;
Are all that to our process doo conduce:
Water the mother is th' Element,
And the sole matter of the Sophicks Ens.
Thy Lyon is th' Hermeticke Stone and Brasse;
And the white fume a watry nature has.

<vocab>Magistery, white fume, green Lyon, brasse of Hermes, aqua fatida, Sophicks Ens, Hermeticke Stone</vocab>

--- Page 131 ---
<lang>English, Latin</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>Discourse 37.</header>

As three essentiale things, which are the foundation, walls, and roofe, are required to the building of every edifice, whereof one being absent, there can be noe perfection of it, soe are the many desires, in order to complete the philosophers compound, which are here named by their proper names: The author of Aurora, chapt: 20. speaking of the separation of the Elements, sayth, Earth is left in the same place, that the other three Elements may be radicated in it; if it were not for that, the Elements could have noe foundation to build upon it a new jewell house: This foundation is here called Aqua fatida, which is the mother of all the Elements, witness Rosary, from which, and by which, and with which the philosopher doe prepare it, that is, the Elixir in the begining, and in the end: It is called Aqua fatida, because it sends forth a Sulphurous stink, and the smoke of sepulcher: This is that water, which Pegasus struck out of parnassus with his hoofe, which Nonatris a mountaine of Arcadia produceth springing out of a rocke at the top, which can onely be contained in the hoofe of a horse by reason of its most excessive strength: This is that Dragons water, as Rosarius calls it, which ought to be made by an Alembike, without adding any other thing, in the making of which there is an extraordinary stink: Which words some having heard, betooke themselves to distille the dung of men or other animals, in which operation they did indure <unclear>feele</unclear> a most vehement stink, but found dung in the dung: But thinke not the philosopher to be fools, which operate in dung, you must know that the stink, if there be any, is presently changed into great fragrancy, as Lulley asserts of his Quintessence, to which hee ascribes such a sweetness of savour, if it be rightly made, that being placed at the top of a house, it allures birds flying to it, and causeth them to stand: But hee put his Quintessence in dung, by the most temperate heat of which that fragransy followes, which some men having tryed with strong wine, but in vaine, did therefore accuse Lulley of vanity, whereas themselves ought rather to be reproved of folly, who never tasted Lulleys wine; but that golden poet better understood Lulley, who in his first booke of purification thus sings:

-> At non ille quidem sensit, quæ dicere prima <-
-> Est fatis visuj, neq[ue] tum stillantia vina <-
-> miscebat, &c. <-

-> Hee thought not what at first hee seemd to say, <-
-> Nor then mixed stilling wines, &c. <-

After Aqua fatida comes the green Lyon: concerning which sayth Rosarius; you have sought for greenness, supposing, that brass was a leprosy body, because of that greenness which it hath: and therefore I declare to you, that whatsoever is perfect in brass, is that greeness

alone, which is in it: because that greenness is by our magistery suddenly 
turned into our most true gold, and this wee have had experience of: 
yet you can noe way prepare the stone without diverse green and 
liquor, which is seen to spring in our mines: O blessed greenness, 
which dost generate all things: From whence you must know, that 
noe vegetable, and noe fruit appears to germinate without a green 
colour: in like manner you must know, that the generation of this thing is 
green, wherefore the philosophers call it the bud. These are the words of 
Rosarius: This is the philosophers gold, and brasse, and stone noted in 
the chapters, a fume, vapour and water, the spittle of Luna, which is joyned 
to the light of Sol: This green Lyon fights with the Dragon, but is by him 
overcome, and in length of time devoured; and the Lyon being putrefyed, 
sweetness is expected to proceed from his mouth, as being slayne by Sampson: 
The Dragon getting the upper hand, soe fills himselfe with the Lyons 
flesh, as to burst a little and dye: Of which, the fatt alone of a Lyon curing 
quotidian Feavers, and grace and favour being obtained between kings 
and people anoynted therewith, a most excellent medicine may be made, 
very komodious for many maladyes. In the third place follows 
the white fume, which, if it be coagulated, becomes water, 
and performs the office of water in washing, dissolving, and 
taking away spots like Soape: This is that fire against na=
ture, which for that you find, soe called, because it is 
contrary to nature undoing and destroying that, which she 
with diligent care compounded: This fire is not endless as 
the spirit of wine or oyle, but as an incombustible matter, of 
equale duration and heat, and is a fire without light and combusti=
on of great virtue and efficacy, which to find in darkness is a 
thing of noe little difficulty, because it shines not, but of much 
greater to apply rightly to the worke; the circumstances and 
propertys of which wee have sufficiently in severale places de=
scribed.

# Embleme 38. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Rebis is, as Hermaphroditus, produced from the two moun=<-
->tains of Mercury and Venus.<-

## Epigramme 38.

Rebis is by the Antients call’d a Twinne,
Containing male and female sex therein:
For <term>Hermes</term> and <term>Venus</term> their <term>Hermaphrodite</term>
Is in two mountains sayd to have birthright.
A double sex despise not, for a King
Will from the selfe same man and woman spring.

<vocab>Rebis, Hermaphroditus, Mercury, Venus, Hermaphrodite, Antients</vocab>

--- Page 134 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>120</page-num>
<header>Discourse 38.</header>

Socrates being demanded, what Country man hee was, answered a
Citizen of the world: by which his intention was to signify, that though
hee was borne at Athens as to person, yet in mind hee freely beheld
the whole world, as his Country, and the things therein contained,
the whole earth being a wise mans Country, where hee lives well:
Soe if any one aske the philosophers what Countryman their
Hermaphrodite is, they answer, hee belongs to the world, or is in all
corners of the world, where the Elements can be found, as the Sonne
of the wife, who hath a country common with them: But seeing that
it happens not that any man is borne twice or oftener, nor first enters
this light in severall but in one place, as Socrates is owned to be an
Athenian, soe is <term>Rebis</term> reputed an inhabiter of two mountains, that is,
<term>Mercury</term> and <term>Venus</term>, from whence also the name of <term>Hermaphrodite</term>
given him by both parents; a noble and large Country is indeed noe
small helpe to doe things excellently well, in which Citizens are pre=
ferred before forreigners, and promoted to publicke offices, that they
may not lye in obscurity (as it happens in a meane place, and see
something of light come to them from the Country) but shew them=
selves, rather with their owne virtues, even in the small affaires of
a house, and give light themselves to their owne country: After this
manner these mountains unknowne to many men doe acquire fame
from the Hermaphrodite by reason of his illustrious actions, and name
famous throughout the whole world. For who though never soe
little versed in the philosophy bookes hath not knowne <term>Rebis</term>?
Who hath not seen or beheld, <term>Androgynus</term> with two heads? <unclear>Hee</unclear> verily
has been knowne to the Judges themselves, and his fame hath been
dispersed farther, than the fame even of King Alexander: Many
men doe travell out of farre countrys to see and discourse some
learned man, or otherwise remarkable for singular military in=
dustry, art or Science, but many more will betake themselves to
the sayd mountains of <term>Rebis</term>, provided they can learne where
they may be found: With how great study and care did <term>Morienus</term>
being departed from Home seeke and, at length find <term>Adfarus Alexan=
drinus</term>, hee himselfe declares in his booke, and therefore was to be
esteemed more happy, and acceptable to God, in that hee had from a
living Father, and not from dumbe masters learnt, and beheld this
thing, that is, the native place of <term>Rebis</term>; noe lesse assiduity and
diligence ought they to use, who seeke the country of <term>Rebis</term> by
themselves, being informed by reason, and the documents of books:
But though some books may sometimes seem to be clear and conspi-

nuous, yet they are soe veile, and clouded with intricacy and ob=
scurity, that by reason thereof it is very difficult to make distinction:
Wherefore wee must cautiously proceed with them, that they, which are
prepared for remedy, may not be as for poyson: They are certainly
a vast ocean, in which expert mariners sayling may by Astronomi=
call instruments know the latitude, or elevation of the Aquator above
the Horizon, the magnet showing the North pole, but by noe means
the longitude, or how great an intervalle of degrees they are distant
from the first Meridian, which is next to the Fortunate Islands; Soe
that they are uncertaine in what place they may be between west
and east: What therefore is here to be done? That which the same
mariners use to doe, consult experience with reason, and thereby
learne how to determine a long voyage by particular signs, promon=
tories, Islands, and other things, that they may not for want of consi=
deration fall upon sands and rocks: But how is lesser danger, if the
thing prosper not, but if soe, greater gaine, than there, where goods
and life are lost in an houre: But the mountains of the philoso=
phicall Mercury is not Vesuvius, nor Atlas, where sometimes it is reported
to be brought forth, but parnassus with two tops, in one of which
Hermes, in the other Venus resides: there also is Apollo with the Mu=
ses, and Hippocrene, the fountaine of pegasus, with laurell alwayes
green: it is one mountaine by name, but really two, as Hermaphro=
ditus is beheld with two heads, and two members in one body: But
what man of a thousand persists in ascending to the top of this
mountaine? Who does not begin to hesitate at the bottome being
impeded by I know not what obstacles? Who ever attaines to the
midst of it? for Non levis ascensus, si quis petat ardua, Sudor

> plurimus hunc tollit, nocturnae insomnia olivae
> Immoritur, dolet, quod mox laudaverat in se;
> Qui cupit aeternae donari frondi honore.

Wherefore it is noe strange thing, if onely one of tenne thousand un=
dertake these Herculian labours, soe as to sett foot on the top of the
mountaine, and enjoy the immortale reward of a laurell garland,
which that all those that are upright, docible, and addicted to vir=
tue and learning may perceive with joy, but the vaine and envi=
ous deprived thereof, is much to be desired.

### Embleme 39. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Oedipus having overcome Sphynx, and killd his father Lajus,<-
->marryed his mother Jocasta.<-

---

### Epigramme 39.

> Sphynx with her riddles dies who'd the many dread,
> But subtile Oedipus her wiles betrays:
> The question was, who in the morning might
> Stand four foot, two at noon, and three at night.
> Unknowne his father hee deprives of life
> And his owne mother takes to be his wife

<vocab>Oedipus, Sphynx, Lajus, Jocasta, Secrets of Nature, emblem, epigram</vocab>

--- Page 137 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>Discourse 39.</header>

Bacassor a philosopher in Turba sayth, that which you seeke for is of noe small value; for you seeke the greatest treasure, and most excellent gift of God.

And Barne, O yee students, that which the philosophers have long since intimated, saying, the truth is not discerned but by errour, and nothing begets more greife to the heart, than errour in thy art and worke: For when a man thinks hee hath done, hath a worke, hee will find nothing in his hands.

These same things the ancient philosophers were pleased to signify, propounding a Sphynx, as if shee denotes the obscurity and meander of art.

For this reason the Aegyptians, in their sacred Isiaci, which were celebrated in honour of Osiris, by mitred priests, with heads shaven, and all parts of the body clothed with a white linnen garment downe to the ankles, that they might not be knowne or discovered by the common people; erected a Statue of silence, which was called Sigalion, in the entrance of the altar, the assistants being enjoyned to keep silence, and turne their eyes to that image, and for the same cause they added the effigies of Sphynxes at the corners of the altar, which did represent the mysticall knowledge of sacred things; as Boissardus doth from ancient authors demonstrate:

<margin>Boissardus?</margin>
For Sphynx is a certaine kind of monster most obscure to the Thebans, propounding riddles, nor onely to them, but as before to the Aegyptians, soe after all other to those that aspire to art, and waiteth in the philosophers books as before the gates of Thebes; if any man can passe by the monster, hee suffers noe evill by him, but hee that through presumption of mind or witt endeavour to resolve his riddles, and cannot performe, acquires his owne destruction, that is, greife to the heart, and damage to his affaires by errour in his worke.

Hee that refers such allegories to history, hath a mushrome for a head, and a millon for a heart, as the Comedian sayth, and knows noe more than that hee deviates from the right way.

These things are too childish, and fitt for old wives, if they be taken literally, otherwise the signs and tokens of profound learning. Monstrous wild beasts are indeed sayd to be in Africa by the name of Sphynxes, but here wee discourse not of them, though the originale and denomination of this fiction seeme to be derived from them.

The philosophers Sphynx understands and useth humane speech, namely greeke, as also propounds subtile, genuine and aenigmaticall questions, in which appears a singular sharpness of understanding and learning, not soe obvious to all other men, from which brutes are exceeding distant. Hee that is conversant in the philosophers assertions will easily know them to be such: For where one thing is sayd, and another thing signifyd, there equivocation begets errour, which is not onely permitted, but also enjoyned

<vocab>Bacassor, Turba Philosophorum, Sphynx, Aegyptians, Isiaci, Osiris, Sigalion, Boissardus, Thebes, aenigmaticall, equivocation</vocab>

--- Page 138 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>124</page-num>
<header>Discourse 39.</header>

to the philosophy alone: The City of Thebes therefore being long perplexed with the riddles of Sphynx, at length came one Oedipus, who gave such responses to the questions offered, that Sphynx could not forbear throwing herselfe downe from a rocke. But who is this Oedipus? The sonne of the King of Thebes, who being borne, it being predicted to his father by an oracle, that hee should be slayne by his sonne, hee commanded Oedipus to be kild, who being hanged upon a tree and left with a rope fastened crosse his feet, was released by another countryman and brought up; who coming to mans estate had indeed swolln feet, but sufficiently declares a quicke witt above all men in unriddling the enigma propounded by Sphynx. But Sphynx is reported to have had many riddles, though this the cheife, offered to Oedipus. What is that which in the morning goeth upon foure feet, at noone upon two, in the evening upon three? what was answered by Oedipus is not knowne; but some interpret it to the age of man, who are deceived: For a Quadrangle or the four Elements of all things are first to be considered, from hence wee come to the hemisphere having two lines one straight the other crooked, that is, while Luna, from hence to a triangle, which consists of body, Spirit, and Soule, or Sol, Luna, and Mercury: whereupon sayth Rhases in his Epistle, The stone is a triangle in being, a Quadrangle in quality. Hereto also relates the one and twentyeth emblem, and the exposition thereof. Oedipus moreover is notorious for parricide and incest, which are two the most detestable sins, as can ever be thought of, but nevertheless they promoted him to a kingdome, otherwise due to him, hee having unawares killed his father refusing to give way to him, and married the Queen, the wife of Laius, his owne mother. But this is written not to be imitated by any man as history or example, it being onely feigned and allegorically introduced by the philosophers to discover the secret of their doctrine. For in the worke both happen; the first efficient, that is, the father, being kild and destroyed by his effect, that is, the sonne, and afterwards the same effect joyns the second efficient to himselfe, till they become one with him, and soe the sonne is coupled to his mother by matrimony, and enjoys his fathers kingdome, as it were by a triple right, of armes, wedlocke, and succession. Hee hath swolln feet, because hee cannot runne, and is as a toad, as hee contains the greatest secret, or a lead, going with a slow pace; because it is fixed fixing another, and not avoiding or fearing fire, which though a medium of mean repute the philosophers can by noe means be without.

<vocab>Oedipus, Sphynx, Thebes, Quadrangle, four Elements, hemisphere, Luna, triangle, body, Spirit, Soule, Sol, Mercury, Rhases, parricide, incest, Laius, efficient, effect</vocab>

## Embleme 40. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Of two waters make one, and that will be the water of Sanctity.<-

### Epigramme 40.

->Two streaming fountains here you may behold,<-
->One with hott water, but the other cold:<-
->That's call'd the Boy's, but this the Virgin spring,<-
->Joyne them, that they may be one onely thing:<-
->Thy river will participate of both,<-
->As Jove (sirnam'd) Hammon's fountaine doth.<-

<vocab>Embleme, Secrets of Nature, water of Sanctity, Epigramme, fountains, Jove, Hammon</vocab>

--- Page 140 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>126</page-num>
<header>Discourse 40.</header>

The miracles of waters are soe many and soe great, as scarce to be
comprehended in a large volume; concerning which severall authors
have here and there treated: But above all, the two philosophicall wa-
ters are celebrated with that name, because they doe as it were not on-
ly emulate, but also exceed the virtues and propertyes of all the rest.
The river Sybaris, Axus of Macedonia, and Melas of Baotia make
cattle blacke, if they drinke thereof; but the river Crathis, Clitumnus
of Mevania, and Cephisus make blacke cattle white. The water of
Sinnessa in the country of Campania takes away barrenness from
both Sexes. The river Apsidisius makes women barren: Labura a foun-
taine of Mesopotamia hath water of a sweet smell; the water of Any-
gor in Peloponnesus stinks very much: The fountaine of Jupiter Ham-
mon is cold in the day time, hott by night, and morning and evening
luke-warme by turnes. Wee need not insist upon other, all things even
contrary one to the other are performed by the philosophy water. Of
which sayth Lully in his booke of the Quintessence; distin: 3. of inte-
ration; And soe there is a twofold consideration in the art, that is, of
the single nature of one metalle to make two contrary liquors in the
composition; one, which hath a fixing, congealing, and hardning vir-
tue, and the other, which is volatile, unfixes, and soft. But that se-
cond liquor is hardned, fixed, and congealed by the first. From both
which liquors results one stone congealed, fixed, and hardened, which
hath the virtue of congealing not a thing congealed, so harden a thing
mollifyes, and to mollify a hard thing: From which it is evident,
what these two waters are, and why to be reduced into one water:
For the stone is called water, because fusible, and on the contrary
water stone, because frangible: But these waters are drawne out of
different places, sometimes in a long tract, as may be seen at Rome
about Aqua Virginia, and other artificiall fountains, and then they
must be mixed by conflux, that one may be made of two. For if
one be of a hott and the other of a cold virtue, being mixed together
they will acquire mixed virtues, and after a wonderfull manner re-
gulate one the other: From hence are most excellent medicinale, and
bath waters to be produced, which will profligate all sort of diseases
and maladyes, and restore a man to firme health. Nature doth in-
deed by her owne artificie of composition confound and mix many
waters in the bowels of the earth with severall virtues of Mynes, which
are wholsome and remedyous to many sicke and infirme persons, but
if art be added with due regiment, all evacuations being premised and
other things to be pretermittted, and mix those things that are to be
mixed together, that composition will be farre more effectuall:
Which, though it seem artificiall, is notwithstanding meerly naturall,
because of things different it is made one simple homogenous matter,

which can never be effected by art. A mixtion and confusion is indeed cau=
sed by art, without the helpe of nature nor has any naturall union,
which is made by nature alone: There is an artificiall mixtion of various
simples in <term>Triacle</term>, which is performd by contrition and fermentation,
but noe man will (except inconsiderately) affirme it to be a naturall
composition, much lesse a homogenous medicament: As to the artificiall
mixtion of substances it is manifest, that they doe not enter one
into the other in the least part, though they cannot againe be divided
and separated one from the other by the industry of man. But as to
the mixtion of all qualityes wee must enquire, whether the first <term>Tri=
acle</term> of all simples may be reduced into one <term>Quintesence</term>; <insert>as whether they</insert> <meta>strikethrough</meta>
or yet in their powder or substances be, as before, as accident in a subject,
or colour in a wall; and then what is to be sayd of the second, third, and
fourth qualityes? probable it is that all the qualityes are yet inherent
in their own Subjects, and not compounded together by a true natu=
rall mixtion; otherwise, if the qualityes should relinquish their bo=
dyes, there would be four <term>Quintesences</term> in every composition arti=
ficiall, according to the number of the order of the qualityes, first,
second &c: without their bodyes, and separable; which they say
is not soe with the <term>coagulum</term> of a Hare, which being in flux
by the tenuity of the blood stops and as it were coagulates it,
but in the coagulation and contrition putts and resolves the same,
for the operation of vinegar, lead, and many other things are con=
trary according to the different use of them, because nature hath
soo wonderfully mixes them: Soo also hath the <term>philosophicall
water</term> different and contrary virtues, because nature hath by the
helpe of art mixes it with things contrary into one indivisible
substance, which is nothing els but a <term>Quintesence</term>, in respect of
other things to be mixed with it.

### Embleme 41. Of the secrets of nature.

->Adonis is killd by a Boar, to whome Venus hasting tinged the<-
->Roses with her blood.<-

### Epigramme 41.

> Myrrha, conspiring with her Sire; gave breath
> To th' fair Adonis, tush'd by a Boar to death:
> Sore Venus runs, with a thorne in foot, which bleeds,
> And therewith tings the lilly Roses reds;
> The Goddesse weeps, the Syrians can't refraine,
> Who's layd amongst the lettuce being slayne.

<vocab>Embleme, Epigramme, Adonis, Venus, Myrrha, Boar, lettuce</vocab>

<header>Discourse 41.</header>

How improperly some of the Mythologists resolve the Allegory of Adonis, and ascribe him sometimes to the Sunne; the Boare, by which hee was slayne, to a hard winter, sometimes to the seed of wheat, which is six moneths with Proserpina under the earth, and as many above the earth with Venus, is elsewhere sufficiently explained, and refuted by us. Here wee doe with the consent of all men declare the philosophicall Sunne to be meant by Adonis: From whence the Verses:

->Omnia sunt idem, Dionysus, Sol, et Adonis.<-

And Orpheus: . . . . . , Qui vario <unclear>lersari</unclear> nomine Adoni,
->Germinum et idem author, pariter puer atq; puella.<-

All which are by no means to be understood of the Coelestial, but a philosophicall Sunne; for this expresseth both sexes, that not: Soe also they attribute the very same thing to Dionysus, and the Sunne; as to Adonis, and on the contrary, as also to Osiris. Adonis is killd by a Boare, that is, by <term>Acetum acerrimum</term>, or the solutive water, which hath terrible teeth like a boar, with which hee tusheth Adonis; because the philosophicall Sol is mortally wounded by that boar, loosnes in the members and puts off; But Venus endeavours to helpe her paramour, who being dead, shee layes him amongst Lettice, and watchs him. After the same manner is Osiris killd by Typhon, and cut into severall peices, which Isis, the wife of Osiris, gathers up, and being joynd together buryes. The same mourning, which followes the death of Osiris every year in Ægypt, was also celebrated after the death of Adonis in Syria and the bordering kingdoms; where for some dayes weeping and wailing being heard, afterward were signs of joy given, and dancing, as if hee, that was dead, now lives anew being conveyed to heaven: From whence arose the vanity of their religion or heathenish superstition, which grew to an excessive height, the Devill giving occasion, and procuring false miracles. Adonis was (as they feigne) borne of Cinyras King of Cyprus, and Myrrha his daughter, hee is sayd to be borne in incest (if history be considered, with her, if an allegory, not unlawfull, but principally necessary: For if the mother and Sonne, or the father and daughter be not joynd together, and an offspring produced from them, nothing is perfected in this art. For here, the nearer the man and wife are one to the other in blood, in the first degree of consanguinity, or second, the more fruitfull they are, and on the contrary, the more remote, the more unfruitfull, which is not tolerable in the matrimony of men. For this cause Oedipus marrys his owne mother, Jupiter his sister, soe also Osiris, Saturne, Sol, the royall servant, Gabritius; Sol (in a metaphor of Belinus in the Rosary),

speaks thus of <term>Adonis</term>, that is, of himselfe: Know yee, that my father <term>Sol</term> hath given mee power above all power, and cloathes mee with the gar=ment of glory: And a little after: For I am his onely sonne and more like my father, &c. I divest my servants of their power and nature, and cloath them with my beautifule splendour and light (which my father gave mee) in all their works: For I am excellent, who doe exalt and depresse all things, and none of my servants is above mee, but one, who is permitted to be repugnant and contrary to mee: And hee destroyes mee, yet destroyes not my nature: And hee is <term>Saturne</term>, who separates all my parts. Afterwards I goe to my mother, who gathers together all my divided and separated members: I illuminate all those things that appertaine to mee, and cause light to appeare openly in the way from my father <term>Saturne</term>, as also from my mother, who is an enemy to mee. The words hitherto spoken are soe clear, as to disperse darkenesse from the eyes of the mind, even to him that is but meanly conversant in the reading of authors, and discover the light of the sunne, which is abundantly perspicuous in the coherentyes insinuated of things and per=sons: For those things which are true, though shaded under the veyle of an allegory, doe consent in wonderfull harmony one with another, but those things which are false are repugnant, and inconsistent with themselves, and other.
