## Embleme 42. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Nature, Reason, Experience, and reading must be the guide, Staffe, Spectacles, and Lamp to him that is employed in Chymicall affairs.<-

### Epigramme 42.

> pursuing art, let Nature be your guide,
> Tracing her steps your foot will not backe-slide:
> Let reason be your staffe, experience add,
> Strength to your sight, discerning good from bad:
> In darkenesse reading will as light direct,
> That ambiguity you may detect.

<vocab>Nature, Reason, Experience, Chymicall, art, ambiguity</vocab>

--- Page 146 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>132</page-num>
<header>Discourse 42.</header>

The chances, which may happen to travellers, are innumerable, especially if they take a journey on foot by night through slippery-
and dangerous places; to which four things are requisite as chief-
ly necessary, not to speake of the victualls, provider, and a strong
body: In the first place; a companion or guide not ignorant of the
wayes, through which hee must passe: For if one ignorant man
guide another, the same thing happens to them as to blind men, if
they doe not both fall headlong into a ditch, yet into errors and
mistakes: Secondly, a staffe; by which the slipperinesse and perils
of the way, lest it be detrimentall to a man, may be avoyded: Third-
ly, sound eyes; for such journeys are most dangerous to the blind or
dimme sighted: Fourthly, a lamp or lighted torch, that the diversity
and difference of the wayes may be discerned. After the same
manner if a man expose himselfe to a most difficult journey in
pursuit of the <term>philosophicall medicine</term>, hee will, besides charge
and strength of body, desire four things exactly parallell and cor-
respondent to those aforesaid, to witt, Nature, Reason, Experience,
and reading: whereof if any one be wanting, the rest will doe
little or noe good at all: For by these, as by four wheeles, the philo-
sophicall chariot moves, to which one of the wheeles cannot be
wanting, if it be lost, it avayls nothing. Nature presupposeth natu-
rall bodyes and spirits, as subjects first ministred by nature, upon
which art must afterwards act, preparing, purifying, and fitting it,
that thereof may be made that, which art promiseth for the end:
Soe a potter takes water and earth, a Glasse-maker ashes and
sand, a Smith iron, copper, tinne, lead, silver or gold, a Tanner
raw hides, and soe other men other things: Such respect alsoe
hath the operator of <term>Chymistry</term> to his materials: Their materi-
alls are to them very well knowne even the first day, here, when
hee begins, continues for the most part ignorant of <unclear>them</unclear> for many
years, I will not say, during his whole life. Nature doth indeed
point at the matter, but there are many things, which obscure the
impression of nature, that it cannot be knowne. The first intention
therefore is seriously to contemplate how nature proceeds in her ope-
ration, to the end that the naturall subjects of <term>Chymistry</term> may
without defect or superfluity be had: Therefore Nature must be
the guide and companion of soe great a journey, whose footsteps
must be traced. Secondly, reason must be as a staffe to keep the
steps and foot steady and firme, that they may not slip or waver;
For without the exercise of reason a man will be apt to fall

<vocab>philosophicall medicine, Nature, Reason, Experience, reading, Chymistry, operator</vocab>

--- Page 147 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>Discourse 42.</header>

into errors, and therefore says the philosopher; Whatsoever you hear,
consult with reason, whether it can be soe, or noe: For noe man is
forced to believe or performe things impossible, except hee be of a
weake memory, dull genius, and foolish imagination to impose upon
himselfe; accepting things false for true, and rejecting true for false:
They say alsoe that they care not for words, whatsoever may be
spoken, but onely for things, what may be understood; And that
words are for things, and not things for words; As for example; suppose
a man say that glasse is made malleable by the <term>philosophicall
tincture</term>; Why shall not I believe it, if provided reason dictates it?
Thirdly, Experience will be as Spectacles, by which things may be
seen at a distance; These are optical instruments, invented and made
by art, to helpe and amend the weaknesse of many eyes. Much like to
these are experiments about the <term>minerals matter</term> of every kind, dyed,
seen or truly heard, which the more they are in memory, the more
will a man of reason draw from them, and compare them among
and with other things, that hee may perceive, what is true, what not.
Fourthly, Reading dos as it were kindle a clear lamp in the understan-
ding, without which there will be every where darknesse and thick
clouds. But the reading of good authors ought to be often repeated,
otherwise it will not be at all effectual. Whereupon <term>Balassor</term> in <term>Turba</term>
sayth:

> Hee therefore, that is of an even temper, and exerciseth pati-
> ence without regrett, will attain to thy art as by a direct line, but
> hee, that thinks himselfe able sooner to reap benefit from our books,
> is deceived, and it had been better not to have looked therein, than
> ever to have touched them:

And soe on . . .

## Embleme 43. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Giue ear to the Vultur's words, which are noewise false.<-

### Epigramme 43.

The Vultur on the mountaine stands in sight,
Himselfe proclaiming daily, blacke, and white,
Citrine, and red; nor false: The Raven is
The same, which without wings by nature flyes
In dismale darkenesse, and in glorious light,
For either will to th'art conduct you right.

<vocab>Embleme, Secrets of Nature, Vultur, mountaine, blacke, white, Citrine, red, Raven, flyes, dismale darkenesse, glorious light, th'art</vocab>

--- Page 149 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>Discourse 43.</header>

Wee daily in many places hear birds prattling or imitating the voyce
of man, such as parrotts, Ravens, Dawes, and pyes. For pliny writes
that, at the time wherein hee publishes his history, Agrippina the
wife of Claudius Caesar had a thrush, imitating the speech of men.
The young Caesars had also a Starling and Nightingales accustomed to
the Greeke and Latine tongues, and daily speaking new things, with
a long forme of words also. But such birds now frequently happening
they are thought less worthy of admiration, for all birds endued
with a broad tongue may by use and custome be brought to speake
and babble. But that vultur, which the philosophers make mention
of, did not learne words, if any hee utter, by use, but by nature tacit-
ly expresseth them. The philosophers doe indeed say, that hee
continually cryes out, and with a loud voyce pronounceth, who and
what hee is, wherein hee imitates great princes, who in the beginning
of their intimation will allwayes make their titles and inheritance
known, not in token of any pride, but for other reasons, that they
also themselves may know, what principalityes they governe, or
right of inheritance they pretend to. Soe it is very materiall to
know, what colours, as ensigns of armes and titles, the philosophi-
tall bird is adorned with, and wherein hee excells all other. I, sayth hee,
as Rosarius hath it from Hermes, am the blacke of white, and the ci-
trine of red, and certainly I speake truth and lye not. Here affirming
himselfe blacke, white, citrine, and red, and such hee really is, for
though hee doth not as yet actually possesse the last colours, yet hee
expects the inheritance of them. Thereupon sayth Rosinus in his booke
of divine interpretation; Take the Stone, which is blacke, white,
red, citrine, that admirable bird, which flyes without wings in the
darknesse of the night, and clearnesse of the day. For coloration
ariseth from the bitternesse which is in his throat, but more water
proceeds from his blood, as Alexander says. Take (my Sonne) the
stone of four colours. The philosophers books doe abundantly de-
clare and insinuate that the stone hath all these colours, which
are principall, in a sucessive order. But why the philosophicall
subject is called a vultur, there is reason to be alledged. Of Vulturs
the blacke are of most esteem, they are rapacious, but fly slowly, be-
cause of the bignesse of body. They say that this bird conceives
without the seed of the male, and generates without coition, and
that their offspring doe arrive to a great age even to a hundred
years. Their nests they build in high rocks, nor man hath attained
to them. Twoe kichens are for the most part seen. They are helpe-
full and commodious agaynst Serpents. They are offended and

<vocab>vultur, philosophers, Agrippina, Claudius Caesar, Starling, Nightingales, Rosarius, Hermes, Rosinus, Stone, coloration, Alexander, Serpents, kichens</vocab>

--- Page 150 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>136</page-num>
<header>Discourse 43.</header>

perplexed with the East wind. When they begin to lay eggs, they
bring a thing out of the Indyes, which is as a nutt, having within a
thing that may be moved, and forthwith yields a sound, which when
they have applyed to them, they produce many young things,
but one onely remains, which is called Immusulus. Pomponius Ton=
sius in Pliny is a witnesse, that Vultures of all animals are the
most innocent, because they doe not at all meddle with any of those
things which men sow, plant, and breed. Moreover they kill noe
animals. They also abstaine from birds, though dead, by a certaine
instinct of affinity. For this cause they were best esteemed in divina=
tion, as the originals of the Citty of Rome declare. The philoso=
phicall bird expressing almost all these propertyes of Vultures, is
therefore by Hermes and others not undeservedly called Vultur, which
is also slow in flight, and blacke of colour; It conceives by itselfe;
For thus saith Rosarius about the end; And it is the Dragon, which
wedds itselfe, and impregnats itselfe, and brings forth in its owne
season &c. And Rosinus to Sarratant. And that is the Serpent, ad=
ministring pleasure to itselfe, impregnating itselfe, and bringing
forth in one day, &c. It lives and endures a very long time, and
multiplyes itselfe. For that which Virgill writes of the bird called
a phoenix, is as proper to this, because it is the same.

-> *Alipedem Cervum ter vincit, at illum* <-
-> *Multiplicat novies phoenix reparabilis ales.* <-

-> Three ages of a Hart the Raven lives, <-
-> Which the rare phoenix nine times ore survives. <-

It is very difficult to climbe this birds nest. It fights with the mercuriall
serpent, and overcomes it, that is, Sol with Luna. It is conceived by
the wind, and carryes in its belly, and breeds in the aire. The
Stone white, containing a little Stone sounding within, is by many
men called <insert>it</insert> Etites. One onely Immusulus is found in the philoso=
phers nest. The philosophicall bird is also most innocent, because
it hurts noe body, it is beneficiall to all that know it, and most ex=
cellent in divination. But why doth it make its nest on a mountaine
and sitting soe, cry out? Rosinus answers according to Rhasy, who sayth
Behold the most high mountains, which are both on the right and
left, and climbe up thither: there is our Stone found, and in another
mountaine, which produceth all sort of paintry colours, and Spirits
or Species, where likewise it is: Morienus: Climbe up the high mountaine,
planted with trees, because there is our stone found, and hidden: And Hermes:
Take you the blessed Stone, breake it to peices, and wash the red stone, from
which it exhales what which is found in mountains, and especially sometimes in old sinks.

### Embleme 44. Of the secrets of Nature.

-> Typhon kills Osyris by deceipt, and disperseth the parts of his body on all sides, but the famous Isis gathered them together. <-

### Epigramme 44.

> Adonis Syria, Greece hath Dionyse,
> Egypt Osyris, all the same o'th wise:
> Isis, Osyris into peices cutt
> By cruell Typhon, all together putt:
> But the productive part is lost ith Sea,
> For Sulphur Sulphur's father's fled away.

<vocab>Typhon, Osyris, Isis, Adonis, Dionyse, Sulphur, Egypt, Syria, secrets of Nature, Alchemy, Mythology</vocab>

--- Page 152 ---
<lang>en</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>Discourse 44.</header>

The allegory of Osyris was had elsewhere (namely in the first booke of Hieroglyphicks) reduced to its true originals, which is Chymicall, and fully explained it: therefore here to repeat the same things (though the same things ought to be spoken of the same things) was judgd superfluous: but nevertheless we will here forme a parallell discourse, which will always be and remaine within the prison of that ancient Chymy, which is solely decanted and deciphered by the poets: <margin><unclear>M.</unclear></margin> Will you perswade mee that Osyris was a God, or King of the Ægyptians? I will not believe, though you perswade mee to believe; For doggs (as it is in the proverb) smell farre otherwise, farre otherwise Swine: I doe plainly deny him to be a God, and you will assent to mee, unless you be a heathen, or of an opinion contradictory to right reason: And that hee was noe King, is demonstrated by all the circumstances which are elsewhere considered: The Sunne hee is, but philosophicall, the name of which being in many authors often attributed to it, the vulgar, who know noe other light but that of the world, have interpreted for it. The philosophicall Sunne hath denomination from the sunne of the world, because it contains the properties of nature descending from that celestiall Sunne, or agreeable to it: Osyris therefore, Dionysus, Bacchus, Jupiter, Mars, Adonis, Œdipus, perseus, Achilles, Triptolemus, pelops, Hippomenes, and pollux, are the Sunne, but Isis, Juno, Venus, the mother of Œdipus, Danae, Deidamia, Atalanta, Helena, the Moone; as also Latona, Semele, Europa, Leda, Antiope & Thalia. And these are the parts of the compound, which before operation is called Stone, and by the names of every metalle, Magnesia: After operation, Orus, pyrrhus, Apollo, Æsculapius. The adjuncts are Typhon, python, the Boar: The Artists are Hercules, Ulysses, Jason, Theseus, pyrithous: The labours and perills, which were undergone by these artists, are innumerable: The labours of Hercules, the errors of Ulysses, the perils of Jason, the attempts of Theseus, and the removals of pyrithous may be seen: This is the great volume of matter and doctrine, through which in every page Vulcan, Mercury, and Saturne doe runne backwards and forwards, the first, as the father and cause of all, without which nothing is effected, the second, as the matter and forme; the third, as the efficient. Sol takes his sister Luna to wife: Jupiter Juno, as Saturne Rhea, and Osyris Isis: Dionysus is ripped out of his mother Semele being burnt by the lightening of Jove to be matured in the thigh of Jupiter his father, Æsculapius out of Coronis his mother: Dionysus coming to maturity shows men a new way of drinking wine; undertaking an expedition even to the Indyes; Osyris and Triptolemus, the sowing and use of fruits, Æsculapius, the administration of me-

## Embleme 45. Of the Secrets of Nature.
->The worke is perfected by Sol and his shade.<-

---

### Epigramme 45.
> The Sunne noe bodyes dense can penetrate,
> And soe the adverse party a shade create:
> Which, though it bee of all things least sett by,
> Is much conducing to Astronomy;
> But more to th' Sophi are the sunne and shade,
> Because they perfect the auriferous trade.

<vocab>Sol, shade, Sophi, auriferous trade, Astronomy, Secrets of Nature</vocab>

--- Page 155 ---
<lang>en</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>Discourse 45.</header>

As in a round or sphericall pallace, a fire kindled in one place illustrates the parts above and below round about all the walls, except those, where a table or the like standing in the midle may by its objection and interposition impede, and admitt a darke shadow: Soe also the sunne being placed in that great pallace or curvas theater of the heaven dos by its rayes illuminate every concavity of heaven, the bodyes containd therein which are diaphanous and receptive of light, that is, all starryes whatsoever, both erratick and fixed, except where the density of the earth in the midle may prohibit: For there a blacke and darke shadow, which is called night, continuing soe long, till it be driven away by the presence of the Sunne, and light established and made conspicuous in stead thereof: A shadow therefore and night is the privation or absence (as day on the contrary is the irradiation and circumfusion) of the Solar light. A shadow is that which cannot endure the aspect of the Sunne, and therefore avoids and absconds itselfe sometimes in this sometimes in that part of the earth, as the Sunne is in opposition. & the Sunne and shadow never saw themselves together, though, if nature would admit, they might in every moment, but the Sunne considering her as an enemy to himselfe, <margin>Buchanan ?</margin> allways pursues her flying, and can never take her weary, as Buchanan in his booke of spheres ingeniously hath declared. For imitation and example of that great Sunne, and shadow thereof, the philosophers have observed that their Sunne hath also a blacke, cloudy, and flying shadow: whereupon sayth Hermes, my sonne! take from the ray its shadow, that is, see that you bring your sunne round about by the primum mobile, of which Vulcan is made overseer, that even that part of your Earth, which is now covered with a shady night, may obtaine the clear light of the Sunne: for if the whole Firmament of the heaven with all things containes were not every naturall day, that is, in twenty four hours altogether circumvolved by the first motion, but the Sunne onely moved by its owne (called the second and annuale) motion, it would happen that they, which are Antipodes below us, would for the spase of almost six moneths have a night, and wee a day, and afterwards on the contrary they a day and wee a night; soe that the whole year would consist of one night and day, as now those courses are by experience and reason confirmed to be most true under both the poles: But it seems farre otherwise to divine providence, which therefore hath ordained two motions of the planets, the first and second, and soe distributes the year into many dayes: Now that shadow and sunne doe together make day and night, which the sunne by itselfe alone could not effect, whose properly it is to illuminate all opposite places and bodyes, but not make a shadow, except accidentally by his absence: Soe also the philosophicall Sunne des with its shadow make a day, that is, light and night or darknesse, namely Latona or magnesia, whose shadow Democritus teacheth, as appears in the third booke of his

<vocab>sphericall pallace, Solar light, Buchanan, booke of spheres, Hermes, primum mobile, Vulcan, Antipodes, divine providence, Latona, magnesia, Democritus</vocab>

<header>Discourse 45.</header>

Golden table at the beginning, must be extinguished and consumed by a fiery medicine. The utility of shadow in Astronomical affairs is soe great, that without them that science can scarce be perfected; Chymists doe also ascribe it to their shadow, that their art comes to perfection; for what is the Sunne without a shadow? As a clapper without a bell: & that indeed makes the first motion to a sound, but they give the sound; that is the quill, they the instrument; that the tongue, they the great mouth. A shadow is a most contemptible thing, not next to a Being, soe also the philosophers shadow is a thing blacker than blacke, as they terme it, or of lesse esteem than weeds, not in respect of itselfe, but the opinion of men and plenty; What more commodious than fire, what more pretious than water, what more amiable than earth, which yields flowery and all amiable things, what more pleasant than aire, which being restraind and obstructed, all things cease to be pleasant? yet nevertheless, because they are exposed to many uses in their large sphere, they are esteemed most vile, by a praepostrous imagination; soe also is both the common and philosophicale shadow undervalued: & they that live long in subterranean shadows, if brought suddenly to the cleare light of the Sunne, doe loose their eye-sight, soe they that reside and operate in the philosophicale shadow alone, and doe not joyne the Sunne with it, are deprived of judgment and the sight of the mind, and frustrated of the effort. The celestiall Sunne being elevated upon the south there is greater heat and lesse shadow; soe also here heat being increased the shadow is diminished, and on the contrary; Wee must therefore begin the sunne being from the meridionale side bending itselfe againe to the top of our head, in Capricorne; and the first operation will be even finished at Aries, then begin the womens worke, even to Leo; and afterwards labour proceeds from labour, till the year takes the tayle with the head, as a snake, that is to say, is compleated.

## Embleme 46. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Two Eagles come together, one from the East, the other from the West.<-

### Epigramme 46.

> Great Jove two Eagles out of Delphi sent
> To th' East and Western parts, for this intent,
> That hee the midst of the earth might find;
> Which there returning, well resolv'd his mind.
> But these two Eagles are two stones, which hast
> One from the East, the other from the West.

Cicero in his booke of the nature of the Gods declares the most antient 
Apollo to be the Sonne of Vulcan, and keeper of Athens: Which opinion, 
if it be; as it ought, transferrd to an Allegory, is indeed most true; because 
Vulcan produceth the philosophicall Sol, which is Apollo. But it hath 
prevayld, that hee was borne of Jupiter; and that Latona having two 
children in her wombe, Apollo and Diana, which shee conceiv'd of Ju=
piter, Juno being jealousy sent python a Serpent of prodigious magni=
tude and malice, to persecute and perplexe her being great with child. 
The miserable woman after tedious wandrings was at length brought 
by a Shipp to the Island Ortygia, to her sister Asteria, who therein 
reigned: And that Island, being almost all overflowed with the Sea, 
gave place to Latona being in travaile, whereupon it was called Delos, 
which before was Adyndos: There therefore being in labour, first came forth 
Diana, and shee did the office of a midwife to her mother labouring 
to bring forth her brother Apollo; From whence it came to passe 
that women going with child did in the time of their labour 
invoke her deity, and call her either Lucina or Ilithya, be=
cause shee shows light to infants borne their eyes being opened. 
Apollo therefore being borne and growne up slew python the tor=
mentor of his mother with arrowes, and killd the Cyclops because 
they made lightening for Jupiter to destroy his Sonne Aesculapius, 
whome Jupiter condemned to hell blasted with lightening, because 
hee had restord Hippolytus to life being torne in pieces by horses. 
Wee have in many places demonstrated these things to be meerely 
Chymicall: For Latona, Cynthia, Apollo, and python are requisites of 
the art, which have such a relation one to the other, as hath been 
declared; but these things being divulged in the writings of the most 
antient poets, as Orpheus, Linus, Musaus, Homer, &c. have given the 
ignorant occasion to ascribe religion and veneration to Apollo: Where=
upon Apollo is in many places of Europe and Asia worshipped, 
and innumerable temples erected to him; And especially at Delphos 
hee had a temple to be worshipped by antient religion, wherein a great 
number of statues made of solid gold and silver of great weight 
and artifice was reposited by Kings and princes, with other most 
pretious gifts, which were hidden in sacred places for religion sake 
by men of all degrees. Pausanias reports that a brazen Skeleton of 
admirable artifice was hung at the top of the Temple by Hippocrates. 
That famous Tripod was also consecrated to Apollo by pelops, at the 
time when hee marryed Hippodamia the daughter Oenomaus King 
of Elis, which Tripod Mulciber made; and gave to pelops: It was 
erected in the middle of the temple; in which pythia sitting

receiv'd the inspiration of the Devill, gaping with a wide mouth, and then

declared, altogether Chymicall by originate, though the Devill afterwards
confirmer the superstition of men, and gave prophesyes under that name:
The two Eagles are two stones, whereof one comes from the East, the
other from the West, which the philosophers have many wayes demonstra=
ted. Jupiter sends them forth, as by Ensigne=bearers: The Eagle seemes in=
deed a friend to Apollo or the Sunne; in proving her chickens at the Sunne,
which if they cannot endure, shee disownes them as illegitimate: Its feathers
being joyned with other things, are sayd not to putrefy, and to devoure
the feathers of other birds, easily admitting gilding also: By old age, or
sicknesse it dyes not, but by famine; For the upper beake growes soe
crooked that it cannot receive food: which having cast off it plunges it=
selfe three times in a fountaine, and soe is sayd to returne to youth.
Whereupon sayth the psalmist: Your youth shall be renewed as the
youth of an Eagle: This of all birds is never affected with Lightening:
It warrs with the dragon, which hunts after its eggs for the sake of
them: All the endowments of whose nature have afforded the philo=
sophers reason to predicate the Eagle in their art, and assimilate the
stone to it, whereof innumerable examples being obvious in their
bookes, wee will not here alledge more.

The philosophers have in many places made mention of two stones given to us
gratis, as Isaac, Arnold, and others, amongst whom Avicenne sayth they lye in
dung neglected, and rejected by the vulgar, which, if they be joyned together, are
able to complete the Magistery: Some men extoll the occidentall Mercury, which
hath preferd it selfe before gold, and overcome it: But the Author of the ad=
vise of the wedlocks of Sol and Luna out of the epistle of Aristotle doth
best of all describe the two stones, where hee sayth: That there are two
principall stones of thy art, the white and the red of wonderfull nature: at
the setting of the sunne the white begins to appear upon the surface of the
water, hiding itselfe till midnight, and afterwards declining to the bottome: But
the red operates contrariwise; it beginning to ascend upon the water at the
rising of the sunne till noone, and then descend to the bottome: These
stones therefore are the aforesaid Eagles sent by Jupiter from Delphi;
These also are the wolfe and dogge coming from different or opposite parts
of the earth, which bite and worry one the other, and both became mad, as
Rhasis in his epistle declares: These stones are the most true Bezoar,
whereof the east Indyes send the best, drawne from the bowells of
wild beasts, the West Indyes yeild another but of lesse efficacy, had
at peruana out of animals made tame; For the east affoards a
most fierce wolfe, the west a dogge familiar to men, that is, Sulphur
comes from the East, Mercury from the Western tract, whereof this is
gentle and tractable; that cholericke and angry; which two, when
first they find one another, doe fall together by the ears: and
the dogge being of notable bignesse obtaining the first victory,
prostrating the wolfe, and bringing him to deaths doors: But the
wolfe recovering strength afterwards overthrowes the dogge, and be=
ing cast downe never leaves him till hee be utterly killed and dead;
In the meane time receiving from the dogge noe lesse wounds nor lesse
mortall, than hee gave him, till they worry one another to death: As=
touching the wolfe sayth Rosinus to Euthicia: That hee is a fortifyed
souldier the conquerour of two, of much esteeme and most intense
strength, perforating bodys when hee meets with them, and is white
in apparition, prov'd by experiment, and is the man which tooke Luna
to wife, whom some men suppose to be gold of a most precious con=
nexion and composition, whose congealed part is never dissolved, nor
footsteps or impressions defaced, which God hath freely bestowed
upon the pious and elect philosophers: You must know, that nature
hath gotten an equall as an enemy: These are his words. And a little after
hee sayth: That Sulphur is most strong, and fighting against fire
which it containes and is contained: for a most precious colour proceeds
from them being joyned together, and that Sulphur, which is naturally
volatile, can never afterwards flye away, because the soule hath

perforates it, in like manner the tincture of the soule hath both perforated
and is mixed with the body, and the body hath contained the soule, and
restrained the naturall part from flying. And then to one demanding which
of the stones was strongest, hee answers: That stone which is noe stone
is stronger than the other enemy: but the red is stronger than that
which strengtheneth its companion by its fortitude. The Orientall wolfe is
therefore stronger than the Occidentall dogge, though hee obtaines not the
victory effectually, but perisheth together with the enemy: but the stinging
poyson is produced from both: The difference between a wolfe and dogge is
small, a Mastive or great dogge representing the forme and shape of a
wolfe, soe as to seeme a wolfe originally, but made tame by long proge-
neration: After the same manner Sulphur and Mercury doe little differ one
from the other, because that proceeds from this, or this from that: Mercury
did indeed beget Sulphur, but Sulphur purges Mercury, and made it soe.
Concerning these the same Rosinus, the question being asked, from
whence comes its colour? answers, from the most intense bitternesse of it:
and againe: From whence comes it bitternesse and intension? hee answers
from the impurity of its metall: And againe: Is its red colour never su-
pereminent? hee answers, yes: And againe: Is it also never hotter than
fire? hee answers, fire in respect of it is as water in respect of fire: And
againe: Is it not stronger than fire? hee answers, not. And againe: Why
therefore doe you affirme it to be stronger than fire? hee answers, because
fires meeting together doe one destroy the other. It is therefore manifest
that one becomes the food and aliment of the other, and soe much as one
decreaseth, soe much the other increaseth, till that which increaseth pre-
vayles, and the Dragon devoures the serpent: In great fights it oftentimes
happens, that they that undergoe the greater slaughter doe winne the
spoils and victory; Soe also though the dogge lay prostrate, yet was not
altogether overcome, hee holding his enemy soe fast, that hee could not
live without him, nor hee dye without the other.

### Embleme 48. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->The King falls sicke by drinking water, and is restored to health<-
->by physitians.<-

---

### Epigramme 48.

> In wealth nor people doe the King delight,
> Soe much as to have water brought in sight:
> Cup after cup hee drinks, growes swolln and wanne
> Urging reliefe from his physitian,
> Where finding art by various means assays,
> His health restord, and humor was allayd.

<vocab>Embleme, Epigramme, physitian, humor, swolln, wanne, assays, allayd, nature</vocab>

--- Page 164 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>150</page-num>
<header>Discourse 48.</header>
<meta>This page continues the alchemical commentary on Emblem 48 of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens". The handwriting is a 17th-century mix of secretary and italic hands. The text uses historical and legendary examples to illustrate the value of the "physician" (alchemist) and the "King" (the matter of the work).</meta>

Xerxes that most potent King of persia leading his army through dry
and rugged places in hott weather, being thirsty, refuses not a draught
of muddy water offered by a souldier, but dranke most freely, and rewarded
him that brought it very amply. And indeed if a man at this very time
(as some of the latest historians affirme) travell through the borders of
persia, it is sayd that fountains of sweet water are very rarely found,
standing waters being brackish, and the soyle it selfe in the surface
yeilding much saltness: After the same manner the King, which the
philosophers have made mention of, being perplexed with thirst, com-
manded plenty of sweet water to be brought him, of which hee dranke
his fill; as is manifest to any man by the Allegory of Merlines. The
cure of this sicke and discoloured King is undertaken by severall physi-
tians: The Aegyptians administring their medicines stirred the humors
being yet crude, which Hippocrates affirms ought to be purged being
first concocted, unlesse they be fluxible and uncertaine: For then they
must be forthwith expelled, that they may not peradventure make an
assault upon the more noble parts or bowells. Whereupon dangerous symp-
toms, such as <term>Lipothymy</term> and <term>Syncope</term> happened to the King, but the phy-
sitians of Alexandria coming last of all, and ofttimes more successefull
in a chronicke disease, restored the King to his former health. It is well
worth while to cure soo great a King, who being made sound extends a
liberall hand and a serene aspect to his physitian. Wee have read, that
many many times have been very nobly rewarded by severall Kings, as
that of Democides by polycrates a tyrant of Samos with two talents, of
Erasistratus (who as pliny writes, was schollar of Chrysippus, begotten
upon the daughter of Aristotle) for curing King Antiochus being sicke
for the love of his stepmother Stratonice, with a hundred talents by his
sonne ptolemy, of Jacob Cocterius, physitian of Lewis the XI King of
France: from whome hee receivd four thousand crowns every month as
a stipend, and other later wee make no mention of; but the cure of
this King is recompensed with yet a farre greater gratuity or reward. For
thus say Hermes and Geber in the <term>Rosary</term>: Hee that hath once com-
pleated this art, if hee should live ten hundred thousand years, and
every day feed four thousand men, would not want. Thy Senior con-
firms, saying: Hee that hath that stone, whereof the Elixir is made
is soe rich, as hee that hath fire, can give fire, to whome hee will, and
when hee will, and as much as hee will without his owne detriment or want.
The father of Democritus was soe rich, that hee gave a feast to the
army of Xerxes, and pythius a man exceding wealthy offered to the
same pay for his whole army, and victuals for five months, provided
that the youngest of his five sonns, which was the comfort of his old
age, might be permitted to stay at home, and not be constrained to goe.

The text concludes the alchemical interpretation of the King's health, using the historical anecdote of Pythius and Xerxes (as recorded by Sabellicus) to contrast earthly wealth with alchemical "riches," and referencing the alchemist Count Bernhard of Trevisan.</meta>

stone as I know. This is hee, whome Count Bernhard mentioneth, that
gives to his six servitors soe much of his kingdome, as hee himselfe possesseth,
provided they waite, till hee recover youth in the bath, and be adorned
with various garments, namely a blacke breast-plate, a white shirt, and
purple blood; for then hee promiseth to give some of his blood to every
one, and make them partakers of his riches.

# Embleme 49. Of the secrets of Nature.
## The philosophicall infant is, as Orion, the sonne of three fathers.

---

### Epigramme 49.
->Vulcan with Hermes and Apollo did,<-
->Their seed inject into an oxes hide,<-
->Whence produced was the great Orion:<-
->Three fathers also hath the Sophick Sonne;<-
->The Sunne is first, Vulcan the next is second,<-
->The artist is third father of this child.<-

<vocab>Embleme, Epigramme, Vulcan, Hermes, Apollo, Orion, Sophick Sonne, Sunne, Artist, secrets of Nature</vocab>

--- Page 167 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>Discourse 49.</header>
<warning>There is a large brownish stain on the right margin, partially obscuring a handwritten note.</warning>

Those women, which prostitute themselves to variety of men, doe seldome conceive lively children through the confusion of divers seeds: for nature in the generation of man and the most part of animals admitts no superfetation, except perhaps very rarely: and therefore every offspring is produced from one onely father and mother, whether it be one or more, as is also manifest by historys, and the event of some supposing otherwise; in the first place of Margarett the wife of Herman Earle of Henneberge, who, in the year 1276, brought forth three hundred and sixty five children, which were all baptized, and the males called by the name of John, and the females Elizabeth, their grave being dead is yet to be seen in the Church of Lausdune, one mile distant from the Hague towards the sea in Holland, together with the brazen bason, in which they were baptized, and an inscription of the story: The cause already was, that the Countesse, seeing a poor woman carry two children twins in her armes, called her adulteresse, as if it was impossible for more children to be borne at one conception by one man, but necessarily by severall: whereupon an imprecation being made by the poor woman, who knew her selfe clear of that accusation, the Countesse conceived in her wombe at one time and by one man soe many children, as there are dayes in the year: This is indeed a miracle, but a naturall worke, which happened through the vengeance of God: But in the philosophicall worke that, which is otherwise contrary to nature, is safely admitted under the voyle of an allegory. For here one infant is sayd to have three fathers, or also two, as also mother. Thereupon sayth Raymund cited by the Rosary; Our infant hath two fathers and two mothers, and because hee with his whole substance is tenderly nourished in fire, hee therefore never dyes: Soe Dionysus or Bacchus is sayd to have two mothers, who was ript out of his mothers belly (being burned) before the time of his maturity, and matured in his fathers thigh, soe that the father became a mother: But these things are better declared in the conception of Orion, who is sayd to be produced from the seed of Apollo, Vulcan, and Mercury mixed together, and preserved in the hide of an Oxe for the space of tenne months: These things would be altogether mon-<margin><insert>Ben</insert></margin>strous, not onely fabulous, if a secret of nature were not comprehended under these shrouds, not obvious to all men. Lully in the Theorie of his Testament attributes to the same philosophicall infant soe many and almost the same fathers, namely Sol, who is Apollo, or the celestiall Sol is the first author of this generation, who by his ineffable virtue occult or astrale power operates upon a certaine matter knowne to the philosophers, as upon the matrix

of a woman, and therein produceth a sonne or infant like to himselfe,
to whome afterward hee will leave and resigne his armes and ensignes
of virtue, by paternale right, that is, the faculty of maturing things im=
mature, and the energy of tinging and purging things not tinged nor
purged. For what soever Sol is able to perfect in a thousand years, that
will his sonne performe in halfe an houre; & that therefore virtue
may be made a thousand times stronger in him, than in Sol, his fa=
ther deliver him to Vulcan and the Artist together for education, that
his generous disposition may be polished and multiplyed in virtues by
their means; for manifest it is that to practice from a child conduceth
much: Soe Achilles, Jason, Hercules were committed to Chiron to be in=
structed for the same end. For Milo the Crotonian, who being a boy
carryed a calfe, when a man carryed an Oxe by custome. Besides Sol,
Vulcan and the artist are not erroneously reputed the fathers of this
child, because as hee was the first, they made him to be such and soe
great: Noe equivalent compensation can be made to Masters for in=
stitution, as neither reward to parents for generation. These compose
the body, those the mind. If the mind be better than the body, noe
lesse recompense is due to those, than to these. In the production of
Orion Mercury contributes matter, Apollo forme, and Vulcan heat, or
the externall efficient cause: Soe also in the philosophicall worke
it is proper for three fathers to be seen to have conspired for the pro=
creation of one infant, in whome the philosophers take great delight.

# Embleme 50. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->The Dragon kills the woman, and shee him, and are both bloody<-
->all over.<-

## Epigramme 50.

Fitt for the Dragon digge a cavery place;
And therewith joyne the woman face to face:
Whilst her the joyes of wedlocke reaps, shee dyes,
With whome entomb him, that hee may not rise;
Thus is hee kill'd, and ting'd with blood all ore:
Take but this course, and you need aske noe more.

<meta>Faint catchword "In" at bottom right corner.</meta>

<vocab>Embleme, Secrets of Nature, Dragon, Epigramme, wedlocke, ting'd</vocab>

--- Page 170 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>156</page-num>
<header>Discourse 50.</header>

The mansion of Dragons is in caverns of the earth, but the residence
of men above the earth in the immediate aire; which two Elements
are contrary, and appointed by the philosophers to be joynd together,
that one may act upon the other: But by the woman they meane, as
Basill in key 2^d. saith: For it is not convenient or commodious for an
Eagle to place her nest upon the Alps, because her young ones would dye
through the coldness of the snow at the top of the mountain: But
if you add to the Eagle a cold Dragon, which hath had his habitation
a long time in the rocks, and crept out of the caves of the earth,
and put them both into an <term>infernale Cell</term>, then will pluto blow a
blast, and draw a volatile fiery spirit out of the cold dragon, which
with its great heat will burne the Eagles feathers, and excite such a
<term>sudorificke bath</term>, as to melt the snow at the top of the mountain,
and turne it into water; from which a admirable bath may be well
prepared, contributing fortune and health to the King. Admirable in-
deed it is, that a cold dragon should yield a fiery spirit; yet experiens
declares it to be true in burned serpents, which doe send forth a venomous
flame poysoning the standers by, as in vayne are the keepers of the <term>Chy-
micall</term> treasures called <term>flammivomous</term> dragons, as the keepers of the golden
fleece, the Hesperian gardens, Cadmus, and the like. But this dragon lives
in straight places of subterranean rocks, which you must there take, and
joyne to the Eagle or woman, to her in the grave, to the other, if you
had rather, in the nest: For the nature of a dragon is sometimes to lye in
waite for the Eagles eggs, and wage mortall warre with the Eagle: Some
there are of the Gracian writers who affirme that a dragon in times past
fell in love with a mayd and lay with her. What wonder therefore, if
the philosophers would have their dragon shut up in a caverne with a
woman? Greverus joynes red and blacke dragons together in the deep
gulfe of the mountains, and burnes them with fire, and the blacke pe-
rishing, hee sayth that the keeper of the mountains searcheth for them
every where, and brings them to the mountains: <term>Merlin</term> in his vision (if it
be not supposititious) makes mention of a white and red dragon; These
dragons, whatsoever they be, whether one be a woman, or a female
dragon, doe act one with the other, till they both dye, and emitt blood
from their wounds, wherewith they are embrued. But how the element
of earth and fire is understood by the dragon, and by the woman the
element of aire and water; whereupon saith <term>Clangor bucina</term>: The dragon
is the matter remaining in the bottome after the water be distilled from
it: And according to <term>Hermes</term>: The water of aire being between heaven and
earth is the life of every thing, for that water dissolves a body into a

<meta>Catchword "a" at bottom right corner.</meta>

<vocab>Basill, infernale Cell, sudorificke bath, Chymicall, flammivomous, Greverus, Merlin, Clangor bucina, Hermes, alchemy, dragon, eagle</vocab>

--- Page 171 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>157</page-num>
<header>Discourse 50.</header>

Spirit, and vivifyes a dead thing, and makes matrimony between man and wo=
man: For it confers the whole benefit of the art: Of the earth also we thus
speake. And moreover understand, that the particular earth, which wee tread
upon, is not the true element, yea is elemented with its true fifth element. Nor
doth the fifth elementale substance recede from its elemented body, of which
the earth is formed. And a little after. But the virgin and true element,
which fire cannot burne; is in the center of the earth: This is the dragon,
whereof wee speake, insinuating itselfe even into the center of the earth,
where the heat being great, it conceives within itselfe a flaming heat, where=
with it burnes the woman or eagle. The woman or eagle is indeed an airy
water, which some call the white or celestiall eagle, and will have it made
of common mercury, or sublimed salt, following therein the sense of some cer=
taine men blind in this art, feigning themselves to be quick sighted; But
sayth Count Bernhard in his Epistle, 

> I tell you truly, that noe water dissolves a metallicke species by naturall reduction, except that which continues with it in matter and forme, and which the metalls themselves can perlongate: 

And a little after: 

> Nor doth that water appertaine to bodyes in solution, which remains not with them in congelation: 

And a little after: 

> I tell you truly, that the oyle which naturally invades and joyns natures together, and naturally introduceth a medium for the tinging of other bodyes, is not compounded of any other extraneons thing, but only of the bowells of the body which is to be dissolved: 

This therefore being apprehended, the eagle and woman, as
also the dragon with the secrets of almost the whole art are understood,
which perhaps wee have thus farre too openly explained and declared to
the Sonns of learning, that God may hereby be glorified.

<vocab>vivifyes, matrimony, fifth element, elementale substance, virgin element, dragon, eagle, airy water, mercury, sublimed salt, Count Bernhard, metallicke species, perlongate, solution, congelation, oyle, tinging, extraneons, bowells, Sonns of learning</vocab>
